#2883. PennantPark (PNNT) Q4 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Management points to a 14.7% joint venture yield while weather and car wash softness drew scrutiny.

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 24, 2026 at 4:54PM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

PennantPark (PNNT -5.94%) reported fourth quarter core net investment income of $0.13 per share and a portfolio value of $1.2 billion. Management stated that the portfolio is positioned to take advantage of the current interest rate environment and indicated a focus on rotating equity co-investments into income-generating debt assets. The company reported that its government services and defense investments remain a high-conviction sector due to durable federal funding and resilient cash flow profiles.

Operator: Good afternoon, and welcome to the PennantPark Investment Corporation's fourth fiscal quarter 2026 earnings conference call. Today's conference is being recorded. At this time, all participants have been placed in a listen-only mode. The call will be open for a question-and-answer session following the speaker's remarks. If you would like to ask a question at that time, simply press star one on your telephone keypad. If you would like to withdraw your question, press star two on your telephone keypad. It is now my pleasure to turn the call over to Mr. Arthur (Art) Howard Penn, Chairman and Chief Executive Officer of PennantPark Investment Corporation. Mr. Penn, you may begin your conference.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Good afternoon, everyone, and thank you for joining PennantPark Investment Corporation's fourth fiscal quarter 2026 earnings call. I am joined today by Rick Allorto, our Chief Financial Officer. Rick, please start off by disclosing some general conference call information and include a discussion about forward-looking statements.

Rick Allorto: Thank you, Art. I would like to remind everyone that today's call is being recorded and is the property of PennantPark Investment Corporation. Any unauthorized broadcast of this call in any form is strictly prohibited. An audio replay of the call will be available on our website. I would also like to call your attention to the customary safe harbor disclosure in our press release regarding forward-looking information. Our remarks today may also include forward-looking statements and projections. Please refer to our most recent SEC filings for important factors that could cause actual results to differ materially from these projections. We do not undertake to update our forward-looking statements unless required by law.

To obtain copies of our latest SEC filings, please visit our website at pennantpark.com or call us at 212-905-1000. At this time, I would like to turn the call back to our Chairman and Chief Executive Officer, Arthur (Art) Howard Penn.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Thanks, Rick. I will begin with an overview of our fourth quarter results and a review of the portfolio. I will then discuss the current market environment and how we believe PNNT is positioned going forward. Rick will follow with a detailed review of our financial results, after which we will open up the call for questions. For the quarter ended September 30th, core net investment income was $0.13 per share. This exceeded our base dividend of $0.04 per share per month, or $0.12 per share for the quarter. As of September 30th, our NAV per share was $6.35, which is down 3.2% from the prior quarter.

As we previously disclosed, PNNT has a considerable balance of undistributed taxable income, which we are required to distribute to shareholders. PNNT is utilizing the supplemental dividends to pay out the undistributed taxable income, and the decline in NAV was largely attributable to these supplemental dividend payments. As previously communicated, the December payment will conclude our supplemental dividend program. Excluding the payment of the supplemental dividends in this past quarter, NAV would have decreased only $0.09, or 1.4%. Despite the ongoing macroeconomic and geopolitical environment, our portfolio remains highly diversified and conservatively positioned, with a median debt-to-EBITDA of 4.6 times, interest coverage of 2.1 times, and a loan-to-value of 44%.

As of September 30th, our portfolio totaled $1.2 billion, and during the quarter, we continued to originate attractive investment opportunities and invested a total of $75 million at a weighted average yield of 9.4%, including $25 million invested into five new platform investments with a median debt-to-EBITDA of 4.8 times, interest coverage of 2.2 times, and a loan-to-value ratio of 36%. We added two investments to non-accrual status and removed one investment, and ended the quarter with five non-accrual investments out of a diversified pool of 160 companies, representing 2.1% of the portfolio at cost and 0.6% at market value. Our PSLF joint venture portfolio continues to be a significant contributor to our core net investment income.

On a last 12 months basis, PNNT's average cash yield on invested capital in the JV was 14.7%. As of September 30th, the JV portfolio totaled $1.2 billion. In July, the JV refinanced the AAA tranches of its $300 million debt securitization, reducing the securitization's weighted average spread by 97 basis points to 1.69% from 2.66%. We expect the JV to continue to be a significant contributor to PNNT's NII in future quarters. Turning to portfolio positioning, I'd like to highlight our government services and defense investments. Government services and defense remains one of our highest conviction sectors and has historically been among our best performing investment areas.

We remain highly constructive on the long-term outlook for government services and defense because the sector possesses several characteristics that align well with our investment philosophy. Demand has historically been supported by durable federal funding priorities and long-term contracts that provide meaningful revenue visibility and stability. Many of these businesses exhibit resilient cash flow profiles, variable cost structures, and are generally less sensitive to broader economic cycles than many commercial industries. In addition, the sector continues to benefit from active M&A markets and strong valuation support, thereby providing multiple avenues for value creation. One example of this is Echelon InvestCo.

Through our equity co-investment in Echelon InvestCo, we have an ongoing equity investment in a fast-growing defense tech company, Shield AI. Shield AI recently announced an acquisition and a capital raise at an indicative valuation for investment of $2.6 million above the value on September 30th of $2 million. This increase equates to $0.04 per share. Within the government services and defense portfolio, our investments are focused on businesses that support the Department of War and other mission-critical government agencies. We focus on companies addressing high-priority national security initiatives, including modernization of defense systems and digital infrastructure, cyber and electronic warfare capabilities, modeling and simulation, counter-drone technologies, and next-generation autonomous systems.

We believe these priorities will remain central to U.S. defense spending for years to come, creating a favorable backdrop for continued investment opportunities. On a combined basis, including the joint venture portfolio, government services and defense represents approximately 12.4% of total investments. Given our experience, sourcing capabilities, and the attractive opportunity set, we intend to increase that exposure over time. Another highlight of our portfolio is AKW, a U.K. provider of adapted bathroom and kitchen products that help elderly and disabled individuals live independently at home. Since taking control of AKW in 2018, we have strengthened management, improved operations, and invested in growth.

On October 27, 2026, AKW acquired Mobility Bathing Group, entering the private pay market and creating cross-selling, marketing, and cost-saving opportunities. We are targeting an exit of the investment in AKW in the next 18 to 24 months. As of September 30, we held $48 million of the first lien term loan, marked at par, and common equity valued at $77.7 million against a cost of $5.9 million. Turning to software, our exposure represents approximately 3.1% of the portfolio. These investments follow our core middle market strategy and consist of cash pay loans with financial covenants, moderate leverage, and relatively short durations. These businesses provide mission-critical enterprise software to regulated industries, including defense, healthcare, and financial services.

Let me now turn to the broader market environment. M&A activity has increased recently, although overall conditions remain uneven. Private equity sponsors remain active, and we are seeing a growing pipeline of attractive opportunities across both new originations as well as add-on investments. We are optimistic that activity levels will remain strong throughout the end of the year and into next year. We expect increased transaction activity to drive repayments across the portfolio, including opportunities to monetize equity co-investments and to redeploy that capital into income-generating investments. In the core middle market, the pricing for high-quality first lien term loans remains attractive, typically ranging from SOFR plus 500 to 550 basis points, with leverage of approximately 4.5x EBITDA.

Importantly, these structures continue to include meaningful covenant protections, which is in contrast to the covenant-light structures prevalent in the upper middle market. We believe the current environment favors lenders with established private equity sponsor relationships, consistent access to deal flow, and disciplined underwriting. These are longstanding strengths of the PennantPark platform. We continue to believe that the core middle market offers an attractive risk-adjusted opportunity. Companies in this segment generally have EBITDA of $10 million-$50 million and often operate below the practical threshold of the broadly syndicated loan and high-yield markets.

As a result, lenders can typically conduct extensive diligence, negotiate meaningful financial covenants, structure transactions with appropriate leverage and equity cushions, and maintain regular access to company financial information. Since our inception nearly 19 years ago, PNNT has invested $9.9 billion at an average yield of 11.1%, while maintaining a loss ratio on invested capital of roughly 20 basis points annually, a testament to our consistent and disciplined approach through multiple market cycles. As a provider of strategic capital who fuels the growth of our portfolio companies, in many cases, we participate in the upside of that company by making an equity co-investment. Our returns on these equity co-investments have been excellent over time.

Overall, for our platform from inception through September 30th, we have invested over $635 million in these equity co-investments and have generated an IRR of 25% and a multiple on invested capital of 2x. Looking ahead, our experienced team and broad origination platform position us well to generate attractive deal flow. We remain steadfast in our commitment to capital preservation and maintaining a disciplined, patient investment approach. We continue to focus on investing in high-quality middle market companies with strong free cash flow generation. We capture that value through first lien senior secured loans, and we pay out those cash flows in the form of dividends to our shareholders.

At this time, I will turn the call over to Rick for a more detailed review of our financial results.

Rick Allorto: Thank you, Art. For the quarter ended September 30th, GAAP net investment income was $0.11 per share, and core net investment income was $0.13 per share. Net investment income excludes $1.7 million of general and administrative expenses related to the audits of our unconsolidated significant subsidiaries and $0.9 million of incentive fee expense offset. Investment income was comprised of $18.8 million in interest income, $4.1 million in dividend income, and $0.2 million in other income. Operating expenses for the quarter were as follows: Interest and credit facility expenses were $8.3 million, base management and incentive fees were $4.3 million, general and administrative expenses were $2.9 million, and provision for excise taxes were $0.1 million.

Net realized and unrealized change on investments and debt, including provision for taxes, was a loss of $5.1 million. As of September 30th, our NAV was $6.35 per share compared to $6.56 per share last quarter. At quarter end, our debt-to-equity ratio was 1.3x, and our capital structure was diversified across multiple funding sources, including both secured and unsecured debt. As of September 30th, our key portfolio statistics were as follows: The portfolio remains highly diversified with 160 companies across 32 different industries. The weighted average yield on our debt investments was 11.3%.

The portfolio is comprised of 43% first lien secured debt, 2% second lien secured debt, 15% subordinated notes to PSLF, 8% other subordinated debt, 5% equity in PSLF, and 27% in other preferred and common equity co-investments. 87% of the debt portfolio is floating rate. With that, I will turn the call back to Art for closing remarks.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Thanks, Rick. In conclusion, we remain committed to delivering consistent performance, preserving capital, and creating long-term value for all stakeholders. Thank you to our team for their dedication and our shareholders for their continued partnership and confidence in PennantPark. That concludes our remarks. At this time, I would like to open up the call for questions.

Operator: Thank you. If you would like to signal with questions, please press star one on your touch-tone telephone. If you are joining us today using a speakerphone, please make sure your mute function is turned off to allow your signal to reach our equipment. Again, that is star one if you would like to signal. Our first question will come from Robert James Dodd with Raymond James.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Thanks for the question, Chris. It is roughly the same. Historically, many lenders did not like lending to government services. They did not like the contract concentration, as an example. We viewed, we diligenced and felt comfortable with some of this concentration. Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Yeah. Look, Rick, all options are on the table. We are evaluating that as well as other things. We agree the stock is cheap. We agree the stock is cheap. In certain cases, we have delayed draws that are contractual, so we contractually are obligated to fund. A lot of our deals do have embedded growth, and we do delayed draw term loans to fuel the growth of the company and, of course, the equity co-invest. So, there is that we have the delayed draw term loan is a contractual obligation to fund ongoing loans for certain companies.

Richard (Rick) Shane: Got it. Okay. Then, actually, that is it for me. I appreciate the time as always, Art. Thank you, guys.

Operator: The next question will come from Paul Conrad Johnson with KBW.

Paul Conrad Johnson: Yeah, good afternoon. Thanks for taking my question. Just one more on the audit charge this quarter. I do not recall that you guys have taken an expense like this prior to this quarter, and correct me if I am wrong, but what requirement, I guess, sort of exists for the audit charges this quarter around those companies? It sounds like one of them might be non-recurring, but I guess, what kind of arises to require that expense?

Rick Allorto: Sure. Paul, thanks for the question. You are correct. This has not flown through the income statement in the past. There are two GAAP accounting rules, Rule 3-09, Rule 4-08, that we have to run some calculations and to make a determination as to whether or not any of our portfolio companies qualify as a significant subsidiary. To the extent that they do, we are required to file their audited financial statements as an exhibit to our 10-K. So Jones & Frank back in 2024, and then AKW in their respective fiscal years are 12/31. AKW for 12/31/2025, both were significant subsidiaries, and that is what triggered the filing requirement and triggered the incremental cost.

Paul Conrad Johnson: Okay, got it. That is helpful. AKW, it has obviously been successful there, but it is not often that you hear managers verbally commit, I guess, to a one to two-year sort of expected exit, especially for an equity investment. I guess I would just ask, what do you think could change, or what has to change, I guess, over the next year to potentially stall a sale or an exit or just unsuccessful repayment of that investment?

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Yeah. So it is a good question. Just to highlight it, in this particular case, we are the private equity sponsors. It is a company that was a long time ago mezzanine deal that we ended up converting the mezzanine to equity, and we have been the sponsor for several years now. About two years ago, we brought in a new CEO who really has done great things with the company. It has been on a tear since the new CEO came in. We did this add-on acquisition. The company had been more in the public pay area of accessibility, then this acquisition put it in the private pay, almost doubling the size of the company.

So we have been guided that we should operate the two companies together for at least a year so that we have combined financial statements, then we have our best shot of exiting. There is never any guarantees, but we are being as transparent as we possibly can since we actually are in a control position in this one. It is not like we are riding along the side of another sponsor. This is one we have a bit more control of. And we do not obviously have control of how well the company is going to do, right? And how well that integration is going to go, and what the combined EBITDA is going to be.

And then we certainly do not have control over what people are willing to pay for this company down the road, once they see those financials. So, we are giving everyone transparency as best we know it today. Obviously, we are hopeful that the company operates well as a consolidated entity, continues to grow, and then has a successful exit down the road. But never any guarantees. It is a good point. We never guarantee anything, but this is the game plan as best we know it today.

Paul Conrad Johnson: Got it. Okay. Thanks for that. That is helpful. And then one more control position for you guys, Flock Financial.

Paul Conrad Johnson: I was wondering to maybe get an update of how that company was performing. It looked like the sub note flipped to PIK this quarter from cash. There was a little bit of a write-down in the equity.

Paul Conrad Johnson: You are in control there. I was hoping to get an update if possible.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Yeah. Similarly, Flock is a growing specialty finance company that is involved in the charged-off receivables business, which in this K-shaped economy actually is doing pretty well now. The movement from cash pay to PIK is really to create capital to continue to grow the company, grow the value of the company, grow the value of the equity. So there is a big opportunity right now in this K-shaped economy to lean into building this company. So the flipping of cash pay to PIK was really just to keep capital in the company to grow it. And we are focused on growing it. I think the timeframe is a little bit longer than AKW.

I mean, it is going to be longer than 18-24 months, but we are optimistic that this is a real company that has got a real moat, real reason to be, and has a good growth trajectory ahead of it.

Paul Conrad Johnson: Got it. Appreciate that. And one final one, if I can, just on the new non-accruals this quarter. It looked like there are two non-accruals. One is very small, the car wash, and then Capital Construction, a little bit of a larger loan. Both loans were marked pretty close to par last quarter. I was just, I guess, hoping to get an update maybe in terms of any color on what sort of transpired from last quarter to this quarter on the new non-accruals.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Yeah. Yeah, the car wash, we're in different layers of the capital stack. As we've discussed on prior calls, car washes were doing really well post-COVID, and many individuals bought subscriptions to car washes. In this K-shaped economy, that's something that has been less. That's been more disposable for consumers. I'd be interested kind of line by line, because we have several layers to that capital stack on the car wash company. The other company goes by the name Stronghouse Solutions. That's a roofing-related company, roofing services for residential roofing.

As you may know, and I think you're in the Midwest, Paul, there's been fewer storms, less hail events, fewer needs for roofing repairs in this last time period, and that has impacted the results of this particular company. So that one's a little bit more weather-related. The number of hail storms in the Midwest and elsewhere are not what they were at least a year ago. So we can blame El Niño or whatever we can blame on that one, but that's kind of behind that particular. Again, that's an idiosyncratic one.

I think the car wash one's a little bit more of this kind of post-COVID vintage and behavior changing from 2021, 2022 to today, and the reversion to the mean.

Paul Conrad Johnson: Appreciate it. That's all for me.

Operator: That does conclude the question-and-answer session. I'll now turn the conference back over to Mr. Arthur (Art) Howard Penn for closing remarks.

Arthur (Art) Howard Penn: Thank you, everybody, for participating today. We are wishing everyone an excellent Thanksgiving holiday this week. We certainly have the attitude of gratitude for our investors. We are very appreciative and wishing everybody a terrific holiday. Thank you very much.

Operator: Thank you. That does conclude today's conference. We do thank you for your participation. Have an excellent day.

#2868. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

All’assist per Noslin si aggiungono due primati: 12,203 chilometri percorsi e 17 passaggi riusciti nell’ultimo terzo.

L’assist di tacco per Noslin è la giocata che resta negli occhi. Un’intuizione che impreziosisce la prestazione di Kenneth Taylor contro il Lecce e contribuisce al successo della Lazio. Il suo pomeriggio, però, offre molto altro da raccontare oltre al gesto tecnico del raddoppio. laziopress.itL’olandese chiude con 12,203 chilometri percorsi e 17 passaggi riusciti nell’ultimo terzo di campo: in entrambe le voci è il primo tra tutti i giocatori della partita. A questi numeri aggiunge due passaggi chiave, completando una prova nella quale impegno fisico e partecipazione alla manovra offensiva si accompagnano.È soprattutto l’incrocio tra i due primati a offrire una chiave di lettura della sua gara: Taylor dà continuità al lavoro del centrocampo e partecipa allo sviluppo del possesso nella zona più vicina alla porta avversaria.Taylor, 12,203 chilometri: il lavoro oltre l’assistIl dato sulla distanza percorsa restituisce la dimensione quantitativa della prestazione.

Nessun altro giocatore del match corre quanto Taylor, che termina la sfida superando i dodici chilometri.La distanza, da sola, non permette di stabilire quanto siano stati efficaci i singoli movimenti, né di distinguere il lavoro offensivo da quello difensivo. Il dato sostiene quindi la lettura di un centrocampista coinvolto nel collegamento con l’attacco, capace di contribuire alla continuità della manovra avanzata.

#2848. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2833. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2828. Quitting can actually be the key to success, says expert: It's one of the 'most courageous moves you can make'

Lindsay Crouse wants people to stop treating "quitter" like a bad word.

In fact, the former New York Times journalist believes that knowing when to quit is a crucial component to a successful, healthy life.

But Crouse didn't always feel that way. In her recent book "The Case for Quitting: The Surprising Benefits of Opting Out," published in September, Crouse shared that she used to push herself to the point of exhaustion at work and as a distance runner and marathoner.

"I'd achieved things I'd never dreamed were in reach, yet it didn't feel like enough. I was doing too much, and somehow it amounted to less than before," she wrote.

Now, Crouse wants to challenge the popular narrative around quitting. She spoke to psychologists, endurance athletes and fellow recovering workaholics about what they gained when they decided to let go.

"We're conditioned to see perseverance and quitting in these very binary ways," she tells CNBC Make It. "We think of people who never give up as people who succeed, and people who quit as people who fail."

But in Crouse's view, quitting can actually be a strategic move that allows us to focus on what's important.

"If we're going to succeed and do our very best, we need to learn to walk away," she says. "We need to learn to abandon paths that aren't working in order to find paths that might succeed."

When you're already struggling with something, "it's really natural to want to escalate your commitment to achieving your goal," Crouse says.

That could mean putting even more hours into a job that you know deep down isn't a good fit for you, or pushing yourself harder during marathon training because you're frustrated by an injury slowing you down.

But what many people don't realize is that investing more energy into a failing cause "actually only creates more failure," she says.

In those cases, doggedly persevering only "increases the amount that you lose, whether that's energy, whether it's money, whether it's time, or whether it's just your happiness," she says. "If you don't cut your losses, those losses are only going to grow."

To determine whether your approach to a task is becoming "a little unhealthy," Crouse recommends asking yourself these questions:

"How are you talking about achieving your goal? Are you getting more and more desperate? Are you constantly telling yourself that success is just one beat away, even as evidence might be telling you otherwise?"

If the answer is "yes" to the latter two questions, it might be a sign that you need to take a step back and reevaluate whether it makes sense to keep pushing — or whether it's time to pivot, Crouse says.

It can also be helpful to ask your friends whether they think you're on the right track, she says: "A lot of times, our friends will tell us the truth that we don't want to hear."

If you still feel like quitting is a sign of failure, Crouse recommends looking at it from a different angle.

Quitting is "one of the most courageous moves you can make," she says, because it "inherently involves risk, and being brave enough to do it means that you have confidence that you can look at something uncertain, take it on and make it work out."

#2823. Fed's John Williams indicates higher interest rates likely 'over time' to address inflation

New York Federal Reserve President John Williams said Monday that policymakers have more work to do to bring inflation under control, though he indicated that further action can unfold over time as the economy continues to grow.

In remarks consistent with previous statements, the influential central banker expressed confidence that inflation will get closer to the Fed's 2% target in 2027 before hitting the goal the following year. He also said that while artificial intelligence is pressuring prices, there's "little evidence" it will be the source of more persistent inflation.

"But getting inflation back to 2% is 100% essential," he said in remarks at the University of Mount Saint Vincent in New York. "We must take actions over time that will ensure that we get inflation back to our 2% goal in a timely manner and on a sustained basis. There is still work to do to get that done."

The comments come at a delicate moment for monetary policy, as the economy continues to grow and inflation stays well above the Fed's goal.

Fed officials have differed over how quickly to raise rates, with some advocating more aggressive action while others, including Williams, have emphasized a measured approach.

Williams ascribed the current inflation pressures to the conflict in Iran, demand for goods associated with the AI build-out and tariffs, the effects of which he said "have largely abated" as a source of pressure. Nevertheless, he expressed confidence that inflation will subside substantially in 2027 even as economic growth remains robust and unemployment edges lower.

The Federal Open Market Committee next meets on Dec. 8-9, with markets fully pricing in a quarter percentage point interest rate increase from the current target range of 3.75%-4%. The remarks from Williams are expected to be the last major policy statement from any Fed official before the December decision.

"Monetary policy cannot move ships or reopen pipelines and refineries, but it can — and must — ensure that these shocks do not lead to broader or persistent inflationary dynamics or erode well-anchored inflation expectations," Williams said, according to his prepared remarks.

Williams did not explicitly address the December meeting expectations, instead pointing to the statement released after the prior FOMC meeting in late October that said the "accumulation of more information" will help decide the future policy path.

"The data that we have received since then show that the economy has remained strong and that inflation has continued to run too high," he said.

#2819. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2818. Russia accuses NATO member Estonia of hostile actions ‘on the verge of unleashing open war’

Russia's Foreign Ministry has lashed out at Estonia's government, accusing the NATO member of carrying out a range of "hostile actions" and putting the Baltic state "on the verge of unleashing open war" with Moscow.

The outburst underscores rising tensions between Russia and countries on NATO's eastern flank and comes after Estonia announced it will close its road border crossings with Russia from Dec. 1.

Russia's ministry said Monday it had lodged a formal protest to Estonia's chargé d'affaires, Marek Uhtegi, over Tallinn's decision to close its border crossings and accused the European Union member of imposing a naval blockade against Moscow.

"The Estonian diplomat was again informed that such provocative and extremely dangerous actions are on the verge of unleashing open war with Russia," the ministry said in a statement, describing measures taken by Estonian lawmakers as "Russophobic."

The ministry also accused Estonia of "tacitly" providing its airspace for Ukrainian drone flights and sharply criticized Tallinn's ban on the transit of Russian and Belarusian grain through Estonian territory, referring to a policy that was introduced at the start of October.

Estonia, which shares a 294-kilometer (183-mile) border with Russia, swiftly denied ever allowing its territory to be used for military operations by another country.

It also responded to Russia's allegations by saying the Kremlin appears to be seeking to divert attention from its own efforts to undermine European security.

"Russian missiles have reached Polish territory, Danish security services have identified an assassination plot in Denmark orchestrated by Russian intelligence services, merchant vessels in the Black Sea are under constant threat from Russian attacks, and in Estonia, an arson attack targeting a defence industry company, planned on the orders of Russian military intelligence, was foiled," Estonian Foreign Minister Margus Tsahkna said Monday.

"All this is happening while Russia continues its war of aggression against Ukraine," he added.

The rhetoric marks a significant escalation in tensions between Estonia and Russia. Tallinn, a staunch Ukraine ally, has frequently raised the alarm over sabotage attacks against NATO countries that have been supporting Kyiv since Russia launched the full-scale invasion of its neighbor in February 2022.

Earlier this month, Estonia said it would close road border crossings with Russia from Dec. 1 as it blames Moscow for an attempted "sabotage" of one of its defense companies, an allegation the Kremlin denies.

Tallinn has said the border closure will help to reduce Moscow's ability to conduct hostile activities against it and mitigate risks related to sanctions circumvention.

Ukraine has stepped up its attacks on Russian critical targets in recent weeks, damaging Russian President Vladimir Putin's prized Crimea bridge, striking a Russian cargo ship in the Baltic Sea and destroying one of Russia's newest ships at a Libyan port.

The suspected Ukrainian attack on a Russian military-linked cargo ship in the Gulf of Finland on Saturday prompted Finnish Prime Minister Petteri Orpo to warn that the Ukraine war is "spilling over" into its immediate vicinity.

Finland, one of NATO's newest members, has also closed its land border crossings with Russia.

Ukraine's latest attacks on some of Russia's most protected targets have demonstrated Kyiv's capabilities to hurt Moscow even without ample U.S. military support, according to Mario Bikarski, senior Europe and Central Asia analyst for Verisk Maplecroft, a political risk consultancy.

"Ukraine will continue to look for ways to degrade not only Russia's economy, but also its geostrategic interests in the wider region, including the Mediterranean and the Middle East," Bikarski said.

The prospect of an imminent peace deal between Russia and Ukraine appears to be slim, Bikarski said, although "the latest surge in diplomatic activity and mounting losses for both sides signals an appetite from all sides to reach an agreement."

#2816. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guidance; Extension of Comment Period

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations

Agencies seek comment on proposed third-party risk management guidance and issue statement on community bank engagement with core service providers

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

#2813. Nvidia shares fall more than 5% as Huang comeback rally cools

Nvidia shares fell 5.86% on Tuesday, paring a massive Monday rally that was sparked by the return of Jensen Huang as CEO.

Other U.S. semiconductor stocks also traded lower after posting strong increases Monday. Micron declined 10.97%, Marvell Technology lost 9.10%, Arm Holdings dropped 8.55% and AMD shed 8.86%.

Neoclouds gave back some of their Monday gains, with CoreWeave losing 11.84%, Iren declining 11.54% and Nebius shedding 10.74%.

Huang, who resigned as CEO of Nvidia earlier this month after admitting to an extramarital affair, was reinstated on Monday. The stock closed over 30% higher following the news of Huang's return.

The company's board of directors said it unanimously voted to reinstate Huang following "a series of discussions and meetings" with him.

President Donald Trump also claimed he was personally involved in Huang's return. The president told CNBC that he spoke with the chipmaker's board before it reversed course and reinstated its co-founder.

"I made the deal," Trump told CNBC's Eamon Javers on Monday during an interview on "Squawk on the Street."

Trump said Nvidia's board asked for guarantees that Huang, who resigned less than two weeks ago, was not a threat to national security.

"And we gave them assurances, at least with everything we know," the president said. "We believe he's fine. He's absolutely fine."

Many Nvidia employees welcomed Huang's reinstatement, with multiple floors of employees greeting the CEO with cheers Monday at Nvidia's headquarters. In an employee meeting Tuesday, Huang struck a somber tone and acknowledged the impact of his actions. In the first such meeting since his return, Huang doubled down on his stance regarding succession planning, saying Nvidia's unique business model doesn't require it, according to people inside the room.

After Huang's reinstatement, several Wall Street analysts turned more bullish on the stock and upgraded their ratings on Nvidia.

"We believe reinstatement of CEO Jensen Huang RETURNS Investor CONFIDENCE in the AI trade," Mizuho analysts wrote in a note on Monday.

Huang and Nvidia have been central to the AI story, supplying the chips that power AI and financing companies across the ecosystem.

The tech giant remains under scrutiny as questions remain about Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, an alleged Chinese spy.

The U.S. is investigating Alice Huang and Megaspeed, the company where she worked. Questions also remain regarding whether Nvidia chips are making their way into China despite export controls.

Huang is set to publicly testify in front of the Senate Banking Committee on Dec. 3. Sen. Elizabeth Warren, who is set to take over the Committee in January, called Huang's reinstatement "reckless."

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Warren said in a statement on Monday.

Huang is also expected to sit for a closed-door interview with the House Foreign Affairs, Intelligence and China Select committees on Dec. 1. The hearing falls on the same day Huang, before his resignation, was scheduled to give the keynote address at Nvidia's annual GTC conference in Washington, D.C.

"Reinstatement should not be confused with the end of scrutiny," Bank of America said in a note on Monday. "We therefore expect elevated attention around governance, China/export controls, customer relationships and compliance to persist even with the CEO question resolved."

— CNBC's Luke Fountain, Seema Mody and Dan Mangan contributed to this report.

#2807. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

#2806. Does the Nvidia-led rip mark the market bottom? Maybe not

The key accelerant to Monday's ripping rebound was a shocker. But the level that halted the rally was right where you'd expect.

The S&P 500's 4.7% surge on Monday lifted it out of a steep-sided hole following last week's washout selling storm. Its high for the day was just under 7300, a level last seen on Thursday, corresponding to the index's summertime low and representing the floor of the former multi-month trading range. In other words, the pop stopped at the point from which the tape had broken down; the index slipped three-quarters of a percent from there by the close.

More than a third of the S&P's gain was provided by the 31% jump in Nvidia shares alone following Jensen Huang's stunning reinstatement as Nvidia CEO. Here, too, the stock closed right near the Iran-selloff low, which it cracked through some 12 days ago. It remains under all the relevant moving averages and trend lines.

This isn't to minimize or denigrate these moves of rare magnitude, but simply to place them in context. The market was going to attempt a solid bounce no matter the news. S&P 500 futures were indicated higher by more than 2% even before the Huang bombshell struck before Monday's open.

By near-universal agreement, last week's flush had generated the kind of indiscriminate liquidation, oversold conditions and urgent hedging that typically set up at least a snapback rip. Some of these readings were as extreme as near the April 2025 low following the tariff panic, though not all.

The reinstallation of Huang at the apparent behest of the White House triggered an emotional, spring-loaded ramp in the AI trade, a collective panic by the under-invested. When the government jolts the market with a policy shift like this, it not only assuages a prevailing fear in the market, but it grants psychic permission to investors who were frantically selling one day to ravenously buy back the same stocks the next.

It's slightly ironic, given that a common line last week on the recent market setback was that – unlike in the tariff panic or the Iran war corrections – there was no single policy reversal that could short-circuit the selloff and trigger a ferocious recovery.

And maybe there still isn't. Such one-day comebacks from a deeply oversold state are to be respected but not fully trusted. Looking back at prior one-day gains of such amplitude – or prior sequences of a daily drop and gain of more than 5% in the Nasdaq-100 – generate a fair number of episodes inside of nasty bear markets, along with a few consequential market bottoms.

A few further observations and questions at a potentially tenuous tactical moment for the markets:

-The standard playbook is to assume that we've put in "a low, not the final low." Oversold rallies can carry on a while – say, to the 20-day moving average, which for the S&P 500 is up another 3% from here – without getting escape velocity. Graybeards argue for keeping the market on a short leash, expecting there will at least be a retest sequence. Prudent to assume so, though it's inescapable that V-shaped bottoms have become a bit more common in recent years, as recently as at the end of March, on the merest hint that President Trump would consider ceasing Iran hostilities without forcing open the Strait of Hormuz.

-Index corrections of at least 10% that happen quickly have generally been more associated with temporary bull-market dips than the start of nastier downturns. This latest S&P 500 correction happened over 33 trading days, among the dozen quickest. That would be reassuring, except that places it right between the declines of November 2007 and February 2022, which indeed were the start of bear markets.

-Remember that the pain of this setback was inflicted after months in which Wall Street was constantly extolling the virtues of a "broadening" market (equal-weight S&P 500 outperforming) and marveling at the elegant rotation into "quality" and defensive stocks that was supporting the S&P 500. Those mechanics can work fine in phases, but never allow for a proper market-wide reset and, clearly, they don't inoculate the tape from eventual washouts.

-We might have a new binary trading relationship to watch: It's early in the game, but with the Nvidia news jolting most AI plays higher, Anthropic in its third trading session since its IPO fell 1.4%. If Nvidia is an advocate for and backer of global open-weight models, then its revival could dim the view of Anthropic's closed frontier AI platform, while diminishing the advantage provided by Anthropic's $200 billion cash capex war chest.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "The growing evidence of capitulation is encouraging. Bearish sentiment was arguably the market's best attribute coming into last week and still is. Sentiment became even more extreme over the past week, pushing our model to its lowest reading since the 2025 bottom, and reaching levels comparable to what we saw during the 2022 bear market. The six-month outlook has improved, but whether this is a V bottom thanks to the return of Jensen Huang, or the market undergoes basing and a possible retest, remains unclear."

-I'm making my way through "The AGI Chronicles: The Inside Story of the Race to Create an Artificial Superintelligence," the new book by former New York Times technology writer Kevin Roose, a smart, engaging and brisk history of AI development and the personalities driving it. For a shortcut through the material, Roose's discussion on the New Yorker Radio Hour podcast is worth a listen.

-In returning to Nvidia headquarters Monday without his signature leather jacket, Jensen Huang was executing a Reverse Fonzie.

In the first season of "Happy Days," the mega-hit sitcom of the '70s and early '80s, ABC forced the Fonz character to wear a cloth windbreaker, fearing he'd alienate viewers who'd take him for a "hoodlum." Creator Garry Marshall argued to the network that a leather jacket was a safety requirement for motorcycle riders and pushed to let him wear. When the show took off, the leather became standard.

Huang is now perhaps trying to seem a bit less edgy, humble and relatable absent the biker attire.

President Trump's direct intervention to press Nvidia's board to reinstate Huang, juicing the Nasdaq for at least a day, reintroduced investors to the notion that stocks can only fall so far without the administration trying to punish the sellers.

But can Wall Street enjoy both a Trump put and a Fed put? If conviction about the AI trade and its aggressive spending is revived, and the economy runs hotter, and private-sector borrowing rises, will that not invite a more hawkish Federal Reserve – at just the time that many were handicapping a dovish pivot given the unsettled markets of late?

It's a live question. More than a month ago, as a fourth-quarter post-election rally was becoming consensus, I granted the setup but with a key caveat:

"Still, against most probabilities, 2018 happened. By which I mean a 19% S&P 500 peak-to-trough decline in the fourth quarter of a midterm-election year, one that saw total S&P 500 earnings grow by more than 20%. The 2018 tailspin had followed a massive momentum crescendo and reversal several months earlier, which gave way to an erratic tape that couldn't absorb what seemed at the time relatively unthreatening Federal Reserve tightening actions."

Since then, after the market retrenchment, the 2018 analogy has grown so popular that I doubt it will unfold exactly that way. Still, as Renaissance Macro noted after Monday's rally, Nvidia's gain, which helped the S&P 500 recoup half of last week's loss, "may have fixed the S&P 500 loss but not the Fed, the [Treasury] curve or cash credit."

Higher real yields remain a potential constraint on equity valuations. This chart from Wellington Management shows the yield on 30-year Treasury Inflation Protected Securities – the long-term real yield – is now above the S&P 500's free-cash-flow yield for the first time in half a generation.

In a related vein, JPMorgan economist Bruce Kasman in his 2027 outlook describes a certain narrative dissonance: "Financial markets have projected strong earnings expectations, in part, because central banks have preached immaculate disinflation and been supportive of easy access to credit. The 2027 landscape — of above-potential growth, a sixth year of elevated inflation, and a renewed labor market tightening — poses a meaningful challenge to the magical thinking held by markets and central banks."

#2801. Commercial real estate activity falls 14% in October, as interest rates rise

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

Interest rates moved higher in September, and that had a swift impact on commercial real estate deal-making. After settling in the low-to-mid-4% range, the yield on the 10-year Treasury moved above 5% and has been wavering around there with the potential to move even higher due to stubborn inflation.

As a result, commercial real estate activity dropped 14% in October from September, according to an index from LightBox, a commercial real estate data platform. Of the index's three components, commercial property listings fell 20% from September, environmental site assessments were down 1% and lender appraisals dropped 11%.

The appraisal measure is at its weakest level of the three and is now down 17% from a year ago. It shows that both lenders and borrowers are stepping back, unable to pencil deals and move from listing to closing.

"What makes this so interesting is that during tariffs, it didn't dip. During $100 oil earlier this year, it didn't dip. When we saw interest rates marching up the first half of this year, it didn't dip. In fact, it kind of increased, and now we've fallen off a map in October," said Manus Clancy, head of data strategy at LightBox. "It's like we've reached this straw that has broken the camel's back in terms of people saying, 'Okay, enough is enough. I can't keep putting money to work without some clarity on where interest rates are going.'"

Clancy said deals were happening earlier this year because investors had gotten more comfortable with where interest rates had settled. The office market was beginning to recover as more companies ordered workers back. Multifamily, which had seen a glut of new construction, was also starting to see vacancies fall and rents rise.

This takes the market back to 2023, when deal-making froze up because interest rates had shot up sharply from very low, pandemic-induced levels. As property values repriced, the gap between what the sellers wanted and what the buyers were willing to pay widened. That is what is happening now.

"The wheels of progress just completely stop," said Clancy.

Whether they are refinancing a loan or getting a new loan to purchase a property, investors, developers and property owners are now deciding to wait to see if interest rates come down at all. While the Federal Reserve is expected to raise its lending rate further, an end to the war with Iran could bring Treasury yields down. Especially for refinances, borrowers would be out millions of dollars at these higher rates. On the buy side, deals simply don't work anymore at higher rates.

"I think it is disorienting as people try to rebase values, and in the midst of transactions, you're seeing value retrades at the 11th hour, deals getting pulled, and volume slowing down yet again in the market," said Ryan Cotton, head of real estate at Bain Capital in a recent Property Play podcast. "It's definitely a confusing and complicated time to be a real estate investor."

Higher interest rates were the leading factor for the slowdown in October, but LightBox cited another contributing factor. Lending remains open, but sentiment has weakened. It points to the Mortgage Bankers Association cutting its 2027 growth forecast for commercial mortgage lending from about 8% to 1%. In addition, the Commercial Real Estate Finance Council, or CREFC, Sentiment Index fell 17.5% in the third quarter of this year to a three-year low.

"People are lending for construction. They're lending for office conversion. They're lending for new purchases," noted Clancy. "We look at this index as kind of the tip of the spear. That if something's going to go sideways, this is the first indicator that people are stopping the ordering of appraisals, and we think that this portends that the next quarter will show a lot of decline in bank lending on commercial real estate."

CNBC's Property Play with Diana Olick covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, delivered weekly to your inbox.

#2800. Federal Reserve announces College Fed Challenge winners

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

#2796. Kushner-backed OakNorth secures U.S. bank charter as global digital banks push into America

The U.K.'s OakNorth has secured a federal bank charter, saying it is the first foreign digital bank to do so, as international competitors vie for U.S. market share.

The charter clears OakNorth, which is backed by Jared Kushner's private equity firm, to acquire Michigan-based Community Unity Bank, subject to an $80 million capital injection, according to approval documents issued by the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency on Nov. 19.

The deal, which is expected to close by the end of 2026, would allow OakNorth to provide loans, take deposits, and offer other banking services across all 50 states.

Kushner's private equity firm, Affinity Partners, took an 8% stake in OakNorth in 2025, with backing from major Gulf investors, including the sovereign wealth funds of Saudi Arabia and Qatar. SoftBank is also an OakNorth investor.

The bank reported $295 million in pretax profit for 2025 in its annual report, up from $280 million the previous year and close to three times the $100 million in profits five years prior.

It said principal losses on $20 billion in credit facilities issued over the past 10 years totaled £9 million, or less than 0.05%. Its 1.4% return on assets placed it among the top five banks in Western Europe and in the top 1% of commercial banks globally, according to The Banker's rankings.

"I would say that the level of animal spirits in the U.S. is always higher, right? So the momentum of business and the optimism is always a step up," OakNorth CEO Rishi Khosla told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Monday.

He said U.S. economic growth, investment in artificial intelligence and infrastructure spending would create opportunities for the bank.

OakNorth has focused on lending to lower mid-market businesses rather than competing for consumer customers in a crowded U.S. market.

The lender targets businesses with annual revenues of $5 million to $100 million with loans worth $5 million to $50 million. The bank entered the U.S. market in 2023 and has lent about $3.5 billion to U.S. businesses to date. The U.S. accounted for 40% of its $12.4 billion in new loans in 2025.

"These are really growth companies which are scaling up, and given that focus, you really haven't had an institution in the last decades that focus on that customer segment, and that's opened up the opportunity for us," Khosla said.

He cited commercial real estate lending as a particularly strong growth opportunity in the U.S.

Securing the charter is notoriously difficult, with many regulatory hurdles. It took OakNorth 20 months, a restructure of the bank's corporate ownership, and relocating its holding company from the island of Jersey to the U.K. to meet Federal Reserve requirements.

Other international fintechs seeking a foothold in the U.S. have struggled to gain traction. Monzo and Revolut withdrew their initial U.S. applications for a full banking charter between 2021 and 2023 after regulatory pushback.

Monzo closed its U.S. operations in April just four years after it launched, amid a crowded consumer market. "We have made a strategic decision to focus on scaling in our home market and Europe and to step away from the U.S.," the company said.

But the regulatory landscape has since shifted. Revolut and Brazil's Nubank have received conditional approval for U.S. banking charters from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency.

Dutch digital bank Bunq reapplied after withdrawing its 2023 application, but its second application was denied in August.

Another U.K. digital lender, Starling, is also seeking U.S. growth. It sells its Engine banking software to mid-tier U.S. lenders and is looking to acquire a domestic bank to serve business clients.
