#2790. Six key questions raised by Jensen Huang's return to Nvidia

Jensen Huang made a dramatic return as Nvidia CEO on Monday, less than a week after board Chairman Harvey Jones said directors did not intend to reinstate him. The board later voted unanimously to bring Huang back.

Nvidia shares surged as much as 36% during the session and closed 31% higher.

The reversal followed a steep decline in Nvidia shares, which closed at $125.53 on Friday even after the company reported strong third-quarter results. A petition calling for Huang's return had drawn more than 200,000 signatures, according to its organizers.

The rally spread across the broader market, with the Nasdaq Composite gaining 6.5% and the S&P 500 rising 4.7%, each posting its strongest session since April 2025.

Nvidia's board said it had received input from senior U.S. government officials and that its investigation into Huang's conduct would continue.

Still, the board's reversal, President Donald Trump's administration's role and continued scrutiny of Nvidia's China business leave major questions for both investors and lawmakers.

Sen. Jack Reed, a member of the Senate Banking Committee, urged Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick on Monday to appear before the committee to discuss the case.

Reed did not lay out a specific list of questions for the two officials, but his statement cited reporting that Trump had been briefed by intelligence officials led by then-National Security Adviser Marco Rubio before intervening in support of Huang's return.

Reed's call adds to mounting pressure on both Nvidia and the Trump administration to explain what they knew and when.

Rep. Gabe Amo, D-R.I., vice ranking member of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, said Huang’s reinstatement could broaden congressional scrutiny rather than bring it to a close.

“I think it frankly broadens the scope of the query,” Amo told CNBC, adding that lawmakers should also examine administration officials involved in decisions affecting Nvidia and chip sales to China.

Amo said he expects the planned closed-door interview with Huang on Dec. 1 to proceed and that Congress would continue seeking answers both privately and publicly in the coming months.

“He should start purchasing his plane tickets to Washington,” he said. “It is abundantly clear that this is ripe for questioning by Congress.”

On Nvidia's third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 17, Jones said the board had accepted Huang's resignation and did not intend to reinstate him.

Less than a week later, the board reversed course, saying Monday that it had unanimously voted to bring Huang back after discussions with him and input from senior U.S. government officials.

Trump said he had spoken with board members and that his administration provided assurances based on the information available to it. But the board has not publicly detailed what it was told.

"The board decided to do what we wanted to do," Trump told CNBC, adding that he believed his intervention had been important.

"I've been speaking to the board directly and indirectly, and I felt very strongly about it," he said.

Trump also called into an Nvidia board meeting to argue for Huang's return, Semafor reported, citing people familiar with the matter.

Reed has called on Huang to disclose his communications with Trump and on Nvidia to release a transcript of the board's call with the president.

Lawmakers are still seeking answers about the alleged smuggling of Nvidia products to China, their possible diversion for military use and possible advancement of Chinese AI capabilities.

The Senate Banking Committee has scheduled Huang to testify on U.S. export controls on Dec. 3. House lawmakers have also confirmed closed-door interviews with Huang on Dec. 1 and Nvidia General Counsel Tim Teter on Dec. 2.

"The Nvidia board's brief internal investigation and reinstatement of Jensen Huang do not erase the national security threats and questions that must be addressed," Reps. John Moolenaar, Rick Crawford and Brian Mast said in a joint statement.

Their inquiry may also examine what Nvidia knew about the customers and resellers involved, as well as what controls the company had in place.

Other lawmakers have also sought to preserve the paper trail. Sens. Elizabeth Warren and Chris Coons had asked senior administration officials on Nov. 11 to retain records related to Nvidia exports of advanced AI chips to China and associated national security concerns.

Nvidia has promised to hire a chief compliance officer and establish a committee overseeing national security and export controls. The board's investigation into Huang's conduct also remains underway.

Josh Gilbert, eToro's lead analyst for APAC and the Middle East, said investors will be watching whether those new safeguards carry meaningful authority.

Gil Luria, head of technology research at D.A. Davidson, said Nvidia has a limited window to prepare for the possibility that a future president may be less supportive of Huang.

With Trump's term ending in two years, Luria argued that Nvidia needs a clearer plan for who could take over if Huang were forced to leave again.

Gilbert pointed to Nvidia's roughly 75% revenue-growth forecast for the next financial year as evidence of strong near-term demand. The longer-term question, he said, is whether customer orders and artificial intelligence investment can sustain that pace.

Luria told CNBC that concerns about the broader AI trade remain, including the cost of financing data centers, potential overbuilding by neocloud companies and obstacles to building new facilities.

Bernstein analyst Stacy Rasgon said worries about whether AI spending is achievable and sustainable were not caused solely by Huang's departure. UBS, meanwhile, flagged Nvidia's roughly $390 billion in supply commitments and the need to keep demand flowing through its ecosystem.

Gilbert said part of Monday's rally reflected investors recovering earlier losses rather than pricing in new growth. Bernstein restored its "Outperform" rating and kept its $200 price target, while UBS maintained its $220 target.

"For the share price, it comes down to whether Nvidia can keep delivering as it has done over recent years," said Gilbert, adding that Huang's return could ease some concerns about Nvidia's longer-term outlook.

"The real worry has been what happens beyond next year. Having Huang back at the helm goes a long way to easing those concerns, because he's the one who sits down with the biggest customers and gets those multi-year orders over the line," he said.

— CNBC's David Sucherman, Justin Papp and Eko Prasetyo contributed to the story

Greg Newsome II is going to miss more than just his revenge game.

#2788. Lakers eye biggest test of season with more questions than answers

LOS ANGELES — It feels like we've already experienced various phases of the Lakers between the start of the season and Monday's 109-94 win over the Utah Jazz.

The team's fast launch out of the gates was highlighted by a nuclear start to the season for Luka Dončić. Despite surviving a regression to what feels far below the mean, the Lakers came out of their worst stretch of the season on Monday with a 4-1 record and 11 wins in their last 12 games to remain atop the conference with a 15-3 overall record.

And yet, with their biggest test of the season against the San Antonio Spurs on the horizon, Los Angeles's last week of games has assuredly raised some questions. The first being: are they ready for it?

"It'll be good to see," Walker Kessler said of the forthcoming clash against the Spurs. "It'll be a challenge for sure, but I think we're ready. I'm excited to go out there and compete."

San Antonio is tied with the Lakers for the league's top record. Los Angeles has three days off before Friday's game while the Spurs play Portland before the marquee showdown. Nonetheless, the matchup will be perhaps the most anticipated of the season as both teams will play for early bragging rights.

The biggest wildcard — and another one of the Lakers' biggest questions — is if Austin Reaves will be available. The star guard has missed the last five games with a left heel bruise and his absence has been felt in all facets of the game.

When Dončić is on the floor, opposing defenders can focus more on him. When he's off, the time typically reserved for Reaves to run the lineup has seen the Lakers struggle to create offense. Head coach JJ Redick said pregame that he's optimistic Reaves will return to the lineup against San Antonio, but actions speak louder than words.

Just as Redick pointed out in the lead-up to Monday's game, the Lakers had the No. 1 offense through the first 12 games with Reaves on the court for a majority of that time. Since then, with Reaves out, their offense has struggled. And yet, again, they're 4-1 without Reaves, which is a major reference point for the team.

"It's encouraging," Kessler said. "Obviously Austin is a very important piece to this team. And so seeing how we compete without him and not at our full strength is good. It builds resiliency because [you're] not going to be always at full strength going in the season. Things happen. It's a long season

"So it's encouraging to see, but, obviously, we miss him and excited for him to be back out there."

Trials and tribulations are part of an 82-game season. But the fact that the Lakers have endured all of this and still stay atop the league early in the season might be the answer to every question.

"Every day we continue to get closer and closer," said Lakers rookie Cameron Carr after his career-high nine-point outing on Monday. "We have a very unique group of guys and I think God put us here for a reason. We just connect. … The puzzle pieces are put together."

#2787. Op-ed: Trump’s Nvidia board intervention put a price on national security. Our adversaries will take note

To learn more about the CNBC CFO Council, visit cnbccouncils.com/cfo

President Donald J. Trump has accomplished something extraordinary. He directly inserted himself into the boardroom of one of America's most strategically important companies, Nvidia, helping to reverse a corporate leadership decision, and personally vouched for restored CEO Jensen Huang as having a clean bill of health regarding the national security investigations into his business activities with China.

On Monday, after Nvidia's board reinstated Huang following his resignation amid a counterintelligence controversy and allegations that Alice Huang, with whom he had an affair, is under suspicion of being a Chinese intelligence asset, Wall Street celebrated with a historic market bounce. But the market's enthusiasm should not obscure a far more consequential question circulating in Washington, D.C.: Does American national security have a price?

What happened at Nvidia raises the frightening possibility that it does, and it fits a recent pattern that is exactly what geopolitical rivals around the world want to see. Consider the economic pressures surrounding the Iran war. Disruptions to energy markets, shipping, supply chains, and consumer prices have complicated American strategic decision-making. These are notably different circumstances, but they raise the same troubling question about the costs of defending American national security interests, and when they become economically unpalatable to those in the Trump administration.

Beijing, Tehran, and Moscow are watching — as are our allies and partners — and they all may agree on one thing: America has an Achilles' heel that military and technological superiority cannot overcome. It is possible to impose sufficient economic pain to alter Washington's strategic calculation with friends and foes alike. Bleed American capital markets, disrupt commerce, squeeze consumers, and eventually Washington may look for an exit.

Despite the narrative that the president is weaving, there is a fundamental difference between responsibly weighing economic consequences and allowing adversaries to conclude that they can impose an economic veto over U.S. national security policy. Trump told CNBC in an extensive interview on Monday morning that when it comes to national security, "losing the brain power of somebody like Jensen ... would be a far bigger threat than anything we're talking about."

"The bigger impact was the long term. The bigger impact was you know he's got other chips in his brain that nobody else knows about, including me, that nobody else understands," Trump said. "This was a big loss. This was a tremendous loss. And you saw that it affected the market. It affected even, I think it even affected the perception of our country in the world," the president said.

And that's true, but not only in the way Trump thinks it did.

This is not about whether Huang is a brilliant executive or whether Nvidia is enormously important to American competitiveness. Both are true. The question that America, particularly its government, must ask is whether Huang's economic importance justifies subordinating corporate governance, interfering with counterintelligence procedures, and stymying national security oversight.

Trump characterized the Justice Department's assessment as having exonerated Huang. Yet Nvidia's board reinstated him while its own review remained open.

The allegations involving reported contacts with Chinese intelligence assets do not establish legal wrongdoing. However, presidential personal assurances are not substitutes for independent counterintelligence assessments and investigations or congressional oversight. Monday's rising stock price might only measure what shareholders believe Huang at the helm of Nvidia is worth, while discounting or ignoring the unresolved counterintelligence risk that he may still pose to Nvidia and the entire artificial intelligence ecosystem.

The Trump administration, Silicon Valley, and Wall Street may believe the matter is closed.

"That thing's like a rocket ship going in the wrong direction. Now it's a rocket ship going in the right direction, and it's taking the whole market," Trump told CNBC about Nvidia's 31.5% Monday gains.

One glaring vulnerability still on the horizon is what Washington does not know. If Beijing possesses additional damaging information, it controls whether, when, and how that information might be weaponized in an ongoing and now reignited strategic competition between the U.S. and China. Beijing retains the capacity to reopen this matter at a time of its choosing. Chinese intelligence could drip-feed disclosures, compel repeated explanations from Huang and the president, and transform Monday's declaration of resolution of this matter into a prolonged national security vulnerability.

There is also a striking irony here. Some pundits are comparing Trump's intervention to China's state-controlled market. To be sure, the Chinese government has a very heavy hand in its economy, but one thing the Chinese Communist Party will never do is put an individual ahead of national security and political authority despite the economic impact that this person or company is perceived to have. Xi Jinping and China's Communist Party have demonstrated a willingness to make almost the opposite calculation about national security and economic growth. Under Xi, national and political security generally takes precedence over the interests of individual companies and brilliant executives.

Consider Jack Ma. In 2020, Beijing halted Ant Group's planned $37 billion initial public offering and subsequently imposed sweeping regulatory restrictions on Ma's business empire. Beijing accepted substantial economic consequences to reinforce national security and political authority. This is not an argument for importing China's authoritarian model into American corporate governance. But the strategic asymmetry is noteworthy to revisit in this latest comparative context. Beijing has demonstrated that even its most celebrated entrepreneurs are not indispensable to the party and the nation. Washington risks signaling that some of its entrepreneurs are in fact indispensable to the state, which ironically potentially makes them even bigger intelligence targets for foreign adversaries.

As an economic matter, the commercial realities of Nvidia's China business make this situation even more perplexing. China, including Hong Kong, accounted for just 9.1% of Nvidia's reported global revenue in fiscal 2026, down from 19.2% the previous year. Its latest fourth-quarter guidance assumes no Chinese data center compute revenue. Nvidia did change the way it reports geographic sales, to customer headquarter address, in late 2025. But anyone familiar with Chinese strategic documents, including the 15th Five-Year Plan, should understand that Beijing has no intention of becoming permanently "addicted" to American chips. Its objective is technological self-reliance, not expanding Nvidia's market share and by extension its dependency on them.

Whatever the exact figure, I cannot think of a number that is worth selling out U.S. national security over, but it certainly isn't  9.1%, which is also a number that will continue to shrink. Furthermore, if the AI ecosystem is critical to our economy and our national security, then selling advanced chips to China or loosening export controls is the opposite of what we should be doing because the chips are used to juice the compute of Chinese LLMs that compete with ours, and to increase the effectiveness and lethality of Chinese weapons systems. The questionable sales of Nvidia chips in Asia, which are at the heart of this story, support neither our economy nor our national security. They undermine them both.

There is also an extraordinary corporate governance precedent that extends well beyond Nvidia and Huang, one that is escalating and growing more bold at each turn. While Trump has intervened in the markets before, in cases including U.S. Steel and Intel, he has never gone to this level, making the argument that a company is so critical to the national security of the nation that at any point its decision making could require executive branch intervention and specific direction.

Based on the president's rationale, what should the boards of key national security relevant companies like Boeing, Lockheed Martin, Palantir, Anduril, SpaceX, or even Rio Tinto — whose jointly owned Resolution Copper project in Arizona could eventually supply up to 25 percent of America's copper demand — expect?  Does their strategic importance justify presidential intervention in executive appointments, succession planning, corporate strategy, or commercial relationships? A precedent established by one president becomes an idea, option, and instrument available to future presidents, including those with fundamentally different political preferences.

All of this brings us to Congress. If you take Trump at his word, that Nvidia's strategic importance justifies extraordinary executive branch intervention, it also justifies extraordinary legislative intervention and oversight. Article I grants Congress authority to regulate foreign commerce. Congress established the statutory framework governing executive export-control authority through legislation, including the Export Control Reform Act of 2018. Those authorities were delegated, not surrendered. The president may have driven this Nvidia CEO decision by telephone. Congress possesses something more enduring: the authority to establish the rules through legislation. The Senate Banking Committee's scheduled December 3 hearing on export controls provides an opportunity to begin.

Congress should do much more than demand classified briefings on the counterintelligence concerns.  Lawmakers should pass, with veto-proof majorities, requirements for stronger board-level counterintelligence responsibilities, mandatory defensive briefings to vulnerable tech executives and innovation sector leadership, much tighter export-license oversight with enforcement mechanisms that have teeth, and enforceable reporting requirements. Legislative proposals including the AI Overwatch Act, Match Act, and Remote Access Security Act must be passed as soon as possible. Nvidia and its leadership may warrant considerably tighter legal and regulatory scrutiny given its importance as the fulcrum of the AI ecosystem.

It is important for Congress to remember that the legislative branch is a co-equal branch of government, not a rubber stamp or talk shop providing us with commentary about the outcome of executive branch decisions. They have an active and constitutional role to play in our democracy. They need to get it together and get to work. If Nvidia is important enough for the president to enter its boardroom, it is important enough for Congress to exercise its constitutional authority over the rules governing this entire process.

—By Dewardic McNeal, Managing Director and Senior Policy Analyst at Longview Global, and a CNBC Contributor

#2786. This Anthropic proxy play is getting traders' attention – here's where they see it going

Who says you can't tailgate an IPO?Traders are preparing for this week's blockbuster Anthropic IPO in different ways, including buying options on one publicly-traded fund that invests in private assets.The Destiny Tech100 (DXYZ) is a publicly-traded, closed-end fund that invests in equity, equity-linked instruments, or shares of private funds and venture companies to gain exposure to private companies. Currently, 14% of the fund's assets are tied to Anthropic, according to the company's website.Options volume on the fund jumped to more than 10x the 30-day average Monday, according to Cboe LiveVol data. Of the 42,300 contracts traded by midday, 41,800 were calls, with just 141 puts likely opened by buyers.

Just shy of $5.5 million in options premium on DXYZ traded on Monday, with almost all of it tied to calls, but just marginally more spent buying than selling calls, according to SpotGamma. The highest-volume contract is currently the 30-strike call expiring Friday, a 90-cent contract that needs the fund to rally 19% into the weekend to pay off.Traders may want to be wary of how the fund traded around the SpaceX IPO: DXYZ surged almost 150% in May before unwinding all the gains in the weeks ahead of SpaceX's debut. Since then, the fund has barely moved, trading around $26.There are other bullish signs surrounding Anthropic's IPO in the derivatives markets. Current prices for perpetual futures on the blockchain exchange Hyperliquid are implying an opening price of $98, a big pop from the $70-$78 price range quoted in the company's amended S1 filing. The AI giant is expected to price Wednesday evening and begin trading on the Nasdaq on Thursday under the ticker ANT.

#2785. AG Blanche, FBI’s Patel dodge questions about Jensen Huang investigation

Attorney General Todd Blanche and FBI Director Kash Patel on Tuesday dodged questions about the conclusions of an investigation President Donald Trump cited to push Nvidia's board to bring back Jensen Huang as CEO.

Asked by a reporter whether the Department of Justice had investigated Huang and the results of that probe, Blanche said, "What the president said is that he talked to a lot of people going into the decision that he made to encourage the board to reinstate him."

"And so I'm not going to get into communications I have with the president," Blanche said at a news conference in Washington.

Huang was reinstated as CEO of Nvidia on Monday morning, less than two weeks after resigning amid allegations he had affairs with a purported spy for China and two Nvidia employees plus concerns that advanced semiconductor chips made by his company had been diverted to China in violation of U.S. law.

In an interview with CNBC on Monday, Trump was asked what led him to personally confirm to Nvidia's board that Huang was not a national security risk.

"We've done a very thorough investigation, and the investigation showed that he did, I mean, he had bad judgment having to do with somewhat unrelated incidents, but he did nothing wrong," Trump replied.

Trump in a Truth Social post on Monday said the U.S. government had no reason to believe Huang's return to the company "poses any risk to National Security," and that Blanche had confirmed the government "does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

Nvidia, in its statement Monday announcing Huang's return, said, "The Board received input from senior levels of the United States government, and based on that input, the Board is confident that Mr. Huang's reinstatement poses no national security risk."

Blanche on Tuesday said, "Nvidia is a good corporate citizen."

"Obviously, if Nvidia was the target, or was the subject of some criminal investigation, or they were obstructing or thwarting our investigation, then that wouldn't be the message that the president is saying to the American people," he said.

Patel did not answer a reporter's question of whether the FBI has spoken to Huang and whether the FBI had been made aware of an assessment by U.S. intelligence agencies that Huang's reported paramour, Alice Huang, was an asset for China's spy agency.

Jensen Huang is not related to Alice Huang, who was an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based customer of Nvidia's semiconductor chips.

The DOJ and Commerce Department on Nov. 17 said they were investigating Megaspeed for potential diversion of Nvidia chips in violation of U.S. export law.

Asked how the DOJ could rule out Jensen Huang having potential criminal liability given the fact that the investigation remains ongoing, Blanche replied, "So, when you say the export investigation is still ongoing, I don't know what you're talking about."

"We have hundreds, if not more, export investigations going at any time at the Department of Justice," Blanche said.

#2782. Utah drops criminal charges against Kalshi founders after company suspended operations in state

Utah on Tuesday dismissed criminal charges against Kalshi and its two co-founders following the decision by the prediction market platform on Monday to suspend operations in the state.

The state originally filed the 14 criminal charges against the company and founders Tarek Mansour and Luana Lopes Lara on Oct. 28, claiming that Kalshi operated an illegal gambling operation in the state.

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission — the federal regulator for prediction markets which views itself as the sole body that can develop rules over the growing industry — sued Utah the day before the state announced its charges.

Mansour and Lara were slated to appear in a Utah district court on Dec. 4, according to court documents.

However, Kalshi suspended operations in Utah on Monday, citing legal uncertainty. Utah Attorney General Derek Brown celebrated the decision in a statement announcing the move to dismiss the state's charges.

"Every company in this space should take note," he said. "If they promote, support, or enable gambling, we will hold them accountable."

Brown's office noted in the statement that dismissal is without prejudice, meaning if Kalshi were to begin operations again in the state the charges could be refiled.

"Good faith jurisdictional disagreements should never be criminalized," Kalshi's general counsel Yaakov Roth said in a post on X. "We stand ready to work with State AGs on reasonable interim measures until courts resolve the dispute over who regulates prediction markets."

While Kalshi faces legal setbacks in Utah, it faced some relief in New York on Monday when a state judge denied an emergency temporary restraining order against the company to halt its operations in the jurisdiction. The court will hear arguments on the matter again in January.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2780. Fantasy Basketball: Landry Shamet, AJ Dybantsa are among players on hot streaks — but what do the stats tell us?

Fantasy basketball managers should keep an eye on these hot streaks. Landry Shamet is shooting 51.5% from 3 over his last five games, Donovan Clingan is racking up double-doubles and blocks, and Jalen Smith is in the middle of a career year for the Bulls. Corey Kispert and Aaron Wiggins are sleepers who are fueling the Hawks' 11-6 start with sharpshooting, while rookie AJ Dybantsa is heating up in Washington. Whether you want a long-term hold or a short-term streamer for roto, category or points leagues, here is what each trending player's recent stats mean for your lineup.

Shamet has been on fire lately, averaging 16.4 points on 55.8% shooting, including 51.5% from deep over the last five games, helping the Knicks go undefeated over that stretch. He reached the 20-point mark twice in the five games and has hit it three times on the season, which is remarkable considering he only topped 20 points twice through all of last season.

He is averaging 26.9 minutes per game through 17 appearances this year, which is up from 23.0 minutes last season and just 15.2 per game in his first campaign with the Knicks. In his extended playing time, he is averaging a career-high 2.5 made 3-pointers per game, while shooting a strong 44.2% from beyond the arc this season. He has also been very good from the foul line, knocking down 81% on 1.2 attempts per game, helping fuel a career-best 66.4% true shooting percentage. With a championship under his belt, the 29-year-old, ninth-year guard may have found a new level of confidence that has him on track as one of the most valuable shooters in the league.

Clingan is emerging as one of the league's most complete young big men, averaging 11.6 points, 11.3 rebounds and a career-high 2.1 blocks in 26.1 minutes per game. His scoring and rebounding are down marginally from last season, but that's a product of reduced minutes rather than any decline in play, as the Trail Blazers added offseason talent that reshaped the rotation.

The third-year center ranks eighth in the league with eight double-doubles and has already clocked in impressive season highs of 24 points, 18 rebounds and five blocks (twice). His 11.3 rebounds per game also ranks fifth in the league. In addition to his counting stats, he provides efficiency, shooting 59.4% from the field, while averaging 1.0 made threes on 2.4 attempts per game, which amounts to elite 41.5% shooting from long range. He is also extremely reliable when fed in close, as he is averaging 73.5% shooting from within five feet of the basket. Clingan can be expected to continue to excel in his role without needing to be the center of attention.

Kispert is off to an anomalous start to the season, managing to provide a notable impact for his squad in only 12.4 minutes per game through 10 appearances. The sixth-year marksman is averaging 9.3 points on 55.9% shooting, including a blistering 48.8% from deep, with 2.0 made 3s per game.

Wiggins is doing similarly impressive work by averaging 13.9 points on 45.5% shooting, including 40.5% from deep, with a career-best 2.8 made 3s in 25.8 minutes per game. On the other hand, he is hitting a career-low 61.5% from the foul line on a total of 13 attempts through 17 games this season.

Both Kispert and Wiggins' ability to provide such a valuable contribution in limited time has been a big help in the Hawks getting out to an 11-6 start to the season. The sharpshooting is also a defining aspect of the team's identity, as they have been heavily reliant on the long ball, ranking sixth in the league with 14.8 made 3s per game.

Smith is working on a career season, averaging highs across the board, with 14.1 points, 7.5 rebounds, 2.2 assists, 0.7 steals and 1.0 blocks in 25.3 minutes per game. He is also shooting an efficient 54.7% from the field, which is all the more impressive considering he is also averaging a career-high 2.1 made 3-pointers on 5.1 attempts per game, for a 40.3% shooting mark from long range. Additionally, it can not be overlooked that he is hitting 74.2% from within five feet of the basket, which is helping make up for the increase in 3-pointers and boosting his impressive overall shooting percentage.

Smith got a boost from the fact that Nic Claxton was sidelined for the first few weeks of the season, and his strong play has allowed him to keep the starting role. The seventh-year big man has been showing flashes that he had more to offer for a while now, and it finally looks like the tide has turned in his favor. He should continue to build on the momentum of his strong start, which has included four double-doubles and season highs of 26 points, 13 rebounds, three steals and four blocks.

Dybantsa has hit a hot streak, averaging 19.2 points, 8.4 rebounds, 3.6 assists and 1.6 steals over the last five games, reaching the 20-point mark three times and securing two double-doubles in that stretch. He is shooting 44.3% from the field but just 23.8% from deep over the five games, which, although unremarkable, is still better than his season average of 42.3% from the field and 23.5% from deep.

The rookie has a lot of competition for the spotlight within the Wizards' lineup, as Trae Young, Anthony Davis, and Kyshawn George are leading the team in scoring. However, Dybantsa is still averaging the second-most shots per game on the team, with 14.4 attempts per outing. Moreover, he is doing well getting to the line, with 4.9 attempts per game, but he could still work on his efficiency in that area, as he is hitting 72.1%, for an average of 3.5 makes per outing. He is also doing a decent job of taking care of the ball, with an average of 2.3 turnovers in 34.0 minutes of action per outing. He should continue to develop more flow to his game and is already showing a positive trajectory towards being able to lead his team offensively.

#2779. Congress shreds Nvidia's 'outrageous' decision to bring back Huang: 'Failure of character'

Members of Congress from both parties on Monday expressed outrage and incredulity, and doubled down on their calls for investigations, after Nvidia announced it has reinstated Jensen Huang as its president and CEO.

The resurrection of Huang, who had resigned from the artificial intelligence behemoth just 11 days earlier, raises more questions about the company's decision-making and the Trump administration's national security assessments, on top of existing concerns about Nvidia's advanced AI chips being diverted to China, the lawmakers said.

"It's inexplicable to me why they want this guy back as CEO," Rep. Don Bacon, R-Neb., a China hawk who is retiring from Congress, told CNBC in a phone interview. "To me it's a failure of character."

President Donald Trump told CNBC in an extended interview later Monday morning that he personally intervened to convince Nvidia's board to bring Huang back, saying, "I made the deal."

Bacon, chairman of the House Subcommittee on Cyber, Information Technologies, and Innovation, said he wants the next Congress to conduct thorough oversight, while questioning the Trump administration's motives.

"Somebody's responsible for this, and it may be" Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, whose department is responsible for setting U.S. export controls on AI chips, Bacon said. "But I think we should get to the bottom of this."

Huang, who is scheduled to testify before multiple committees on Capitol Hill starting Dec. 1, is "going to get high scrutiny in a bipartisan way when he comes next week," Bacon said.

Huang's closed-door appearance before a trio of House committees is on the schedule, as the panels seek answers on the national security implications of Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, MS NOW reported. The Senate banking committee announced after Huang's reinstatement that he will appear for a public hearing Dec. 3.

John Fisher of Trident, a spokesman for Huang, declined to comment on the Nvidia CEO's scheduled appearances.

"He better be there," Rep. Gabe Amo, D-R.I., a member of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, told CNBC about Huang's scheduled Dec. 1 appearance. His reinstatement will only intensify congressional probes into Nvidia and its government connections, Amo predicted.

"I think it frankly broadens the scope of the query, and it makes Howard Lutnick and his dealings — supporting his nepo children's operation at Cantor Fitzgerald — party to inquiry as well as any other administration official that has facilitated a status quo in which chips can be sold to China or Chinese operated intermediaries who don't intend good things for the American people," said Amo, referring to the financial firm run by Lutnick's sons that has business ties to Nvidia.

A Cantor spokesperson didn't comment directly when asked about Amo's comments, saying the firm "holds no material proprietary position in Nvidia" and noting its Nvidia positions "arise primarily from equity swaps executed for clients."

The Commerce Department didn't immediately respond to requests to comment on Bacon's and Amo's remarks.

Nvidia in a statement Monday said it "received input from senior levels of the United States government" and that it is confident that Huang's reinstatement "poses no national security risk."

David Rudenstine, a constitutional law professor and national security scholar at Cardozo School of Law, said the frenzy around Huang's comeback is secondary to lingering national security concerns."The real responsibility for national security is with the administration and not with the CEO of a private company," Rudenstine told CNBC."You're opposed to China getting the chips. You recognize that the intermediary companies are sending chips to China, and you don't put a stop to it. I don't get it," he said.

The CEO's reinstatement, while celebrated by the White House and in Silicon Valley, led to heightened calls on Capitol Hill to seek answers on AI chip exports to China and what the Trump administration's role has been in facilitating that.

"We are sending high-end computer chips to China, and yet we're saying we want to win the AI war. It's a contradiction, and it's a failure of leadership in the White House," Bacon told CNBC.

Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon, the top Democrat on the Senate Finance Committee, told CNBC in an emailed statement that Huang's return "proves that under Trump, Vance, and Rubio, national security is for sale to the highest bidder."

"We will all pay the price of letting China plunder America's cutting edge technology. Democrats will be pushing for accountability for anyone in the administration that put corrupt deals over our country's security," Wyden, who is also a Senate Intelligence Committee member, said.

Trump in a Truth Social post Monday added that Attorney General Todd Blanche "has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

Lawmakers, who have already launched multiple investigations related to Huang, Nvidia and related entities, quickly filed in to question those conclusions.

Sen. Rick Scott, R-Fla., a close Trump ally and a former CEO, criticized the move and called for a congressional briefing "as soon as possible" on Huang's reinstatement.

"Nvidia's board is free to vote however they want, but the federal government owes the American people answers about what actually happened," Scott told CNBC in an emailed statement. "This looks terrible on its face, and it drives home the need for Congress to be informed on the full impact of Jensen Huang's actions on our national security and economy."

Rep. Scott Peters, D-Calif., a member of the House Energy and Commerce Committee, told CNBC that it's still not clear to what degree Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy might have compromised national security.

"The fact that the president of the United States is involved in putting him back in there, presumably because maybe he has stock or he just cares about the stock market, it's really reached a bizarre place," Peters said. "And obviously that man has not learned anything from the election about what people think of him, or he doesn't care."

Monday's move also gave fuel to lawmakers who have already questioned Trump, his administration or Nvidia.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., said in a statement via the Senate Banking Committee, where she is the top Democrat and is expected to become chair in January.

Sen. John Cornyn, R-Texas, more tersely questioned the official assurances that Huang poses no risks to national security.

"No national security risk? Based on what?" he wrote on X.

Rep. Raja Krishnamoorthi, D-Ill., who serves on the House Select Committee on the Chinese Communist Party, in an X post called Nvidia's decision to reinstate Huang "outrageous."

"We still don't know what information Chinese intelligence may have obtained from him, and neither the government nor Nvidia's own board has completed its investigation," wrote Krishnamoorthi, who is also a member of the House Oversight and Intelligence panels.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan, Garrett Downs and Luke Fountain contributed to this report.

#2778. Chiefs sign tackle Fred Johnson to active roster

Early on Tuesday, NFL Network's Ian Rapoport reported that the Kansas City Chiefs would be signing tackle Fred Johnson — who joined their practice squad last week — to the active roster. Johnson had previously been with the Philadelphia Eagles before being waived on November 13 after seven appearances with the team in 2026.

The player himself appeared to confirm Rapoport's scoop later in the morning.

Later in the afternoon, the Chiefs made the move official. To create a spot for Johnson on the active roster, fullback Ben VanSumeren was placed on the Reserve/Injured list. VanSumeren left Kansas City's Week 11 win over the Arizona Cardinals with a knee injury.

The Chiefs have navigated uncertainty at the tackle position all season in the absence of 2025 first round selection Josh Simmons. The team also appears set to play on Thursday against the Buffalo Bills without right tackle Jaylon Moore, who is recovering from an ankle injury. Moore has missed the Chiefs' last two games and has not practiced this week.

Kansas City was fortunate to be able to add a veteran lineman with positional versatility who has played in 86 NFL games. Whether Johnson will see playing time against Buffalo after being with the team such a short time remains to be seen.

"Fred, he's had time," head coach Andy Reid said before Tuesday's practice. "Enough time? We'll have to see on that. He seems like a sharp kid. He has a ton of experience. He's got skills. We'll see how all that goes as we roll."

Johnson's short time with the Chiefs has made an impression on their most famous player.

"Obviously, he's played football for a number of years — and played really good football," observed quarterback Patrick Mahomes. "He can play guard and tackle, so that versatility is going to help out a ton. We'll continue to try to get him more and more work within our offense and within our scheme. I know one thing — he's a big guy, and he's athletic. You see why he's had success in this league. It's about getting him more and more accustomed to how we do things here."

With Tuesday's transactions, the Chiefs will have an opening on their practice squad.

#2776. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2775. Peterson: Here’s what Bessent’s about-face on Jensen Huang reveals about Trump and China

For a minute there, it seemed like someone in the Trump administration might actually be willing to stand up to China.

For about a week and a half, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was the face of an emerging harder line on Nvidia over the potential risks its co-founder Jensen Huang's affairs posed to national security. Bessent was giving voice to China hawks who had felt sidelined by the Trump administration's willingness to sell artificial intelligence chips to  China.

They see China's AI program as a major threat, and want to restrain its growth by cutting off the supply of advanced chips to China. The resignation of Huang, who pressed the Trump Administration to allow the exports, seemed to signal that the hawks had found new influence in Washington.

By this weekend, Bessent had softened his public resistance to Huang's reinstatement. That came just in time for Nvidia's board to reappoint Huang as CEO at President Donald Trump's urging.

Whether any real damage was done to national security isn't clear. But it is safe to say that the incoming Democratic majority in Congress won't take Trump at his word when he said on CNBC Monday that the Department of Justice "did a pretty good investigation" that exonerated Huang.

Congress will look deeply into the worries Bessent himself raised about Huang's acknowledged romantic relationship with a woman The New York Times and others have reported was a Chinese intelligence asset.

Nvidia's board said it had received assurances from top U.S. officials and had concluded his return wasn't a national security risk.

A top concern is whether China was able to use the affair to help divert Nvidia's high-end artificial intelligence chips toward helping its military. Bessent in a Nov. 13 interview with CNBC outlined the stakes.

"Any leakage is very frustrating because it hurts the — it hurts our great companies and in the AI race it gives the Chinese an advantage," he said.

Bessent said that his department and others had wanted to blacklist Megaspeed, the company previously run by Alice Huang, Jensen Huang's paramour. But "Commerce has the pen," Bessent said.

The Commerce Department is headed by Secretary Howard Lutnick, Bessent's rival.

Putting Huang back at Nvidia wasn't originally Bessent's preference. He said in a Fox interview on Nov. 15 that the board should "go outside the company" to find a new leader. But by this past weekend, Bessent had apparently gotten on board with Trump's wishes.

"Jensen is on the outside now," Bessent told CBS on Sunday, in trying to explain his apparent policy shift.

Commerce has since taken steps to tighten some restrictions meant to prevent U.S. chips from helping China advance in the AI race.

But there is bipartisan worry about the Nvidia episode. "I think we need to catalog the damage that's been done," Sen. Tom Cotton, R-Ark., told The Wall Street Journal. He said Lutnick had not responded to letters he sent saying the administration should be "taking the threats of communist China more seriously."

But Trump has clearly put an upper limit on how aggressively anyone in his administration can push back on China. The president said Monday that he was swayed by the pageantry in his visit to China last week.

Xi "met me at the plane, which is something they've never done, which was a sign," Trump said.

Trump said he felt like he needed to intervene to back Huang because the stock market was plummeting in the wake of Nvidia's troubles — a development he also blamed on Democrats' midterm victory.

"I watched our country lose $5 trillion because of a bad election," he said. "If I didn't get involved, you'd probably have a very sad Nvidia for a long time to come." Now, its stock performance will be "unbelievable."

Bessent on Sunday played down the market risks. The company "continues to be fantastically profitable, and there is near unlimited demand for their chips worldwide," he told CBS.

Lutnick has embraced Trump's transactional urges. He has backed deals in which the U.S. takes equity stakes in companies such as Intel, saying the taxpayer deserves some of the upside.

Lutnick in January said he had heard Huang pitch Trump on allowing Nvidia to sell its relatively more advanced H200 chips into China.

"There are strong — two sides. We shouldn't sell them any or we should sell them." Where does he stand? "Right in the middle," Lutnick told Bloomberg. Trump decided to go ahead with the deal.

Bessent has said he is "neither a hawk nor a dove" on China, and is simply trying to manage the ultra-sensitive relationship between the world's two biggest economies. But the upshot of the Huang affair seems to be that Bessent wasn't able to sway the president on a matter that seemed cut-and-dried just days ago.

Trump said he discussed whether Huang should return to Nvidia with Lutnick, Bessent and Vice President JD Vance. "Howard Lutnick was very good. Very, he's very professional," Trump said on CNBC Monday.

"I call them the opposites, Scott and Howard," Trump added, before declaring them "both good."

#2774. Cowboys Week 12 opponent: Another look at the 2026 Eagles

The Dallas Cowboys are coming off one of their best offensive performances of the season, but they won't have much time to celebrate. Dallas returns to the field on Thanksgiving to face a familiar division rival, the Philadelphia Eagles, in a rematch that comes with a few new wrinkles.

The Eagles are coming off a 19-16 upset victory over the Pittsburgh Steelers, snapping a seven-game losing streak and improving to 3-7. More importantly, they have a new quarterback under center: former Cowboys quarterback Joe Milton III.

Philadelphia signed Milton off Dallas' practice squad in October, and he is now getting an opportunity to lead the Eagles' offense. It's an unusual twist in an already important NFC East matchup, especially considering the Cowboys handed Philadelphia a 38-21 loss the last time these teams met. The Eagles are still trying to find consistency, but Sunday's win showed that they shouldn't be taken lightly.

The biggest storyline for the Eagles heading into Thursday is undoubtedly Milton. The former Cowboys practice-squad quarterback is now preparing to make his third NFL start, and his first against his former team. Philadelphia turned to Milton after Jalen Hurts was shut down and they struggled to find consistency at quarterback. The move has given the Eagles a different look under center.

Milton's performance against Pittsburgh was far from perfect. He completed 10 of 20 passes before his final completion, and he missed several opportunities downfield, including an overthrown pass to DeVonta Smith that could have resulted in a 68-yard touchdown. He did, however, make the most important throw of the afternoon.

With just over a minute remaining and Philadelphia facing third-and-six, Milton connected with Smith for a seven-yard gain to move the chains and effectively seal the victory. It was a meaningful moment for a young quarterback who has struggled with consistency, and it showed that head coach Nick Sirianni is willing to put the game in his hands.

Milton has a powerful arm and the athleticism to make plays outside the pocket. Through his first two appearances this season, he has completed 29 of 55 passes for 310 yards, with no touchdowns or interceptions. He has also rushed 14 times for 71 yards.

The Cowboys should know better than anyone that Milton has the arm strength to push the ball downfield. The challenge will be making him prove he can consistently deliver accurate throws and make good decisions. Dallas needs to pressure him, keep him contained in the pocket and avoid giving him easy opportunities to extend plays.

Regardless of who's playing quarterback, Philadelphia's offense still revolves around Saquon Barkley. The running back has dealt with injuries and inconsistent production throughout the season, but he remains one of the most dangerous players on the Eagles' roster. Through 10 games, Barkley has rushed for 371 yards and four touchdowns on 86 carries, averaging 4.3 yards per attempt.

He showed signs of life against Pittsburgh, finishing with 75 yards on 17 carries and a 38-yard touchdown run. Unfortunately for Philadelphia, Barkley left the game after getting shaken up late, and his status heading into Thanksgiving will be worth monitoring.

The Eagles have other backfield options, including Dameon Pierce, Tank Bigsby and Will Shipley. However, none brings the same combination of explosiveness and big-play potential as Barkley. The first meeting between these teams reminded everyone what Philadelphia can do on the ground. A hobbling Barkley rushed for 62 yards on seven carries, averaging nearly nine yards per attempt.

Dallas ultimately controlled the game by establishing its own running attack and finished with 204 rushing yards. That will be an important matchup again, particularly with the Cowboys' defense still trying to find consistency against the run. If Barkley is healthy, Dallas cannot afford to let him get comfortable. The Eagles need their running game to take pressure off Milton, and the Cowboys need to make Philadelphia one-dimensional.

Milton's biggest challenge will be getting the ball to Philadelphia's playmakers consistently. DeVonta Smith is back in the lineup after missing significant time with a hamstring injury. He returned against Pittsburgh and finished with five receptions for 41 yards on 12 targets. The numbers weren't spectacular, but his presence gives Milton a reliable receiver who can create separation and make plays downfield. Smith has 31 receptions for 351 yards and two touchdowns this season.

Tight end Dallas Goedert has also been a productive target, recording 32 receptions for 346 yards and two touchdowns. He remains an important part of the passing game, particularly when Philadelphia needs a dependable option underneath.

Rookie Makai Lemon has emerged as another contributor, leading the team with 38 receptions for 315 yards. Dontayvion Wicks has added 284 receiving yards, while Darius Cooper has 259 yards and four touchdowns. Dallas' secondary has struggled this season, especially in preventing explosive plays. The Cowboys cannot afford to give Smith and the rest of Philadelphia's receivers free access downfield. Making Milton work through his progressions and forcing him to be accurate will be essential.

The Eagles' defense has had its share of problems this season, including a difficult performance against Dallas in October. The Cowboys finished with 416 total yards, rushed for 204 yards and scored 38 points in the 38-21 victory. Philadelphia failed to record a sack, tackle for loss or quarterback hit in that game. Javonte Williams ran through the defense for 155 yards, and Prescott threw three touchdown passes without an interception.

That performance should give Dallas confidence heading into the rematch, but the Eagles looked much better against Pittsburgh. Philadelphia recorded five sacks, held the Steelers to 255 total yards and limited them to just 73 rushing yards on 20 carries. The defense also held Pittsburgh to five conversions on 17 third-down attempts and kept the Steelers out of the end zone on two red-zone opportunities.

Nolan Smith has been one of the team's most productive pass rushers, leading Philadelphia with six sacks. Defensive tackle Jalen Carter has added five sacks, while Byron Young recorded two against Pittsburgh.

The Eagles still have a talented secondary led by cornerbacks Quinyon Mitchell and Cooper DeJean. Mitchell left Sunday's game with a hamstring injury, so his availability is uncertain. Still, significant personnel questions remain. Linebacker Zack Baun is on injured reserve, and Philadelphia traded defensive tackle Jordan Davis. Those are meaningful losses for a defense that will need to find a way to slow down a Dallas offense that has scored 91 points over its last two games.

The biggest question is whether the Eagles can replicate Sunday's defensive performance. If they can generate pressure and limit Dallas' rushing attack, they have a chance to make this game interesting. If they allow the Cowboys to control the line of scrimmage again, it could be a long afternoon.

The first meeting between these teams offered a pretty clear blueprint for how Dallas can beat Philadelphia. The Cowboys rushed for 204 yards on 38 carries, averaging 5.4 yards per attempt. Williams led the way with a career-high 155 rushing yards and a touchdown. Prescott added three passing touchdowns, and Dallas scored on seven of its nine offensive possessions.

Philadelphia simply couldn't stop the run.

That needs to be the starting point again. Dallas has found more balance offensively over the last several weeks, and the running game has become an important part of its success. Against an Eagles defense that just held Pittsburgh to 73 rushing yards, the Cowboys will need to establish that physical identity early.

Defensively, the game plan should be centered on making Milton uncomfortable. He has the arm strength to make big plays, but his accuracy and decision-making remain question marks. Dallas should pressure him without losing its discipline, particularly when he tries to escape the pocket. Easier said than done for this bunch.

The Cowboys also need to keep Barkley from taking over the game if he plays. If Philadelphia can establish its running attack, it can protect Milton and control the tempo. Dallas has struggled to consistently stop opposing offenses this season, and it cannot afford to let the Eagles dictate the terms of the matchup.

There is also a little extra motivation on the other sideline. Milton knows the Cowboys' organization, and he will undoubtedly want to make a statement against his former team. But Dallas has the more established offense and already showed it can take advantage of Philadelphia's defensive weaknesses.

The Eagles are coming off a win, and the rivalry always brings a different level of intensity. Still, the Cowboys have an opportunity to make another statement in the NFC East. If they can run the football like they did in October and force Milton to win through the air, they should have a good chance of leaving Thanksgiving with another division win.

#2772. Celtics have bigger issues, but can strengthen grasp on NBA Cup bid in DC

Celtics have bigger issues, but can strengthen grasp on NBA Cup bid in DC originally appeared on NBC Sports Boston

The Boston Celtics wrap up what's been a thus-far winless four-game road trip on Tuesday night in the nation's capital. And while the NBA Cup is not likely front of mind for the team — and simply righting the ship amid a four-game losing streak is priority No. 1 — the team has a chance to strengthen its grasp on East Group C during a visit to the Washington Wizards.

The Celtics own a 2-0 record in Group C play, while both the Wizards and Hawks — their final two group opponents this week — have both stumbled in group play. Boston has two pathways to being one of the four teams to advance in Cup play: Win Group C outright, or earn the East wildcard spot by having the best point differential among the three East runner-ups.

Boston currently owns the best point differential in the East at +53. Both the Hawks and Wizards sit at +13 in the event that a Group C tiebreaker is necessary.

With wins over Washington and Atlanta this week, Boston would win Group C outright and likely host the East wildcard winner in Round 1, if they maintain the best point differential among all group winners.

A win over Washington but a loss to Atlanta would still leave Boston with a strong chance to advance as the East wildcard, but would require a healthy point differential. A loss to Washington but win over Atlanta would leave all three teams tied at 3-1 in Cup play and make point differential the tiebreaker for both winner and runner-up in the group.

Two losses this week and Boston would be eliminated from Cup play and would instead compete against two non-Cup qualifiers over the second week of December.

When the Celtics won their first two Cup games, we wondered if there was any chance that Boston and Philadelphia could meet in later-round play, potentially accelerating Jaylen Brown's first game back at TD Garden.

Philadelphia is still alive in Group B play, but a 1-1 record and negative point differential complicates its path. The Sixers would need wins over Miami and Indiana, plus the Knicks to beat Cleveland, in order to win Group B via head-to-head tiebreaker over Miami. Two wins could help Philadelphia push for the East wildcard spot, but a minus-19 point differential through two games basically requires them to dominate those final games.

#2771. Anthropic shares fall below IPO price. Traders see even more pain

Anthropic shares are back trading below the IPO price after a 13% sell-off Tuesday and a 1.5% drop Monday, despite a broad rally in most big tech artificial intelligence names. Trading under $76 on Tuesday, the stock's now 8.5% below its initial public offering level and 34% beneath its intraday high of $115.

It took just four days for ANT to fall under its offering price, compared with 21 trading days for SpaceX, which rallied out of the gate.

Options volume on the stock is picking up as the shares drop and implied volatility jumps to 96, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Almost 500,000 options traded by noon ET on Tuesday for a total premium of about $250 million, the sixth-most active single-stock options in the market, SpotGamma data shows.

Almost 300,000 puts traded versus about 200,000 calls, but the sentiment implied by the trades was not overtly bearish, and judging by the biggest single trades of the day, traders are divided on what happens next.

The biggest single option trade of the day was a $7.5 million purchase of almost 11,000 75-strike puts expiring in mid-December. The trader paid above the ask, suggesting an eagerness to get the trade done at a cost of $6.85, according to SpotGamma. With Anthropic trading at $77, the position needs Anthropic to drop 11% by expiry to pay off.

"Anthropic remains bumpy as investors add risk to other names," said noted tech analyst Dan Ives.

Only one obvious call purchase appeared among the top 10 trades: a 3,000-lot purchase of 85-strike calls expiring Jan. 15 that cost $7.10 each, for a total of $2.2 million. That trade needs Anthropic to rally 19% within the next two months to break even.

Arguably the most interesting action was a string of almost a dozen put sales, ranging from $400,000 to $1.6 million each, all within moments of each other, selling the 120-strike puts expiring Dec. 18. The puts bring in $40 each, meaning the breakeven for the trade is at $80. Selling in-the-money puts in high-volatility stocks is often a way for traders to get long stock without the capital requirements of buying the underlying equity.

#2770. How Meta is using Instagram to get an ad boost from its Muse personal AI agent

Meta's ad-free Muse personal artificial intelligence agent is already helping the social media giant boost its advertising business, and it's happening through Instagram, multiple people told CNBC.

Instagram is a major advertising revenue engine for the social media giant, and the company is leveraging existing habits on the platform by prompting users to organize their saved posts in Muse.

About 20% of daily Instagram users save at least one post to the app per day, according to a person familiar with the details, who asked not to be named because the information is not public. Many of those saved items, the person said, are either shoppable items or ads.

A senior executive told CNBC that Muse, even without ads, "is still very valuable for our ads business because it raises conversions for advertisers." It does this by reducing friction in the shopping process and allowing users to go back to items later, said the executive, who asked not to be named because the details have not been reported publicly.

"It gives advertisers a way to get more ROI for the dollars they're already spending, and that's all because Muse is helping people shop," the executive said.

Mark Zuckerberg announced in Meta's last earnings call that 25% of Muse's users shop via the agentic tool — it's the most common use case for Muse — and the average purchase price is over $200. Zuckerberg said the company plans to take a small fee from transactions made through the agentic platform.

All that information about what people are shopping for with their agent is a valuable asset to target ads more precisely within Instagram. If consumers complete purchases with their Muse, that builds Meta's new revenue stream.

Meta's efforts to drive transactions seem to be paying off, and the buzz around Muse has made it a leader in agentic shopping.

In the week ending Nov. 22, 51% of all agentic orders were done by Meta's Muse, according to transactions measured by Forter Intelligence, an e-commerce analytics and cybertechnology company.

Meta dominates agentic AI shopping market share, according to Forter, which said that SpaceX's Grok held a 24% market share in that same time period, followed by 22% to OpenAI's ChatGPT. The remaining 3% is divided between Google and startup Instinct.

Meta is expected to eventually launch ads in Muse, though multiple people said that it's not under any financial pressure to do so and tends to take its time building an audience before monetizing it.

The people said that putting ads in the Muse feed, which is more akin to a newsfeed instead of responses to specific queries, would be a natural way for Meta to test ads on the platform.

#2769. Fernando Mendoza draws praise in Week 12 NFL Power Rankings for Las Vegas Raiders

Fernando Mendoza is two games into his tenure as the Las Vegas Raiders' starting quarterback. While he's 0-2 in those outings, there have been a handful of promising moments, and he's coming off a three-touchdown performance against a strong Denver Broncos defense. As a result, Mendoza has drawn plenty of praise in the Week 12 NFL Power Rankings across the league's media.

The Las Vegas Raiders have lost both games in which Fernando Mendoza started. Yet, the resiliency seen from this year's No. 1 overall draft pick has been impressive. The Denver Broncos built a 17-0 lead Sunday. Mendoza is going to have his ups and downs, of course. At the same time, the rookie signal-caller led his team to 17 straight points before everything started to fall apart again. In doing so, he opened some eyes among the Raiders' division rival.

"There certainly was a lot of respect for Fernando Mendoza in the Broncos locker room," James Palmer reported. "What stood out was how calm he was in the pocket and his feel in the pocket, as well as how unpredictable he is when he starts to move. Most young players have something they do and go to it over and over again. Mendoza scrambles out a variety of ways and scans the field well while doing it." — Brent Sobleski

To be fair, the No. 1 pick did play a role in everything falling apart for the Raiders. After tying the game, he missed Jalen Nailor on an open fourth-down conversion and had the pick-six that sealed the team's fate. However, the defense was undoubtedly the bigger issue. Denver scored touchdowns on its two possessions after the contest was knotted up at 17 with about five minutes left in the third quarter.

Also, it's hard to win when the other team's offense scores 31 points and has 350 yards from scrimmage. Granted, the Raiders outgained the Broncos by 19 yards despite running four fewer plays and losing the time of possession battle by about 7.5 minutes. Mendoza has to avoid the big mistake, but he's far from the biggest reason the Silver and Black lost on Sunday.

I'm fascinated to see what the Raiders can do to improve their defense this offseason because I know this offense can put up points. Fernando Mendoza produced the first strong game of his career, and the Silver and Black delivered an entertaining contest against a superior Broncos squad in Denver, which is a good sign about where Las Vegas is headed in the bigger, multiyear picture. — Nick Shook

This picks up right where the section above left off. Las Vegas ranks third-to-last in yards allowed (382.0) and dead last in points allowed (29.7) per game, signalling big changes are needed on defense in the offseason.

Defensive tackle should be the starting point, and it will be interesting to see how the young corners — Jermod McCoy, Hezekiah Masses and Darien Porter — finish the season. Adding another edge-rusher wouldn't hurt either, but the Raiders will get Keyron Crawford back from injury next season to give the position group a young talent to turn to.

Something to be thankful for: Fernando Mendoza's learning curve

After making his first two starts against Seattle and Denver, the rookie quarterback is a predictable 0-2. The good news is he threw for 267 yards and three touchdowns against the Broncos, and the road gets easier from here. "I was really impressed with (Mendoza's) off-schedule stuff," Broncos coach Sean Payton said. "I was more impressed with his size and his escapability than maybe I saw on tape." — Josh Kendall, Chad Graff

It's funny, being able to make out of structure plays was viewed as one of the Indiana product's weaknesses coming out of college. But something that stood out about his game during the Mendoza Mania series this summer was his ability to make defenders wrong when scrambling; either using his legs to move the ball or dumping it to receivers when defenders don't plaster. We've seen that pop up a few times in the last two weeks, and, as Payton mentioned, Mendoza's escapability was much better from start one to start two.

Bowers will be eligible for an extension in the offseason, and Las Vegas shouldn't waste time locking down a franchise cornerstone. Despite battling knee and ankle injuries this season, he still has been productive, with 50 catches for 676 receiving yards and six touchdowns. Having Bowers around for the early stages of quarterback Fernando Mendoza's career will be essential for the No. 1 pick's development. Negotiations between Bowers and the Raiders could get interesting, given the number of times he has lined up on the outside this season. — Ryan McFadden

McFadden brings up a point that helps add some context/encouragement about Mendoza's first two starts: Bowers has only been on the field for 11 snaps in the last two weeks, per Pro Football Focus. That leaves the rookie quarterback with an underwhelming crew of pass-catchers, and he's still managed to turn heads. The problem is that's music to Bowers' agent's ears, as he's about to get paid like a 'WR1' when the time comes.

Luckily, the Raiders can buy themselves some time in the negotiation by picking up his fifth-year option this spring. According to Over The Cap, that will cost $17.35 million for 2028, which would make him the fifth-highest-paid tight end in terms of dollars per year. Seeing as the two-time Pro Bowler will undoubtedly make more than that when his contract is up, exercising the option is a no-brainer.

Some coaches spend minutes saying absolutely nothing. Klint Kubiak is the polar opposite in terms of meaningful words per second in pre-halftime interviews. — Conor Orr

We're veering away from the main topic here; Orr is referencing Kubiak's halftime interview where he said the refs need to call the game both ways when asked about the penalty discrepancy between the two teams last Sunday. The Raiders were flagged nine times for 63 yards, while the Broncos had just two penalties for 10 yards in the contest.

Even more interesting, Vegas averages the third-fewest penalties per game (5.7) this season while Denver is tied for 16th (7.1), according to TeamRankings. The one that stood out to me the most was Cody White getting called for offensive pass interference, which wiped away a touchdown, while the play below by Courtland Sutton wasn't penalized. Then again, this is nothing new for Raider Nation.

#2768. Rockets clamber over Clippers 116-104

Tonight the Rockets took on a Los Angeles Clippers team, a team that is staggering its way through a dense fog of official opprobrium, that is almost a mirror image of the Rockets. The Rockets stand at 13-5, the Clippers at 4-12. The Rockets are 8-1 at home, the Clippers 0-8 on the road. Would the Clippers find their way to a first road win tonight? If you read the title of this recap, obviously not.

The game, though, was surprisingly close until the end. We can't say that the Clippers fired up the tank machine late, because they don't have their own picks for the next five seasons. There's no reason for the Clippers not to try to win, and try they did. It's just that the Clippers team is currently an island of misfit toys, a clipper ship of the damned, a rag tag band of hard luck sports millionaires just trying for one last score. Well, not really, but it is a somewhat odd team.

The team features Darius Garland, Brandon Ingram playing for the first time all season, just to annoy me, cross town transfer Rui Hachimura, Derrick Jones Jr, shooting around 50% from 3pt range until this game, and the ageless wonder, Brooke Lopez still very large and able to shoot 3pters at 38.

It's not a terrible lineup, nor is it particularly good, with the exception of Darius Garland, who looks healthy for the first time in several years, and you can see the quickness, and ability to get anywhere, and shoot from anywhere, on the court. He scored 26pts on 10-21 shooting, 4-10 3pt, and had 8ast to 1 TO. That's about the extent of actually dangerous Clipper players.

That said, this game was simply awkward. The Rockets let the Clippers hang around, seemingly unable to string together both baskets and stops for very long. The Rockets would build a lead out to around 10 points, and the Clippers would bring that down to around 6 points, or less. There might have been a bit of mild panic that the Clippers would indeed manage to get their first win on the road, if someone got hot late for them, and the Rockets scoring dried up.

The Rockets scoring did not in fact dry up, mainly due to a Rockets player who has been quiet for much of the season, rarely asserting himself to lead the way. A deferential role player known to some as "The Slim Reaper" or "KD". This low shooting volume veteran, Kevin Durant, lead the way tonight with 32 points, on 11-19 shooting, 4-6 3pt, 9 rbs, 4 ast, 2 TO, 1 blk. He did this in only 33 minutes. In fact none of the Rockets starters played big minutes tonight, Durant's 33 minutes was the highest on the team. Alperen Sengun continued his good form, going 20pts. 7-11 FG, 1-2 3pt, 8 rbs, 7 ast, an uncharacteristic 5 TO, and 2 blk. Amen Thompson had a great return to form, and was able to get in close to score, also notching 20 pt, on 9-12 FG, 1-1 3pt, 4 rbs, 5 ast, 0 TO, 1 blk.

This game might have been easier if the Rockets had followed a first quarter where they only allowed 15 points to the Clippers, to 25 of their own, by not letting the Clippers back in the game, losing the second quarter 28-22.

The Rockets got a better, if not outstanding, performance from the bench than they did in Denver. Tari Eason had one of the most Tari Eason sequences ever tonight as well. He followed a powerful late clock dunk by ripping the ball from a Clipper at mid court. He then strolled down the lane, alone, to try, well, I don't know what sort of stylish shot he had in mind. He absolutely bricked this uncontested attempt, which was rebounded by a trailing Clipper, whom Eason immediately fouled. Tarissimo!

We saw 25 minutes from Marcus Smart, as he seems to be creeping towards starter level minutes, despite not contributing all that much on the scoresheet. What joy he brings to Ime Udoka's heart, I suppose we will never quite know, but I suspect it's less than he brings to mine.

One big takeaway from this game is that the Rockets moved the ball quickly, and decisively for the most part tonight, aside, oddly from Fred VanVleet who only had 4 ast. The team overall had 36 assists on 46 made baskets, against 13 turnovers. That sort of performance is good to see, as most of the shots generated were good ones, and we saw less of the over passing that sometimes characterizes the team when they want to move the ball around. The Rockets had six players with four or more assists tonight – Durant (4), Sengun (7), VanVleet (4), Thompson (5), Eason (4), Sheppard (5). Durant lead the team with 19 attempts, so it truly was a group effort on offense.

There's not much more about a game that felt awkward at times, but not exactly in doubt. The Rockets take on the Suns, looking for vengeance, in their final NBA Cup game on Wednesday.

The Rockets continue rolling through the regular season at a great win rate. Until next time, Happy Thanksgiving to all of you, and yours.
