#2766. Trump Account contributions from friends and family pass $1 billion mark, Treasury says

Families and friends have collectively contributed more than $1 billion to Trump Accounts since the July 4 launch, bringing total deposits to more than $9 billion, the U.S. Department of the Treasury said Tuesday in an announcement exclusively provided to CNBC.

The $1 billion in Trump Account deposits come from more than 3 million individuals, according to the agency. That total does not include the government's seed money or philanthropic gifts from donors such as Michael and Susan Dell.

"The American people are investing in Trump Accounts with a generosity that represents the finest instincts of our national character," U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in a written statement.

"Trump Accounts are a transformative government program for American children. It is the largest government start-up in American history," Internal Revenue Service CEO Frank Bisignano said in the statement.

Also known as 530A accounts, Trump Accounts are open to any U.S. child under 18 with a Social Security number.

Parents, guardians, grandparents and others can collectively contribute up to $5,000 per child per year in after-tax dollars up until the year before the beneficiary turns 18. The annual contribution limit is indexed for inflation after 2027.

Children born from 2025 through 2028 may receive a one-time $1,000 deposit from the Treasury. Other funds may also be available, depending on certain criteria.

Most recently, Snowflake chairman Frank Slootman and his wife, Brenda, announced they would give $1,000 to every child in Montana born in 2023 and 2024.

The Treasury rolled out temporary regulations at the end of September that allow Trump Accounts to hold stock donations, a move meant to pave the way for more philanthropic gifts, according to Bisignano.

These rules make it more attractive for donors, according to Ben Henry-Moreland, a certified financial planner with advisor platform Kitces.com.

Wealthy founders and shareholders can avoid the capital gains taxes that would otherwise be triggered by selling appreciated stock and donating the cash proceeds. However, it also means some children's wealth accumulation could be tied to the performance of individual companies.

The Trump Accounts milestone comes as more billionaires and companies pledge support to the investment account for kids.

In an exclusive interview with CNBC's "Squawk on the Street," Bessent on Nov. 20 announced an Anthropic co-founder would make a "large gift" to Trump Accounts over the next few weeks.

A source briefed on the announcement confirmed that the Anthropic co-founder Bessent referred to is Tom Brown and that it is part of his broader philanthropic plan.

"This is the perfect vehicle to donate to the children of America, and they will receive 100% of the proceeds," Bessent said.

Elon Musk wrote in an X post that he plans to give every kid in America a share of SpaceX sometime in 2027, which experts say may come via Trump Accounts.

In a separate funding announcement, SpaceX President Gwynne Shotwell said she would donate around 2 million shares of her and her husband's SpaceX stock to the accounts.

On Monday, Altimeter Capital CEO Brad Gerstner, founder of Invest America and an architect of Trump Accounts, told CNBC's "Halftime Report" that more large-scale donations may be in the pipeline.

"I want everybody to own shares of Nvidia, in Anthropic, in OpenAI," he said. "We created 70 million stock accounts — free stock accounts — for every single citizen under the age of 18 to do just that, and we have huge announcements coming."

#2765. Jensen Huang to face DC grilling the same day Nvidia releases new products at GTC

Jensen Huang is back as the CEO of Nvidia, and is now tasked with selling the company's artificial intelligence vision while also defending himself in Washington.

As Nvidia employees celebrated the return of their leader to the company's headquarters, Huang told the gathered crowd, "I'll be a better CEO the second time around."

Huang will need to be a better CEO to head off the issues surrounding Nvidia and the broader AI industry exposed by Huang's exit. The challenges include a proliferating roster of investigations and hearings from Congress, regulators and state-level officials, as well as technical and competitive questions about how long Nvidia's currently dominant graphics processing units can remain on top.

"There's nothing prohibiting him from doing what he does on his day job," said Katten chair and former federal prosecutor Gil Soffer. But investigations take significant prep time, Soffer said. "You don't just walk in cold."

At Nvidia's GTC conference in Washington, D.C., next week, the company will attempt to convince customers, partners and suppliers that it is developing the future of AI. At the same time, lawmakers will be pressing its newly reinstated CEO over what he knew and when about Megaspeed executive Alice Huang.

Huang, before his scandals, was scheduled to give the GTC DC keynote speech on Dec. 1 to discuss new Nvidia products and the trajectory of the AI ecosystem. In the past, the two-hour speech was the highlight of the event with hordes of fans full of the Huang-inspired "Jensanity."

"People will want to see that it's business as usual, which hopefully for Nvidia shareholders will mean that Jensen Huang announces some new exciting products," said Babson College associate professor Peter Cohan.

Right now, Nvidia has Ian Buck, vice president of hyperscale and high-performance computing, filling the slot. A representative for Nvidia declined to answer multiple questions about whether Huang would appear at the company's event.

On the same day, Huang is scheduled to speak with three House committees — Foreign Affairs, Select Committee on the CCP, and the Select Committee on Intelligence — behind closed doors, where his interview will be transcribed.

"The NVIDIA board's brief internal investigation and reinstatement of Jensen Huang do not erase the national security threats and questions that must be addressed," the chairs said in a joint statement. They said that Huang has confirmed he will appear for the transcribed interview on Dec. 1.

Next Tuesday's interviews are just the first of a slew of investigations.

On Dec. 3, just as Nvidia's conference is wrapping up, the Senate Banking Committee will hold a hearing titled "Protecting America's AI Advantage: Oversight of U.S. Export Controls." Sen. Tim Scott, R-S.C., who chairs the committee, said Huang will publicly testify as the sole witness.

California's attorney general is also investigating Huang's remarks, and politicians from the Senate Finance, Intelligence, and Foreign Relations committees have inquired about the scandal. In the House, Energy and Commerce has requested a company briefing from Nvidia, in addition to the three committees mentioned earlier. That's not counting an investigation from the Department of Justice and a reported investigation from the Securities and Exchange Commission.

Plus, Huang still has to cooperate with an investigation from the board of directors, led by the law firm Wachtell Lipton.

Meanwhile, Nvidia needs Huang to reassure investors and the broader AI ecosystem that the company is developing new products that will be in high demand. Investors had said that while Nvidia's products for the next two years have been fully designed, they were worried that the chipmaker's pace of innovation would slow without Huang.

Nvidia is facing new competition from chips from its biggest customers, including OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Amazon and Microsoft. The companies have been developing their own chips to reduce reliance on Nvidia, as well as to control AI costs.

"There was already this worry that we're spending too much on it," said Stephanie Balaouras, vice president of group research at Forrester. "One of those cost management strategies is to match ... the workload requirements to the right infrastructure. You don't need to put everything on a Nvidia GPU."

Many in the AI industry see Huang as one of the staunchest defenders of "open-source" AI that is free to use for commercial purposes, as compared with "frontier models" from companies like Anthropic and OpenAI, which charge users by the token.

Nvidia has also been spending heavily to develop its own open-source models called Nemotron. Currently, Nvidia Vice President Kari Briski is scheduled to present new Nemotron technology at GTC.

On Monday, Nvidia's board said that "senior levels" of the U.S. government provided input on Huang's potential national security risk. President Donald Trump told CNBC that he was central to convincing Nvidia's board to reinstate Huang and said he gave the board assurances.

Trump's help could draw even more scrutiny from Democratic lawmakers, who were already focused on probing Nvidia's chip sales.

"Today's news that Donald Trump engineered the rehiring of Jensen Huang at Nvidia is Exhibit A about the political corruption and rot at the heart of the Trump administration's big tech and AI policy," said Democratic Sen. Chris Van Hollen of Maryland in a call with reporters Monday.

Republicans have also criticized Huang's return as the focus on the company's China sales intensifies. Still, the investigations could start to weigh on Nvidia's CEO, experts say.

"How could he possibly do the job?" Jeff Sonnenfeld, senior associate dean at the Yale School of Management, told CNBC, citing Senate investigations, House investigations and other state investigations, even before Democrats take over next year.

And the reaction in Washington to Huang's scandal could start to affect Nvidia's sales.

Last week, the Department of Commerce announced that it will prevent Chinese companies from getting around export controls by renting Nvidia's chips across borders. On Monday, Commerce also added Megaspeed to a trade blacklist.

And it's not just Nvidia that is relying on a strong performance from Huang. The company's investors, customers, partners and shareholders will also look to see if Nvidia still wants to be at the center of the AI ecosystem. Before Huang was removed, he pushed Nvidia to invest in AI startups, while selling them products, and helping finance their infrastructure.

Remarks from Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress last week while she was interim CEO suggested that Nvidia would be taking a "very strong look" at using its balance sheet to support the AI ecosystem, but it's unclear if Huang's second stint as CEO will continue with his prior showmanship or abide by a more measured approach.

#2763. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2762. Anthropic closes below $83 IPO price, but analysts are still largely positive

Shares of Anthropic closed down 6.5% on Tuesday, sending the stock below the artificial intelligence company's $83 IPO price.

The AI lab's stock has been sinking steadily since opening at $111.50 on the Nasdaq on Thursday, after Anthropic raised $81.3 billion in the largest offering of all time. Investors eagerly awaited Anthropic's debut in hopes that the company would revitalize the broader AI trade, which has been volatile in recent weeks over concerns about the feasibility of a multitrillion-dollar infrastructure buildout.

Chip stocks Nvidia, Advanced Micro Devices and Intel, as well as neoclouds CoreWeave, IREN and Nebius, all fell during intraday trading on Tuesday. The market is still reeling after Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO, a dramatic reversal from his ouster earlier this month. Nvidia shares surged more than 30% on Monday, but pared those gains Tuesday.

Analysts at Canaccord Genuity said Tuesday that they're focused on Anthropic's long-term potential. They said they believe that the company is building "one of the most important enterprise technology franchises of the AI era" and that the "opportunity is considerably larger than traditional software spending."

Canaccord Genuity initiated coverage of Anthropic with a buy rating and a $135 price target.

"The risks are unusually large, particularly around infrastructure commitments and the durability of competitive differentiation, but we believe the combination of exceptional growth, emerging earnings capacity, and an attractive entry valuation support a compelling investment opportunity," the analysts wrote in a Tuesday note.

Wedbush analysts initiated coverage of Anthropic with an outperform rating and a $120 price target on Monday. They said Anthropic went public in a "tumultuous market" as debate around the AI buildout continues, but that they expect the company to navigate the competitive environment and "capture a uniquely large addressable market."

"While there's going to continue to be many ups and downs, and winners and losers as this ecosystem gets built, in our view there is no real question that it does get built over time," the analysts wrote in a Monday note. "The genie is out of the bottle. And ANT is a strong play on that theme."

Analysts at Oppenheimer initiated coverage of Anthropic with an outperform rating on Tuesday, and they set a $150 price target for the stock.

They said their "bullish stance" on the company stems from their belief that Anthropic will capture growing enterprise AI spending, that its improving compute efficiency lowers cost per task and that its multi-chip strategy optimizes price performance. The analysts added that Anthropic's focus on the enterprise also relies less on consumer subsidies, and that rising customer adoption creates "a substantial runway" for the company.

"While competition and compute commitments remain risks, we believe Anthropic can translate its leadership into durable growth and operating leverage," the analysts wrote in a Tuesday note.

#2760. Consumer confidence shoots up after Democrats' midterm victories — though inflation worries persist

Consumers grew significantly more optimistic in November in the wake of the midterm elections, with a growing number of Americans now seeing improvement in the labor market and expecting inflation to cool.

The Conference Board survey released Tuesday showed improvement across the board, with the headline confidence index jumping to 103.3, up 11.4 points from October and much better than the Dow Jones consensus forecast for 92.2.

Confidence among Democrats and independent voters improved sharply, the Conference Board said, while confidence among Republican respondents fell slightly. Still, confidence among Republicans remained the highest among the three groups.

While respondents "continued to express frustration with high prices and rising costs," they also "cited hope for the general economy," said Fergus McCormick, the board's senior economist for global indicators.

The survey comes with the Federal Reserve continuing to try to bring down inflation, which has run above the central bank's 2% target for some 5½ years. The Fed is expected to raise its benchmark interest rate by a quarter percentage point when it meets again in two weeks.

Despite the lingering worries about the cost of living — a centerpiece issue during the midterms — the survey's present situation and expectations indexes both posted strong gains. Inflation expectations for 12 months from now fell for the second straight month but remained high at 5.7%.

The survey's closely watched gap between jobs considered "plentiful" versus "hard to get" widened to 12.4 percentage points. The "plentiful" side rose to 30.8% from 26.2% while "hard to get" fell to 18.4%, down 3 percentage points.

The survey results are the second in the past week to reveal that Americans are growing more optimistic about the economy. The University of Michigan's November survey, released Friday, showed a 7.3% increase since October, with a similar partisan split.

#2759. Open Thread: Happy birthday to David Jones Garcia

Spurs small forward David Jones Garcia has one of the best Spurs "feel good stories," and that's saying a lot on a team that features Julian Champagnie's path from undrafted player to NBA Finals starter, Keldon Johnson's evolution to NBA Sixth Man, and Victor Wembanyama's emergence as the face of the NBA.

David Jones Garcia was born in Santo Domingo in the Dominican Republic. After seeing scouts at FIBA tournaments, his family sent David to high school in the United States to offer him the best exposure for future basketball opportunities. He attended DePaul where he became a starter after his first full season. He transferred to St. John's where he continued to excel, but the year was emotionally fragmented with the loss of his father. David vowed to his father on his deathbed he would play at least one regular-season NBA game.

Jones Garcia returned to college, but this time in Memphis under head coach Penny Hardaway. He declared for the 2024 NBA Draft but was undrafted. He spent the 2024-25 season bouncing around the G-League, signing contracts with the Philadelphia 76ers, Mexico City Capitanes, and Utah Jazz affiliate. He eventually made the 2025 G-League All-Rookie Team.

That summer he signed on with the San Antonio Spurs, initially for Summer League finally landing a two-way contract.

On October 22, in the first game of the season, Jones Garcia fulfilled his promise to his father and clocked 4 minutes against the Dallas Mavericks. He spent first half of the season coming off the bench. With a penchant for hitting threes and no traces of fear in his court demeanor, Jones Garcia revealed a confident and competent competitor.

He played in eleven total games before requiring surgery on his right ankle. On February 4, he was ruled out for the remainder of the season.

Jones Garcia signed on with the Spurs again for a second year with a two-way contract. He's seen minor action in four games thus far and picks up fourth quarter minutes each time the Spurs pull away early.

Today David Jones Garcia turns 25, the median age of the squad. His star still on the rise, he's continued to find opportunity and make the most of any situation he faces.

#2758. Gold is doing something strange. Here's one way to play it

Gold has stopped trading like a haven and started trading more like a market index this year. The past week has underscored it. Fears around AI, a hawkish Fed, and a stronger dollar sent many investors rushing to raise cash, and gold was sold along with stocks. GLD (SPDR Gold Shares) and the S&P 500 both slid around 9%. Gold then shared in the market's Monday rebound, with GLD up about 3% as the S&P 500 jumped nearly 5%.

Over long timeframes, gold's returns tend to move independently of the S&P 500's. From 2004 to 2025, the correlation between their daily returns has been just 0.05 (+1 means the two move in lockstep and -1 means they move in opposite directions). That relationship fluctuates, and 2026 has seen an unusually strong link. The correlation has been about 0.41 so far in 2026. Its rolling three-month reading peaked near 0.80 in June and now sits at 0.54, still higher than about 95% of rolling three-month readings since 2005. Since the end of August, GLD has fallen on 77% of the S&P 500's down days, compared with roughly half historically.

The options market is also behaving unusually. Gold options typically price calls richer than puts (call skew). Like other commodities, gold is prone to sharp rallies, and investors use calls to bet on them or hedge against macroeconomic risks. However, gold options have mostly shown the reverse this year, with puts currently trading richer than calls (put skew). It's a sign investors are paying a premium for protection against further declines, which is unusual for gold and more typical of stock options.

Those richer puts come as uncertainty around gold runs high heading into December. The PCE inflation report arrives Wednesday, followed by the jobs report next week and the Fed's decision and CPI the week after. GLD's implied volatility, a measure of how much the options market expects the fund to move, is now higher than on about 80% of days over the past year. Options are pricing in an expected move of about 6%, or roughly $20, by Dec. 18. With gold options pricier than usual and puts unusually rich, that presents an interesting setup for traders willing to sell premium.

Based on Monday's close, I'm selling the Dec. 18 310/300 put spread and 360/370 call spread in GLD for a credit of roughly $2.24.

This is a directionally neutral trade. Gold has been swinging with the market and is down about 33% from its January high. With the Fed and inflation data ahead, it's hard to say whether gold has bottomed or has further to fall into mid-December. A neutral position can collect the elevated premium without picking a direction, and the richer put side adds more.

The short strikes sit just outside the range the options market is pricing for Dec. 18, roughly 7% to 8% from Monday's close. That leaves room for a continued selloff or an extended rebound. The trade reaches its maximum profit of $224 if GLD closes between $310 and $360 on Dec. 18. It carries a 62% theoretical probability of profit and can show a gain before expiration if GLD remains relatively rangebound.

The maximum loss is capped by the long options and reaches $776 if GLD closes below $300 or above $370, 10% to 11% from Monday's close. Defining risk is especially important with the upcoming catalysts and recent price volatility in gold. GLD has had 30 daily moves of more than 3% this year, including a 10.3% drop on Jan. 30. The trade breaks even at $307.76 on the downside and $362.24 on the upside.

A close between $360 and $370 can assign the short call and leave a short position in the fund, so investors looking to avoid that should consider managing the trade before expiration. If GLD finishes between $300 and $310, the short put can be assigned, creating an obligation to buy 100 shares per spread at $310. After the credit, that's a cost basis of about $307.76, roughly 8% below Monday's close.

#2757. Saudi national charged in OpenAI bugging to be released on $1 million bond

Abdulrahman Alfozan, the Saudi national accused of bugging an OpenAI executive conference room, will be allowed to be released from jail on a $1 million bond, a San Francisco federal court judge ruled Tuesday.

Alfozan, 35, is expected to be released on Wednesday and be fitted with an ankle monitor, according to his attorney, David Rizk.

"We are simply grateful that the Court made the right decision to release Mr. Alfozan and now we look forward to defending the case," Rizk said in a text message after a hearing before U.S. District Court Magistrate Judge Alex Tse.

Tse's ruled in favor of a release motion by Rizk filed last week that said prosecutors had admitted they had "found no new evidence to support the theory that Mr. Alfozan was stealing trade secrets, or to support the speculation that he was acting as an unregistered foreign agent for Saudi Arabia."

The San Francisco U.S. Attorney's Office opposed the former OpenAI engineer's release, saying in its response that he remains a flight risk due to his "considerable resources and ties to Saudi Arabia."

Alfozan's release request "blithely dismisses the seriousness" of the charge he faces, and "appears to presume, without support, that the government's investigation is complete," prosecutors wrote.

Alfozan, who was arrested Oct. 9, is accused of concealing a recording device in the artificial intelligence company's conference room to secretly record discussions. The device was found in a USB port in a television in the room on Sept. 30, prosecutors have said.

Prosecutors said they have found hundreds of audio files on Alfozan's personal computer, "includingdozens of recordings of meetings or conversations that occurred at OpenAI throughout September 2026, many of which involve discussions of what may be trade secrets."

Jury selection for Alfozan's trial is scheduled to begin Jan. 20.

Alfozan is a venture partner in a Saudi Arabian venture capital firm that lists Prince Khalid Bin Bader Al Saud, a member of that nation's ruling royal family, as a fellow partner.

OpenAI did not immediately respond to a request for comment on Alfozan's planned release.

— CNBC's Ashley Capoot contributed to this article.

#2755. Ravens cut WR LaJohntay Wester after 2 muffed punts in Carolina

The Baltimore Ravens are waiving second-year wide receiver/returner LaJohntay Wester after two muffed punts in Sunday's loss to the Carolina Panthers, per Jeff Zrebiec of The Athletic.

Wester, 24, let his first punt return go into the end zone for a touchback on Sunday, but could not handle the second and third balls from Sam Martin, both in the second quarter. On the first, he signaled for a fair catch but never settled under the ball, instead attempting a diving grab and letting the ball fall through his arms. The Panthers took over at the Ravens' 23-yard line and scored a go-ahead touchdown two plays later.

The second was significantly worse. Wester had ample time and space to make a clean catch, but the ball ricocheted off his helmet and rolled toward Baltimore's end zone. Ravens cornerback Keyon Martin managed to corral it at the 1, forcing the offense to battle out of their own end zone with a minute left in the half instead of pushing for another score.

Wester did not play another snap for the rest of the game and could be seen on the sidelines being consoled by team chaplain Rev. Johnny Shelton. Carolina only punted one more time with Ravens safety Malaki Starks back to return. He let the ball roll to the 1, where it was downed by a Panther whose heel was on the goal line, resulting in a touchback.

#2754. Rebounding may be Dyson Daniels’ best skill after his defense

Dyson Daniels' defense has become his calling card, but his rebounding is making a strong case as his second-best skill.

Sunday's 107–97 home victory over the Detroit Pistons offered another example. The Hawks earned their fourth consecutive win with contributions throughout the roster, but Daniels helped shape the game despite scoring just five points.

He finished with 14 rebounds, eight assists, five steals and two blocks. His rebounds were split evenly between the offensive and defensive glass, giving Atlanta seven additional opportunities while helping end Detroit possessions at the other end.

That balance explains why his rebounding deserves its own recognition. Daniels does more than collect available defensive boards. He actively extends possessions, giving teammates another chance to score when the initial shot does not fall.

For an offense working through inconsistent stretches, those extra opportunities matter. An offensive rebound can turn an empty possession into a putback, a kickout three or another chance to attack an unsettled defense. Daniels can contribute to that process without needing a play called for him.

Head coach Quin Snyder recognizes how unusual that production is from a guard.

"It's been pretty unusual what [Dyson] has been able to do to rebound the ball," Snyder said.

Daniels' anticipation translates naturally from defending to pursuing rebounds. Reading a possession, recognizing where the ball could go and reacting before an opponent are valuable in both situations. His willingness to compete in traffic adds another element to that awareness.

His defensive rebounds also connect directly to Atlanta's preferred pace. When Daniels secures the ball himself, he can initiate the next possession without waiting for an outlet pass. That gives the Hawks an opportunity to advance quickly and find advantages before the opposing defense gets organized.

His eight assists against Detroit illustrated the broader value of having another player capable of gathering the ball and creating for teammates. Rebounding and playmaking can work together, helping Atlanta move from a defensive stop into its offense.

There is a reasonable argument for placing his passing alongside his rebounding when evaluating his strongest skills. However, the Detroit performance showed how controlling possessions can influence a game even when his scoring remains limited.

Daniels' shooting still matters to Atlanta's long-term offensive ceiling. His rebounding does not erase that area for improvement, but it gives him another dependable way to help when shots are not falling.

The Hawks already rely on Daniels to disrupt opponents. His work on the glass helps ensure those defensive efforts produce completed stops while creating second chances offensively.

That is what makes his rebounding so valuable. It strengthens both ends of the floor and reinforces why his impact reaches well beyond his point total.

#2753. Drugmaker Insmed shares rally 14% after Sanofi agrees to buy it in pharma’s biggest deal in three years

Insmed shares rallied 14% on Tuesday after Sanofi announced it would buy the smaller drugmaker for $41 billion, marking the largest pharma deal since 2023.

The proposed acquisition includes Insmed's portfolio of two commercialized respiratory drugs as well as its pipeline of experimental candidates. Sanofi will pay $175 per share in cash.

It's a big bet by new Sanofi CEO Belen Garijo, who took up the role in May to steer the company through its upcoming patent cliff, when its blockbuster immunology drug Dupixent will face generic competition as patents expire.

If closed, the transaction will be the biggest one the industry has seen since Pfizer bought Seagen for $43 billion in December 2023.

Insmed shares closed up 14.1% at $170.15 on Tuesday, while Sanofi stock closed up 10.9% in Paris.

Barclays analysts called the deal a "breath of fresh air," significantly improving Sanofi's Dupixent patent expiry outlook by adding Insmed's established respiratory assets Brinsupri and Arikayce, plus a late-stage pipeline offering further potential upside.

The deal is expected to close in the first half of 2027 and be accretive to earnings by the end of 2028, Sanofi said.

Like much of the industry, Sanofi faces a revenue drop toward the end of the decade as top-selling drugs lose exclusivity and open up for generic drugmakers to compete.

Global biopharma has experienced a sharp uptick in deals in 2026, as the sector is driven by looming patent cliffs, newly buoyant public markets and a race to beef up pipelines. Following a post-pandemic trough in 2022, M&A activity surged in 2025 and has accelerated into 2026.

While companies develop new medicines in-house, acquiring them is often faster and has become the go-to strategy for large biopharma companies with strong cash flows and financial firepower. Investors are also calling for sustainable revenue growth as major drugs lose exclusivity.

"Sanofi could offer a far more attractive investment opportunity given the significantly improved growth outlook," Barclays said in a note early Tuesday.

Formerly CEO at Merck KGaA, Garijo is facing pressure to find Sanofi's next growth driver. Her predecessor Paul Hudson stepped down earlier this year after the board chose not to renew his mandate, following a six-year reign during which his costly emphasis on R&D failed to deliver speedy results.

The Insmed deal is a good strategic fit that will help soften the blow from Dupixent's expected 2031–32 loss of exclusivity, said Jefferies analysts.

Insmed's medicines Brinsupri and Arikayce could add significant long-term revenue, with forecasts of $6.8 billion and $1.3 billion, respectively, in 2035, the analysts said. They see further potential upside if a settlement delays Dupixent's patent cliff, or if Brinsupri is included in Sanofi's alliance with Regeneron and positioned alongside Dupixent in respiratory diseases.

"The deal does not fix Sanofi's R&D/pipeline questions, but it buys time," the analysts said, adding that one potential wrinkle is future competition from AstraZeneca's tozorakimab, which is being studied for treatment in bronchiectasis, a chronic lung condition.

"Insmed builds on our already successful presence in respiratory diseases through its highly differentiated portfolio, strong science and proven commercial success," CEO Garijo said in a statement.

Sanofi shares fell 2.9%, and Insmed rose 24% on Nov. 19 after Bloomberg reported that a deal was underway.

#2751. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2750. Dell earnings beat on AI strength, company authorizes $25 billion buyback

Dell reported third-quarter earnings on Tuesday that topped Wall Street expectations for earnings and sales, and provided a strong guide for the fourth quarter.

Here's how the chipmaker did versus LSEG consensus estimates:

Dell said that it expected earnings of $8.40 per share on revenue of about $56.3 billion in the current quarter. Analysts polled by LSEG were looking for $7.50 per share on $53.61 billion in revenue.

The company's overall sales more than doubled on an annual basis during the third quarter, driven by AI server demand.

The company's largest line is its infrastructure solutions group, or ISG, which makes AI servers and systems, often based around AI chips from companies like Nvidia and AMD.

ISG sales during the quarter were $41.97 billion, topping an LSEG estimate of $35.27 billion. Of that, $22 billion was for AI-optimized servers, up 291% on an annual basis. ISG now accounts for 85% of Dell's total revenue.

"We are now a different company, with Infrastructure Solutions Group driving the vast majority of our profit and growth," said Jeff Clarke, Dell's vice chairman and COO, in a statement. He said that Dell has 7,500 customers buying AI servers.

In prepared remarks, Clarke said that Dell has a competitive advantage over other computer equipment makers because it allows customers to get their computers working within hours of delivery.

"That allows customers to convert backlog into revenue sooner and more reliably," Clarke said in the prepared remarks published by Dell.

The company also raised its forecast for AI-servers in the coming fiscal year to $78 billion, versus $74 billion expected.

The company also sells laptops and PCs, in a group it calls CSG, or client services group. It reported $12.89 billion in sales, which was below LSEG expectations of $14.33 billion.

The overall PC market has been getting whacked of late, with some estimates calling the lull the worst ever, as memory prices have led to higher device prices and consumers are choosing to hang on to their old laptops longer.

Dell said that it had authorized $25 billion in share repurchases, and that it had spent $4.9 billion in the third quarter on share repurchases and dividends.

Dell shares are up over 135% so far in 2026.

#2749. The little things are adding up to something bigger for the Suns

Games in November count just as much toward the standings as games in March, and what we saw from the Phoenix Suns in their overtime win over the New Orleans Pelicans is something we might look back on in March and appreciate, both for how it unfolded and the result it produced.

Going into any opposing team's arena isn't easy, and we know the Pelicans have the positional archetypes that can trouble Phoenix. They have size and length defensively, with the ability to jam up Devin Booker and effectively close out on shooters.

So what if they entered the game 5-13? All it takes is one of their players getting on a roll, feeding off the Smoothie King Center crowd, and suddenly the possibility of losing to them becomes very real.

Monday night's game was a physical one, defined by both teams exchanging runs. Ultimately, the team that made the final run was going to walk away victorious, and to the Suns' credit, it took a collective effort to ensure that was them. Five players scored in double figures, four of whom came from the starting five. Everybody contributed, and when you take care of the little things, the big things tend to take care of themselves.

That big thing was a fourth consecutive victory on this road trip for Phoenix, further reinforcing that the Suns are figuring out how to play team basketball this season. 16 games in, there's still plenty of room for growth. Playing perfect basketball is impossible, but that doesn't mean you shouldn't chase perfection. The one thing potentially keeping this team from going from a good basketball team, which is what they currently are, to a great one is their ability to rebound. From a connectivity and cohesion standpoint, you're beginning to see a consistent level of play from this group. It's another solid win.

What I appreciate most about this stretch of basketball is that the Suns are finding different ways to win. They're learning how to navigate adversity, respond to opposing runs, and trust one another when possessions become increasingly valuable. The rebounding deficiencies remain a legitimate concern, but the collective identity of this team is beginning to take shape. And banking these victories while they're still figuring things out? That's something we might be especially thankful for come March.

#2748. Trump wanted Jensen Huang back. Now, the president is all alone in Washington

President Donald Trump wanted Jensen Huang back at the helm of Nvidia. Now that the chip mogul is reinstated, Trump is cheering alone in Washington.

Trump celebrated and claimed credit after Huang was reinstated by the Nvidia board on Monday, a stunning return to power for the embattled CEO. In a nearly hourlong phone call with CNBC, the president went as far as saying he "made the deal" to bring Huang back.

But Trump's jubilation stands in stark contrast to the reaction from even his staunchest allies on Capitol Hill, who recoiled after the reinstatement and vowed to continue their investigations into Huang — whose relationship with a potential Chinese intelligence asset they fear may have jeopardized U.S. national security.

"The risk remains that there could be information that a Chinese spy knows about a key individual in the supply chain, and that's not worth the risk," Sen. Kevin Cramer, R-N.D., said Tuesday on CNBC's "Squawk Box." "We might even have to put a babysitter on Jensen Huang for crying out loud ... just to make sure that he doesn't do something that's going to risk the United States."

Trump's rare isolation from his party over Huang comes after the GOP was throttled in the midterm elections, as Democrats rode a blue tsunami to capture the Senate and the largest House majority this century. And it suggests Republicans, who have spent a decade in lockstep with the president, may have found an issue on which they can't bring themselves to take Trump's side.

"I can accept what the president has done," Cramer said. "But we have to put guardrails around to protect the United States of America."

Many midterm voters identified Silicon Valley's most visible outputs, artificial intelligence and data centers, as top concerns motivating them to cast ballots for Democrats. The CNBC All-America Economic Survey in October found 63% of registered voters oppose an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% support one.

By timing his extraordinary intervention in Nvidia so soon after the GOP's historic losses, Trump could spark a national debate about corporate governance and responsibility, former Under Secretary of State Richard Stengel said.

"With the repudiation of Trumpism in the midterms and voter revulsion with corruption, I think there will be a turn back to a more value-based duty of care for boards of directors and away from the current maximization of shareholder value," Stengel told CNBC in an email.

"Directors are citizens first, shareholders second. The idea that you put profit above national security should disgust shareholders, not please them," Stengel, who worked in the Obama administration, wrote.

Bipartisan lawmakers are now racing to pass a series of export control bills before the year ends, a call that has only grown louder since Huang was reinstated.

There's "not just frustration or political worry, but actual, substantive national security concern here from Democrats and Republicans," Chris McGuire, senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council on Foreign Relations, told CNBC.

"This has historically been a bipartisan issue," McGuire said. "Democrats and Republicans generally pretty much agree that we shouldn't be sending our most advanced chips to China."

"If it happened in massive quantities, people want to know why, how, and they want to make sure that it doesn't happen again," he said. "And putting your thumb on the scale in this clear of a way seems like it could undercut those efforts."

The White House didn't immediately respond to a request for comment on the political dynamics of Trump's involvement in reinstating Huang.

Republicans will have the first crack at investigating Huang next week before ceding power to Democrats in January. Democrats have vowed their own investigations into the Nvidia CEO and his ties to the president and his Cabinet. The Democrats who will lead those investigations are now hopeful that Republicans will join them.

"I've been seeing a lot of Republicans outraged and scandalized by what has just happened," House Judiciary Committee ranking member Jamie Raskin, D-Md., said Tuesday on "Squawk Box," when asked if Republicans may support a bipartisan investigation of Nvidia.

"When else in American history can you think of a time when a president of the United States called up a corporate board of directors meeting and then essentially dictated to them who was going to be their new CEO right after they had fired the guy?"

Trump throughout his second term has stated repeatedly that achieving AI dominance is required for the U.S. to maintain global superiority. His administration has championed the rapid growth of the country's tech giants and forged close ties with its leaders, especially Huang, while removing constraints on the industry and opposing some new regulatory efforts.

At the same time, Trump has defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick against accusations of slow-walking export controls on China, the fiercest competitor in the AI race. And while Trump recently discussed AI during an in-person meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping, he appeared to deliver few demands and achieved few assurances about Beijing's efforts to acquire U.S. chips.

"To say we can't do AI safety because of China, and then also be actively loosening the controls on China, makes no sense," McGuire said.

Some of Trump's fiercest allies have raised similar qualms.

"Reinstating Jensen Huang is going to blow back on our national security," MAGA activist Laura Loomer wrote in an X post Monday. "Eventually there has to be accountability for jeopardizing US national security. China didn't just get access to Nvidia chips by accident."

Newt Gingrich, the influential former Republican speaker of the House, in an X post after Huang's reinstatement, wrote, "Congress should really dig into this as a national security issue of enormous proportions and also a significant failure in the federal bureaucracy."

Their criticisms sharply contrast with the praise coming from Silicon Valley and some of the Trump-friendly voices within it, such as Elon Musk, who wrote Monday, "The mistake has been corrected."

But that's a shrinking political constituency, McGuire said.

"The tech-right perspective is very out of touch with where most of the Republican Party is on these issues, to say nothing of the Democratic Party," he said.

"Those positions are just not going to be sustainable for much longer," he added. "I think they're already becoming unsustainable."

#2747. Colts sign QB Shane Buechele off Bills practice squad

The Colts have added a third quarterback to their active roster.

The team announced on Tuesday that they have signed Shane Buechele off of the Bills' practice squad. He will slot in as the third quarterback in Indianapolis behind Anthony Richardson and Riley Leonard.

Buechele has been with the Bills for most of the last four seasons, but he did have a brief stint on the Chiefs' roster after Patrick Mahomes was injured last year. He made his regular season debut for Kansas City during that stint and went 7-of-14 for 88 yards in Week 18.

The Colts also announced that they have signed veteran defensive end Carl Lawson off of their practice squad. They placed defensive end Caden Curry on injured reserve and waived offensive tackle Charlie Heck in corresponding moves.

#2746. Jensen Huang tells staff that Nvidia doesn't need a succession plan

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang on Tuesday struck a conciliatory and remorseful tone while leading his first employee meeting since being reinstated, and said how sorry he was for what he had done.

Huang told employees that he let them down and promised to do a better job the second time around, according to people inside the room, who asked not to be named in order to share details. "I hurt all of you," Huang said, according to one of the people.

The CEO also addressed succession planning during the event, the people said. He told staff that Nvidia has built a team that doesn't require succession planning the way a normal organization does, because the company operates a unique business model where there are multiple capable leaders at Nvidia that are leading different parts of the organization.

A succession plan would create unnecessary hierarchy, Huang said, according to one of the people present.

Investors and analysts were left to speculate about the company's future leadership in the wake of Huang's resignation on Nov. 12.

On Monday, Huang was reinstated as CEO of the chipmaker by Nvidia's board of directors. Eleven days before, Huang had resigned from Nvidia after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who is alleged to be a spy for Beijing.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said last week that Huang resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

Huang started the meeting by thanking CFO Colette Kress for her leadership as interim CEO.

Huang told staff that Tuesday's company meeting was the second hardest meeting of his life, the first one being with his wife. He said he is in therapy, according to one of the people present.

In a press release announcing Huang's return on Monday, the company said the board "received input" from senior officials in the federal government about any potential national security risk around the CEO. President Donald Trump told CNBC that he gave the board assurances and "made the deal" to restore Huang to his post.

After the company had its worst week in the stock market since 2008, the stock surged more than 30% the day his return was announced. Shares fell on Tuesday, however.

An Nvidia representative didn't immediately respond to requests for comment.

#2745. Why Draymond Green's NBA ‘loser' comments rubbed Baron Davis the wrong way

Why Draymond Green's NBA 'loser' comments rubbed Baron Davis the wrong way originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Another off-court Draymond Green statement is buzzing around the NBA, and a Warriors icon chipped in to give his perspective on the matter.

Baron Davis, the centerpiece of the 2006-07 Warriors "We Believe" team, was asked about Green calling Phoenix Suns commentator Eddie Johnson, among other critics, "losers" for debating Green's Naismith Memorial Basketball Hall of Fame case.

Speaking on the "Gil's Arena" podcast, Davis explained to hosts Josiah Johnson and Gilbert Arenas why those comments bothered him.

"I just don't like the loser thing, man," Davis said. "We've all played. It takes greatness to get into this league. It takes some hard work. Not everybody's gonna win. There's only one champion, so I just don't like the loser thing, because if Michael Jordan called us a loser, I can deal with that. If Kobe [Bryant] called me a loser, I can deal with that. But I think that we move the goalposts."

Davis went on to say that "everybody has their way of judging" players, emphasizing accolades or individual statistics, and gave props to both Green and Johnson, the 1989 Sixth Man of the Year.

While Davis differentiated between winners and NBA champions, he stated the conversation between the two "goes both ways."

"You learn to appreciate everybody because everybody is winners," Davis said. "They are champions, and so if you don't win a championship, that don't mean you can't talk to champions in a certain fashion. I don't agree with that."

The discussion began with a post by Johnson on Nov. 21 questioning whether Green's statistics were impressive enough to make the Naismith Memorial Basketball Hall of Fame.

Green responded to Johnson's comments before Golden State's 88-87 win on Sunday and was asked about the comments after the game as well.

There is really no player who embodies the debate more than Green, whose Basketball Hall of Fame case is built on intangibles and generational defense despite a lack of elite offensive production.

Green, a nine-time All-Defensive team selection and the 2017 Defensive Player of the Year, never was the best player on the Warriors but played a vital role alongside superstar Steph Curry.

Despite declining at 36 years old, Green was the best playoff defender of his generation and an All-NBA Second Team selection at his peak.

The outspoken veteran's Olympic and college basketball resume at Michigan State strengthens his case for the Basketball Hall of Fame, being named the NABC National Player of the Year in 2012 and holding two Olympic gold medals.

The two Warriors legends might be cemented in franchise history for their impact. but this certainly appears to be a topic they don't agree on.

#2741. Walker Kessler thankful for big stage with Lakers after beating former team

Walker Kessler has a lot to be thankful for as the Los Angeles Lakers earned a 109-94 victory over the Utah Jazz at Crypto.com Arena on Monday, Nov. 23.

The Lakers clinched the season series against the center's former team, giving Los Angeles some momentum ahead of Thanksgiving and a showdown against the San Antonio Spurs on Friday, Nov. 27.

While the Lakers may only go as far as Luka Doncic and Austin Reaves can take them, Kessler is viewed as the third piece to a potential championship puzzle.

Los Angeles invested a lot to acquire Kessler before signing him four-year, $130 million deal during the offseason. In order to secure the big man, the Lakers sent unprotected first-round picks in 2031 and 2033 and first-round swaps in 2028 and 2030 to the Jazz.

All eyes will be on Kessler as he will be tasked with defending Victor Wembanyama of the Spurs on Friday.

The center finished with 10 points and 12 rebounds against Utah for his 10th double-double of the season on Monday.

Kessler had impressed Los Angeles in the season opener with a 26-point performance, having made all 12 of his shots against the Golden State Warriors. He had another high-efficiency night against the Atlanta Hawks, posting 21 points on 8-of-12 shooting and making five of his six free throw attempts.

"I'm thankful for this opportunity to play on such a big stage," Kessler said during his postgame press conference on Monday. "Sometimes you get so tied down in your individual performance and stats and all this stuff, but to be a professional basketball player like as a kid, you look at that, it's like, oh my gosh. That's the dream."

Kessler will be on the big stage later this week with a formidable opponent in Wembanyama.

Wembanyama and the Spurs are the standard in the conference, having won the 2025-26 conference finals and making an appearance in the NBA Finals.

Wembanyama is one of the league's top young stars while Kessler is looking to have a breakout season on a winning team.

Kessler did not play a lot of meaningful basketball during his first four seasons in the NBA, as the Jazz never finished with a record above .500.

Kessler and Wembanyama have an even 3-3 record when their respective teams have played against one another. Considering the differences in the roles those players may have had on those respective teams over the years, it's not surprising that Wembanyama has averaged more points, rebounds, blocks and minutes per game.

Kessler will have to find a balance during the game, having admitted that his emotions have factored into his play at least once this season.

Kessler had a strong start to the season, but an early matchup in Utah led to a two-point performance in a 132-123 victory for the Lakers on Oct. 25.

"I think going into the game I let my emotions get the best of me," Kessler said at the time. "But you have to learn in those moments and move on. I'm just glad we got the win tonight … even though it wasn't my best night, I really appreciate (my teammates)."

Kessler has averaged 14.3 points and 10.7 rebounds per game in 16 games played this season.

#2740. Countdown to Cristiano Ronaldo's 1000th goal: Updated stats for CR7 chasing soccer's all-time career milestone

Countdown to Cristiano Ronaldo's 1000th goal: Updated stats for CR7 chasing soccer's all-time career milestone originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The greatest goal-scorer in professional football is inching closer to a potential milestone that would be widely celebrated as one of the most notable accomplishments in the history of the game.

Cristiano Ronaldo, already owning the record for most goals for club and country in the sport's long and storied history, is getting closer to scoring for the 1,000th time in his senior career.

Having turned 41 years old in February 2026, Ronaldo faces a race against time — and the decline of his own physical capabilities — to reach this milestone. Should he successfully accomplish it, however, it would be an epic triumph of fitness, talent, longevity, and dominance that would ring throughout football's past and present, as well as long into the future.

The Sporting News breaks down where Ronaldo stands presently, how far he has still to go to reach such a level, and when he could theoretically eclipse one of the football milestones previously considered untouchable.

MORE: The leading goal-scorers in football history | Why Ronaldo considers himself the G.O.A.T.

As of November 9, 2026, Ronaldo has scored 987 senior career goals for club and country. He requires a further 13 to reach 1,000 across all of his illustrious football career.

Take a look at the complete breakdown of his goals here.

If Cristiano Ronaldo is able to reach the millennium mark, he would be the first and only in professional football to do so. Even after retiring from international duty, it seems highly likely now that he will reach this milestone, with only a few more required.

At the rate of around one goal per game, understanding he will probably skip AFC Champions League road matches during the league phase, Ronaldo should reach 1,000 career goals sometime in early 2027, potentially February or March.

At his career scoring rate — he has bagged a goal every 1.365 games, on average — it would take Ronaldo around 21 more games to reach 1,000 goals.

Ronaldo is considered by nearly all record-keepers to be the all-time goal-scoring leader in global professional football history, at both club and international level.

The record itself is open to interpretation, with some arguing it is impossible to know for certain due to the lack of accurate recording-keeping in football in the late 19th and early 20th centuries.

Ronaldo and long-time rival Lionel Messi have both overtaken previous record-holder Josef Bican, who scored an incredible 805 goals in the 24 years between 1931 and 1955. However, there is still plenty of dispute over Bican's actual numbers. The late Brazilian icon Pele also claimed to have scored more than 1,000 career goals — though that total includes friendlies.

Another debate centers around Ronaldo's own tally. Some record-keeping aggregators, such as Transfermarkt.com, do not include the six goals in six games that he scored in the 2023 Arab Club Champions Cup as official results.

The Sporting News has reached out to FIFA for clarification on this discrepancy. For now, we have included these in his overall total, as you can see reflected in the breakdown below.

*Goals only in official matches, not including club friendlies or exhibitions.

* Some record-keeping organizations do not consider the Arab Club Champions Cup to be an official competition, but instead an exhibition, and therefore do not include the results of these games as part of official records.

#2739. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2738. Karl-Anthony Towns is so much more than the Knicks’ best player

In New York, it is customary for an athlete to have distractions. How do you hide in a city that adores you? How do you hide in a city full of people counting on your performance to get them from the capitalistic ills of living in New York? So many of our great athletes — everyone from Ruth, Clyde Frazier, Reggie Jackson, Sugar Ray Richardson, Derek Jeter, Alex Rodriguez, Carmelo Anthony — have had off-the-court news show up in Page Six, on TMZ, on the internet, those times when the fame and notoriety in New York becomes too showy, causing discomfort for even the most New York of New York fans. I remember when Stephon Marbury was in the paper constantly, diminishing the goodwill that came from being raised in Coney Island, blending a conflicting cocktail of bitterness towards the press with a heroic sense of duty to working class New Yorkers. Sometimes it was because of his arguments with Isiah Thomas, on the team plane, cursing him out for his lack of loyalty; sometimes it was his inappropriate liaisons with interns. If you are a New York athlete and you don't end up in the newspaper or the gossip columns for merely existing in a way that would not anoint you into a private club of sainthood, then I rebuke your non-existent personality.

The glory of this New York Knicks team, the absolute greatness that exists within the halls of Tarrytown and Madison Square Garden, is that for the first time in my lifetime, we're cheering for a Knicks team that isn't dramatic. It's led by Jalen Brunson, of course, who is a businessman with a sharp crossover, sophisticated at blocking out chatter from elsewhere until he is a champion, in front of a microphone at City Hall about to finally speak about his haters. That deadly professionalism is what the team has become since the fateful day we first signed him from Dallas in free agency. Yet, and this is something that I don't think I realized until the start of the season, the player that I am most proud of for embodying these virtues is Karl-Anthony Towns. Towns, 31, has been the team's best player, by far, and considering that I saw Brunson put the team on his back in the game-clinching game, I don't say that fact lightly. KAT, however, has been the best big man in the world so far. Right now, he is averaging 21.3 points per game and 14 rebounds. Those are terrific numbers, but it is the fact that he leads the Knicks in plus minus (+/-) that is the most surprising part of the season so far. Plus, he's been silencing any critics.

Before the season started, the Knicks and Towns were in talks for a contract extension. The two sides were apart on the numbers and because the Knicks have a rule where they don't negotiate during the beginning of the season, the season started before Towns was able to re-sign for more money. I'm not too concerned. It feels like it'd be a depressing longshot if the Knicks didn't re-up with Towns. In the wake of the championship that the Knicks won, Towns took a point forward role that was completely different from the role that he started the season playing. Controlling the ball at the top of the key, waiting for the offense to set, waiting for those sequences where players grunt and grind off of screens, Towns would be steady, attacking as soon as he saw someone open or he saw a lane to gallop into. Towns became an example of why the bullishness about the Knicks turned out to be a real, tangible thing. Their ace was Brunson but the force, the light saber, was Towns, emboldened to play a style of basketball it would have seemed like a no-brainer to have always allowed him to play.

The games from that run are firmly indelible. The game four against the Hawks, the first time this new offense was employed, when he had a triple double; the first game against the Spurs, where he took it to Wemby, supplying Brunson's thirty points with eighteen points. However, maybe a little less important, but still quite necessary, Towns's personality — unabashedly emotionally intelligent, gentle, and strong — won people over. One of the more meaningful parts of the title celebration was seeing Mayor Zohran Kwame Mamdani connect with Towns, two ethnic New York men, one in politics, the other in sports, connecting with whimsy and joy. Somehow, all of the disses, the fake macho act that his opponents tried to do to him, went out the window. He won, and his way of being won. He's not alone. He's all of us. Why did it ever become popular to pretend like greatness meant being an asshole? Why did it ever become common for folks to be angry while being great? Towns, as a player, is so unique for his emotional gifts, for his understanding of the game. It isn't like he is a soft player, he is rarely getting pushed off the block by anyone. The performance of masculinity, one that is popular for big men of the previous generation, do not apply to Towns.

Last year, I wrote about this. "Towns is an earnest star. His instincts lead him to show emotion on the court, emotion that allows for the most traditional kinds of men to question his mental toughness", I wrote. This piece has aged well, thank you to Towns's overall play and spirit during the Knicks playoffs. His championship has given him newfound respect that I wonder if he would have if we had failed to defeat the Spurs. What if part of the reason that Towns is as universally accepted as he has ever been is because he won the championship? Ring culture, despite the fact that I often dabble in that practice, can be deceiving. Luckily with Towns, it is earned. He won playing a role that Draymond Green hasn't played in years; he won before Jimmy Butler did.

This year has been more of the same. A lesser player and person than Towns would have sulked about the dissolution of the contract talks, but Towns hasn't let it get to his head. He was fantastic against the Pistons three weeks ago with a 20 and 20 double-double, against the Wolves on Thursday night, and against the Raptors, extending his streak of double-doubles to fourteen games. You haven't heard a whisper of discontent out of Towns. Nothing about the lack of respect from the contract, nothing about the fact that he has been the Knicks's best player so far this season, nothing about himself. No one would blame him if he did that. Instead, he has been excellent on the court, adding to his already great legacy with the Knicks, busting lesser big men off the dribble, finishing through hard contact. Malika Andrews said Towns has been the best player in the Eastern Conference and I think she is right. His new trick is his slim body: knowing that he is getting older, Towns lost weight, cutting down on his carbs in the offseason. His body is built to last, all the way through the postseason. Right now, the Knicks are still 9-1 to win the championship, the fourth best odds in the league. When April rolls around, I'd gamble on everyone else catching up to Towns's energy. Until then, we'll rely on him to carry us to the postseason, relying on his steadiness and smile to ground us during a grueling title defense.

Soon, in ten years, we'll be talking about Towns being one of NBA's history's most underrated players, Dirk Nowitzki with somehow less adoration, and a player who helped bring a title back to New York — victory mixed with tears of joy, glory mixed with urban swagger. For this season, a parade down Chambers Street and its surrounding FiDi blocks could happen again. Karl-Anthony Towns is a champion, and when there is will, there is a way.

#2737. NBA Cup 2026 Update: Standings, clinching scenarios, with critical Cup games on NBC and Peacock Tuesday

This is the deciding week for the NBA Cup group stage.

Games on Tuesday (on NBC and Peacock), Wednesday (on ESPN), and Friday (on Prime) will determine the six group-stage winners and two wild cards that will advance to the knockout rounds. The two Tuesday night games on NBC and Peacock — Boston at Washington at 8 p.m. ET, then Dallas at Denver at 10 p.m. — are both critical games in their groups.

Let's break down the group standings, with a reminder that the winner of each group advances, plus one wildcard from each conference, and after head-to-head, the next tiebreaker is point differential — it can be good to run up the score.

1. Dallas (2-0, +17 point differential) 2. Denver (2-0, +16) 3. Phoenix (1-1, +3) 4. Houston (1-2, -7) 5. Utah (0-3, -29)

Analysis: Denver hosts Dallas Tuesday night in a game that will essentially decide this group, and you can watch it at 10 p.m. ET on NBC and stream it on Peacock. If Denver wins, they are in as the Group A winners. If Dallas picks up the victory, it still needs Phoenix to lose to Houston Wednesday night, or Dallas can beat Phoenix on Friday, but either way Dallas very likely would advance.

1. Minnesota (2-0, +32) 2. Oklahoma City (2-1, +36) 3. Memphis (1-1, 0) 4. LA Clippers (1-1, -25) 5. New Orleans (0-3, -43)

Analysis: Minnesota travels to Oklahoma City on Wednesday night in the key game for this group. If Minnesota wins, it is in the driver's seat but still needs a Memphis loss in its last two games to clinch the group (and the Grizzlies are now without Cameron Boozer for a month). The Thunder lost to the Clippers in group play, so OKC still needs LA to lose another game to secure the division even if they beat Minnesota.

1. San Antonio (2-0, +53) 2. Los Angeles Lakers (2-1, -9) 3. Portland (1-1, +7) 4. Golden State (1-1, -21) 5. Sacramento (0-3, -30)

Analysis: Los Angeles at San Antonio Friday night is an epic early-season clash between the two top teams in Group C and the top two teams in this week's NBC Sports NBA Power Rankings. San Antonio also plays Portland on Wednesday in a game with key implications for the group.

1. Orlando (3-0, +17) 2. Toronto (2-1, +10) 3. Detroit (1-1, +13) 4. Brooklyn (0-2, -18) 5. Milwaukee (0-2, -22)

Analysis: Orlando hosts Detroit on Tuesday night and if the Magic win then they win the group. If the Pistons win on Tuesday, the door is thrown wide open, and things get complicated between Detroit, Toronto and Orlando (the Nets and Bucks are not going to advance).

1. Miami (2-0, +32) 2. Cleveland (2-1, +21) 3. Philadelphia (1-1, -19) 4. Indiana (1-2, -27) 5. New York (0-2, -7)

Analysis: We will not have a repeat NBA Cup champion as the Knicks are all but officially eliminated. For Miami, it's simple: Beat Philadelphia Wednesday and they win the group. If the 76ers win, they would take the group with a win over struggling Indiana on Friday.

1. Boston (2-0, +53) 2. Atlanta (2-1, +21) 3. Washington (2-1, +13) 4. Chicago (1-2, -18) 5. Charlotte (0-3, -69)

Analysis: If Boston can beat Washington Tuesday night in a game you can watch on NBC and Peacock (8 p.m. ET), they will be in the driver's seat heading into Friday's game against Atlanta. If the Wizards win we get chaos, because Washington lost to Atlanta, giving the Hawks the head-to-head tiebreaker.

One thing that has remained steady over the four years of the NBA Cup is the format. The only real change this season is that the NBA Cup semifinals will take place on a team's home court (the higher East and West seed remaining), and the NBA Cup Final will be played at the Hinkle Fieldhouse in Indianapolis, home to the Butler Bulldogs — and the place where the real-life game the movie "Hoosiers" was based on was played.

All 30 teams were drawn into one of six groups (five teams in each group, three East groups and three West groups). Each team plays the other teams in its group once — four games total — and those games count double as both regular season games and NBA Cup games.

The six group winners plus a wild card from each conference — determined by record and point differential — go into an eight-team knockout round bracket (East vs. East, West vs. West). The knockout round games are single-elimination.

The Final two teams, one East and one West, will head to Indianapolis for the Dec. 11 championship game.

NBA Cup games are played on specially designed courts with the home team wearing their Statement Edition uniforms.

Money. And make no mistake, that is the biggest motivation. Players talk openly about it. How much money? Here's the breakdown:

Player on championship team: $604,985 Player on runner-up: $241,994 Player on semifinals losing team: $120,997 Player on quarterfinal losing team: $60,499

While even the championship money is comparable to another game check for a star like Giannis Antetokounmpo or Shai Gilgeous-Alexander, it can be a meaningful bump for players on minimum salaries at the end of the bench. The stars realize who they are playing for when things get serious. The winning team gets a trophy, and teams will hang a banner. Even the Knicks. Eventually.

The New York Knicks, behind stellar play from NBA Cup MVP Jalen Brunson, knocked off Victor Wembanyama and the San Antonio Spurs — a Cup Final loss that would be echoed come June and the NBA Finals.

In 2024, Milwaukee bounced back from an ugly 2-8 start to the season, found its footing in NBA Cup games, and went on a run behind Giannis Antetokounmpo, reaching the Cup Finals against Oklahoma City. Once there, Antetokounmpo had a 26-point triple-double, completely controlling the game on both sides of the ball. LeBron James and the Los Angeles Lakers won the inaugural 2023 NBA In-Season Tournament (before it was officially branded as the NBA Cup).

The NBA is moving the NBA Cup Final to Indianapolis's iconic Hinkle Fieldhouse, the home of Butler University.

Famously, Hinkle Fieldhouse hosted the 1954 Indiana high school state championship game that inspired the 1986 movie "Hoosiers," and climactic scenes from the film were shot there.
