#2736. Packers activate Micah Parsons from PUP list to 53-man roster

That's the amount of time it took for Micah Parsons to return to the Green Bay Packers' active roster after suffering a torn ACL (and other damage to his knee) late in the 2025 season. As of Tuesday, Parsons is back on the 53, with the Packers officially processing his return to active duty this afternoon. Green Bay had an open spot on the roster ready for Parsons, having released running back Pierre Strong, Jr. a few days ago, so no corresponding move was necessary.

Despite missing three games in 2025, Parsons was named a first-team All-Pro in his initial season with the Green and Gold. His 12.5 sacks led the Packers last season and was the highest total for a Packers defender over a full season since Za'Darius Smith put up 13.5 in 2019. Eight of Parsons' sacks came in the fourth quarter or overtime as well, demonstrating his value to the team in crunch time.

Without Parsons, the Packers' defense took a significant step back at the end of 2025, giving up at least 360 yards in each of the four games he missed. However, the unit — and the pass rush in particular — has taken a giant leap forward in 2026 under new coordinator Jonathan Gannon. The Packers rank first in the NFL in turnover rate and they are third in the league in net yards per pass attempt allowed. Furthermore, their sack numbers have skyrocketed despite Parsons' absence; 2025 saw the team record just 36 sacks over 17 games, including Parsons' 12.5, but this year, the team is already up to 29 through just 10 games.

And now, the biggest veteran addition that the Packers have made in decades is back and ready to wreak havoc in backfields once again.

Parsons is officially listed as questionable for Wednesday's game against the Los Angeles Rams, but that should be a formality. His ramp-up period is now behind him, and by all accounts, he's ready to go give a boost to a squad that's looking to go on a strong stretch run.

#2735. Titans have only defense in the NFL giving up scores on most of its drives

The Titans' defense allowed the Cowboys' offense to score on every drive on Sunday, with the exception of a kneeldown at the end of the game. That put the Titans into some very bad territory.

Tennessee's defense has now given up a score on 50.5 percent of opposing possessions this season. The Titans are the only defense in the league that gives up points most of the time it takes the field.

Over the last two games, the Titans' defense went on an incredible run: In addition to giving up scores on the Cowboys' first eight offensive possessions in Week 11, the Titans gave up points on the Jaguars' last five possessions in Week 10. That streak of 13 consecutive drives giving up scores is the longest streak of consecutive drives in which any NFL defense has given up points, for as far back as full drive data is available.

The Titans' defense's last 14 drives ended in 10 touchdowns, three field goals and a kneeldown.

Titans coach Robert Saleh has had success as a defensive coordinator, but he went 20-36 in his previous stint as head coach of the Jets, and he's now 1-9 with the Titans. When he's the head coach, his defenses don't get the job done.

#2734. Kenny Dillingham confirms that Jarmaine Mitchell is no longer with ASU

Arizona State coach Kenny Dillingham confirmed that junior offensive lineman Jarmaine Mitchell is no longer with the team. A report came out on Nov. 23 that Mitchell had left the team and that he was expected to enter the transfer portal.

When asked if he had any comment on Mitchell, Dillingham kept it short.

"He won't be with us the rest of the way," said Dillingham.

No further details or reasons were given on why Mitchell left the team with one game left in the regular season, plus whichever bowl game ASU winds up in.

There were high expectations with Mitchell coming to Tempe, who was the highest-ranked junior college prospect to commit to ASU under Dillingham. Mitchell, at 6-foot-8 and weighing 320 pounds, was the No. 1 junior college prospect in the nation out of Copiah-Lincoln Community College in Wesson, Mississippi. Outlets like 247Sports, ESPN and On3 all had Mitchell at the top of their boards.

But Mitchell struggled mightily once arriving on campus. He never broke into the two-deep rotation — even with a starting lineup that resembled a merry-go-round — and saw very limited playing time with the Sun Devils. Mitchell appeared in just one game for ASU, with seven snaps played.

Mitchell was recently removed from a practice last week and did not play in the Oklahoma State game on Nov. 21.

One of the defining characteristics of freshman quarterback Jake Fette has been the ability to play clean football. Through his first three starts, Fette had only one turnover: a fumble — and that came on a whiffed block on his blindside during his first start against BYU. Most importantly, Fette had zero interceptions heading into the Oklahoma State game.

That changed on the final play of the game, when Fette rolled to his right and threw across his body right into the hands of safety Quintin Hammonds for an interception.

The play has been sitting with Fette since it happened, although he is trying to move past it.

"It sucks," Fette said. "You kind of feel like on a play like that in overtime, you let down the whole team, the fans, all of them. Towards the end of the game, I just got into the mindset that we need to press down three in overtime. And I can't do that. Even for how young I am, I can't throw a pick to lose the game for us."

Fette said the team became "lackadaisical" on offense in the second half, leading 24-7, and that the Sun Devils didn't have the same "fire" they did before.

"I kind of let everyone get a little too comfortable at halftime, so I just need to be a better leader and continue to make sure we have our foot on the gas so we can continue to play well," said Fette.

Dillingham appreciates how Fette has responded to the costly play.

"Jake's emotional and it's a good thing," Dillingham said. "He's passionate. His care level is high. That's a positive. I think any time your quarterback doesn't have a high care level, that's a concern.

"I've been blessed. I've coached a lot of guys with high care. Sam (Leavitt) the last few years; super high care level, one of the most competitive people I know, and I love that. I love that about him. He's phenomenal. And then having the opportunity to coach Jake right now and his passion, I love it when people care and their want-to is high."

Offensive coordinator Marcus Arroyo said it's almost impossible to understand what it feels like to have a mistake lead directly to a loss.

Arroyo believes this will end up being a positive learning experience for Fette.

"We're scoring like that in the first half and he's playing like that against the NFL quarterback against him because of him," Arroyo said. "So that's hard to swallow. I've been there. We take hard things as quarterbacks, way worse than we take the good things. But that's part of growing up and that's a part of the maturity of the position. I think he's going to have an amazing time rallying back from that."

#2732. Will the Buffalo Bills defeat the Kansas City Chiefs on Thanksgiving?

Welcome to SB Nation Reacts, a survey of fans across the NFL. Throughout the year we ask questions of the most plugged-in Bills fans and fans across the country.

The Buffalo Bills remain in control of their playoff fate thanks to an AFC-best 9-1 record. Buffalo has been able to meet almost every challenge thrown its way during the team's current six-game win streak.

An early concern, the Bills' defense has demanded respect around the league in recent weeks thanks to allowing zero touchdowns in three consecutive games while surrendering just nine field goals. Since their bye in Week 7, Buffalo has given up just one touchdown (to running back Derrick Henry of the Baltimore Ravens).

There has been plenty of discussion about the quality of quarterbacks the Bills have faced in the last month, but it's impossible to ignore the defensive turnaround post bye. In their first six games, Buffalo's defense allowed 171 points, giving up an average of 28.5 for last in the NFL in scoring defense. In the four games since the bye, that figure has plummeted to 37 points — an average of 9.3 points per game.

For all those saying the Bills have had an easy road, the same can be said of the Chiefs who haven't defeated a team over .500 since Week 1. One thing's clear: It gets much tougher for both teams on the road ahead, starting against one another in Week 12.

Quarterbacks Josh Allen and Patrick Mahomes bring out the best in each other, and they get to renew their friendship during primetime in front of a holiday audience. This time, however, both teams will have a potent rushing attack to lean on. That means we could see running backs James Cook III and either Kenneth Walker III or rookie Emmett Johnson take featured roles in the game's outcome.

Bills Mafia knows how great Allen and Cook are, and that when both are producing it's almost impossible to stop Buffalo's offense. That's especially true with running back Ty Johnson in the fold, and one of the team's most consistently productive receivers.

The same is true of Mahomes and Walker, but Walker has been sidelined with a groin injury. He may return on Thursday, but he won't have to handle the load himself. Buffalo's defense is facing a unique challenge against Kansas City, though similar in ways to the Detroit Lions.

Is another all-time classic shootout in the works for Thanksgiving? The weather may play a role, with sustained soaking rain expected amid temps in the mid-low 30s and winds gusting near 25 mph. Does that play better to either team's strengths?

The Bills and Chiefs both play outdoors and are used to inclement weather. Ball security will be of enhanced concern given the conditions, but it's something that you can be sure both teams have placed as the number-one focus regardless of weather.

Allen and Mahomes are both playing MVP-caliber football this season, and they're likely to have featured roles in deciding which team gets to play in Super Bowl LXI. But first, they have to get through Week 12.

Who wins in Bills vs. Chiefs to score a major coup in the AFC playoff standings on Thanksgiving night? Will the Bills win, and are you confident the team is headed in the right direction?

#2730. The Rams have no choice but to win Wednesday

If the Los Angeles Rams want to make the NFL postseason, they'd better find a way to beat the Green Bay Packers on Wednesday night.

Otherwise, we're likely barreling toward one of the biggest single-season flops in modern league history.

That's harsh, to be sure, but it's also the reality the Rams are currently facing. This was the heavy preseason favorite to win the Super Bowl, currently sitting on the outside looking in at the playoff picture as we enter Week 12. If they can't edge out a team in a similarly precarious position, and one with a devastating weakness, at that, reality will get even more grim.

This overall point is fleshed out by the math. There is massive playoff leverage on the line for whichever wins (and, subsequently, whichever one loses). According to RBSDM.com, Los Angeles currently has a 42% chance to make the playoffs. With a win on Thanksgiving Eve, the odds are bumped up to 56%. With a loss? They plummet to merely 20%.

Compared to The Athletic's playoff predictor, RBSDM's modeling is actually rosy for the Rams. That outlet gives L.A. just a 34% chance of reaching the postseason as things currently stand. It increases to 47% with a win and drops off to 16% with a loss.

The obvious storylines and narratives, and simply looking at the upcoming schedule, flesh out what the models are indicating.

Right now, the Packers are clinging to the No. 7 seed in the NFC playoffs. The Seattle Seahawks are one spot back of them, followed by the Rams and they're all 6-4 entering this week's action. The Packers and Seahawks don't play each other, so that potential tiebreaker is a little more complicated than simply head-to-head results, but Green Bay obviously is in control for the time being. Los Angeles gets at least one crack at both, however, with two meetings against Seattle. Blowing a chance to win the tiebreaker over the Packers would be devastating.

Per Tankathon, the Rams have the second-most challenging schedule left in the NFL, only behind that of the Chicago Bears. The Seahawks are fourth in that metric while the Packers are seventh. None of those home stretches are particularly easy, but it's an especially difficult road for the Rams. Throw in a lost tiebreaker and it gets scary quickly.

It isn't impossible for the Rams to make the playoffs with a loss on Wednesday night, but it becomes exceptionally difficult and it takes their fate out of their hands. Even if Los Angeles sweeps Seattle late in the season, a loss this week means it would need help to crack the top seven in the NFC.

This team has already had to battle through so much, and we're only 2/3 of the way through the season. It's only getting more difficult with each passing devastating injury.

From an objective viewpoint, that desperation is what makes this week's matchup such incredible theater, especially considering the Packers are essentially in the same boat. They're almost certainly toast for the postseason with a loss.

The Rams would love to make that their opponents' reality this week. For their own sake, they have to.

#2729. As Anthropic shares tank, investors scramble to hedge. It could be very expensive

As Anthropic shares tumble toward their IPO price of $83, investors are looking for ways to hedge. Unfortunately, they may have to wait until Monday, when options on the AI giant are expected to list.

If Anthropic breaks its IPO price, it will represent a 27% decline from Thursday's intraday high of $113. The wild price swings offer clues on how expensive it might be to buy protection on the new tech giant once options begin trading.A 20% intraday move points to an implied volatility somewhere between 100 and 150 for Anthropic options, according to estimates from single-stock market-makers on the Cboe trading floor in Chicago.

Options traders are also looking at how SpaceX traded in the days following its offering for clues on how options might price.

Implied volatility in SpaceX started at 116, but that didn't deter options traders big and small from piling into the contracts tracking the stock, with about 1.7 million contracts traded on the first day for a total options premium of about $2.5 billion. Far out-of-the-money call contracts were some of the most popular trades as the stock soared 40% in its first three sessions.One key difference between now and then? Anthropic is trading against the backdrop of S&P 500 volatility close to 30, compared to 17.3 when SpaceX debuted. "I expect ANT options to be super active," said Tom Sosnoff, co-founder of the trading platforms ThinkOrSwim and TastyTrade. "I expect heavy options volume."One thing Anthropic and SpaceX have in common is heavy retail involvement in both IPOs, with retail brokerages receiving a 20% allocation from SpaceX and reports pointing to between a 15-20% allocation from Anthropic on Thursday. "Round one of the Anthropic IPO easily goes to the individual investor," Sosnoff added. "Retail participants bailed early at the best prices of the day while institutional investors paid up to top off their underwhelming allocations. I believe retail was aggressively reloading in the mid-90s."Further signs of retail demand will become evident when options likely begin trading on Monday. One thing to watch: the spread between out-of-the-money call and put contracts, which was heavily skewed toward calls in the case of SpaceX. "I would be careful comparing Anthropic to SpaceX," said Dennis Davitt, CIO and Co-Founder of Millbank Dartmoor Portsmouth. "From a market-structure perspective, the two are dramatically different. The availability and cost of borrowing will be different, the companies are at very different stages of maturity, and SpaceX has allowed employees to monetize portions of their holdings over time. All of those factors matter when pricing options."

#2728. Penn State QB Decision Pits Transfer vs. Alex Manske

STATE COLLEGE, PA - SEPTEMBER 5: Alex Manske #7 of Penn State Nittany Lions in action in the second half of a game against the Marshall Thundering Herd on September 5, 2026, at Beaver Stadium in State College, Pennsylvania. (Photo by Brien Aho/Getty Images)

STATE COLLEGE, PA - SEPTEMBER 5: Alex Manske #7 of Penn State Nittany Lions warms up before game against the Marshall Thundering Herd on September 5, 2026, at Beaver Stadium in State College, Pennsylvania. (Photo by Brien Aho/Getty Images)

There was never any question who would start at quarterback for Penn State in 2026.

Matt Campbell came from Iowa State to be the head coach, and he brought Rocco Becht to be his QB.

It's the 2027 season where things start to get interesting at Penn State at the most important position in sports.

Becht will be gone, and it'll be up to the Nittany Lions to decide whether Alex Manske — who has been with Campbell all along — or an incoming transfer would make more sense as their starter.

GettySTATE COLLEGE, PA – SEPTEMBER 5: Alex Manske #7 of Penn State Nittany Lions in action in the second half of a game against the Marshall Thundering Herd on September 5, 2026, at Beaver Stadium in State College, Pennsylvania. (Photo by Brien Aho/Getty Images)

Manske would be the familiar face, and he's certainly a good guy for Penn State to retain for that reason.

It's not clear whether his ceiling is as high as a player who could be available in the portal — but that's dependent on what player Penn State can actually land in the portal.

The Nittany Lions certainly will look at transfers, because they'd at least need a conceptual backup for Manske with some college experience.

But whether or not Penn State aims for the stars remains to be seen.

GettySTATE COLLEGE, PA – SEPTEMBER 5: Alex Manske #7 of Penn State Nittany Lions warms up before game against the Marshall Thundering Herd on September 5, 2026, at Beaver Stadium in State College, Pennsylvania. (Photo by Brien Aho/Getty Images)

Brad Crawford of CBS Sports projected in a new article on Tuesday that Penn State's starting quarterback next season will be a transfer from elsewhere.

He does also acknowledge that Manske has a legitimate chance, writing this blurb:

Rocco Becht's backup since 2025 at Iowa State, Alex Manske — a former three-star prospect — missed spring ball after open heart surgery, but threw his first career touchdown pass two weeks ago against Minnesota and the Nittany Lions' coaching staff is high on him. That said, Penn State would like a sure-fire starter with previous experience before tossing Manske in front of live rounds in 2027 given his lack of in-game reps.

There's a belief in Happy Valley, too, that if a perfect fit is not found in the transfer market, Manske will get a chance to be QB1 next season for Matt Campbell. The first commit in Iowa State's 2025 class, Manske's been a part of Campbell's programs for two seasons now and originally had offers from Texas A&M, Wisconsin, Nebraska and others before trusting the Cyclones with his future.

Manske does seem like the kind of guy who could take a chance and run with it. But is that a risk Penn State is willing to take?

Only this offseason will begin to answer that question.

#2726. Bucks vs. Raptors Player Grades: Herro and Turner lead the way in slide-ending victory

After losing 13 consecutive games, the Milwaukee Bucks finally tasted victory, defeating the Toronto Raptors 116-111 in a nail-biter. The Bucks had been building toward this—having deserved to beat the 76ers in their last game—and finally put it all together in The Six. Most encouragingly, Milwaukee got key contributions from a struggling veteran in Myles Turner, while both rookies played very well and closed the game. Hopefully this carries into the rest of the season, and the Bucks can stack more wins. Read our full summary of the game here and catch a six-minute audio recap on the Bucks+ podcast, Bucks In Six Minutes, below.

37 minutes, 32 points, 6 rebounds, 8 assists, 1 steal, 1 block, 3 turnovers, 10/16 FG, 4/5 3P, 8/8 FT, +10

What a funny game for Herro, who scored just three points on 1/5 shooting in the first half (granted, he did have four assists), and then 29 in the second half on 9/11 from the field and 8/8 from the charity stripe. Herro mentioned this in his postgame interview, but aside from some nonchalant moments dribbling the ball up the court, I didn't think he played badly in the first half; the Raptors gave him a lot of attention, and so he gave the ball up early. Then in the second half, the vet got more opportunities and found a rhythm, especially at the free-throw line. Herro's composure throughout the second half, to shepherd the young Bucks to victory, was great to watch.

22 minutes, 6 points, 3 assists, 1 block, 3 turnovers, 3/8 FG, 0/2 3P, -16

I don't know what others thought, but it almost felt like Rollins looked a little small out there against the likes of Shead and Walter. He just struggled to make much of an impact on either side of the ball.

32 minutes, 18 points, 5 rebounds, 3 assists, 0 turnovers, 8/15 FG, 2/3 3P, +4

Aside from his customary contested three with 18 on the shot clock, Kuzma was pretty damn solid. He was Scottie Barnes' primary defender, and although Barnes made a bunch of shots over him, most felt well contested. Kuzma's two-to-three-point ratio was much better in this one too; 2-4 attempts per game seems like a much better balance than 6-8. I also love that he didn't turn it over.

24 minutes, 3 points, 4 rebounds, 2 assists, 1/7 FG, 1/5 3P, -4

Again, zero impact from Jaquez. When he starts alongside Kuzma, one of them will get open threes; you can't go 1/5.

28 minutes, 27 points, 11 rebounds, 2 blocks, 4 turnovers, 6 PF, 9/12 FG, 5/6 3P, 4/5 FT, -12

What a game from Turner—best of the season by a long way. When he looks for his shot the way he did last night, the Bucks look so much more lethal, and so does he; Turner had 11 points in the first quarter. Turner made two huge plays down the stretch, and the emotion he showed after both of them was great to see. Also, a solid second audition for the Raptors' front office, just quietly, after Turner bungled the first one. I know you liked what you saw, Bobby Webster.

23 minutes, 9 points, 3 rebounds, 3 blocks, 0 turnovers, 4/6 FG, 1/2 3P, +22

Despite his modest stat line, I thought Ware was great. One triple and three lobs; I love how he's been rolling with purpose and intent lately. How he caught that lob from Ament is beyond me; ridiculous athlete. That said, the most impressive part of Ware's game to me was his defence; for all the (fair) criticism you can level at him, his timing blocking shots is easily one of his best features. Figuring it out.

29 minutes, 14 points, 10 rebounds, 4 assists, 3 steals, 4 turnovers, 4/7 FG, 1/2 3P, 5/6 FT, +20

Impact. Impact. Impact. Burries made four shots and was still easily one of the best players. His poise down the stretch on both ends was amazing. That smooth crossover to get to the cup in crunch time wasn't even his best late play; his steal/wrench on Barnes took that spot. I don't know if I've ever seen a play like that; it reminded me of a former Buck who used to make those sorts of plays late in games… The kid's a maniac in the best way possible.

25 minutes, 7 points, 4 rebounds, 1 assist, 1 block, 1 turnover, 2/5 FG, 3/4 FT, +7

Ament's immense impact in this game will not show up on the stat sheet, and that may be the most pleasing part. I've wanted to feel his impact more and more, and this game felt like his best yet at impacting winning at a high level regardless of his stats. The main part of that was how well he guarded Scottie Barnes. Ament moved with him in space and forced tough shots; he was so good at it that Jenkins closed with him because they needed the length and foot speed he provided to win the game. Also, the Raptors tried to hide their worst defender, Jamison Battle, on Ament, which meant he had to play as a screener to get Battle into the action. I liked the rookie's ability to play out of those actions; he missed Ware on a lob once out of the short roll, but that will serve him well in the long run. Finally, just filing the play where Ament came hard off the Ware screen and threw the lob over Poeltl; I'm not sure he has had a play like that this season. If the rook can add that string to his bow…

I'll get the negative out of the way first: I still don't love starting Juquez and Kuzma, and the stats back that up—starters had a -32.8 net rating in 13 minutes. Other than that, Jenkins pressed many of the right buttons; the call to close with Ament was gutsy, and it paid off in spades. I also thought his overall game management was solid. Going at Poeltl relentlessly allowed 1) the Bucks to get in the bonus early in the fourth, which allowed them to keep the scoreboard ticking over as they went on that field-goal drought, and 2) to foul Poeltl out and thus open up the rim to score with no shot blocker present. Called some well-timed timeouts too when the team was frazzled trying to nurse the lead.

Limited Minutes: Caris LeVert, Kasparas Jakucionis, AJ Green, Gary Trent Jr.

Inactive: Pete Nance, Cormac Ryan, Kam Jones, Bogoljub Markovic

The Bucks could have played much better in many areas, yet they still won. I swear, this team can compete with more teams than people think.

Postgame, Jenkins praised the group's ability to stick to it, saying that eventually, the breakthrough would happen, and it happened.

Myles Turner reached 10,000 career points last night. His reaction: "Man, there are really no words for it. It's a testament to a lot of hard work and a lot of patience… There's no question that I've been struggling a little bit to start the season, but to have a breakthrough like tonight was really big for my confidence and just a reminder of who I am in this league."

The Bucks avoid "beating" the franchise-record 15 consecutive losses. I'm sure it would have felt good for the players to get that monkey off their backs.

The Bucks had 11 first-half turnovers (and 17 overall). I couldn't find the Raptors' points off turnovers in the first half, but they beat the Bucks 15-6 in fast break points for the game. Of course, that would have been 15-10 if Milwaukee converted on either of those easy opportunities in the fourth.

Tyler Herro's averages over the last two games: 41 PPG, 8.5 RPG, and 7.5 APG shooting 64% from the field, 65% from three, and 100% from the free throw line.

The Bucks beat the Raptors 52-44 in the paint battle.

Jenkins cut his rotation to about eight guys after previously using nine or 10.

I do want to recognise how valuable those end-of-game reps will be for Ament and Burries. You can train for those situations all you want, but nothing can replicate the real thing.

In the fourth, the Bucks had a stretch where they had all three of Kuzma, Jaquez, and Ament in the game.

The Bucks try to continue their winning ways on Wednesday night against the Brooklyn Nets. Catch the game at 7:00 p.m. CST on MY24.

"I have to be very honest, it was impossible to expect that we'd be ahead of them," Hoeness said.

"This stadium has an incredible history, including the chapter of the 1990 World Cup. We're happy to play here and be part of its history."

#2724. Despite everything, the Packers still have their goals within reach

I'd like to say 2026 has been a uniquely stupid year of Packers football, but I don't know if that's true. Certainly there have been uniquely stupid aspects of it; I can't think of another year where the Packers have taken such drastic steps to find someone, anyone capable of playing replacement-level football on their offensive line during the season. I can't think of another season where the Packers have had a comparable cavalcade of injuries, except for maybe 2025. Maybe that's just modern football. I don't know. I can't think of another season where so many games have been, if not just affected, seemingly outright steered toward an outcome by the officials.

But handwaving all of that, let's just say it's been a stupid year. If not uniquely so, I think we can land on that.

And yet! Here the Packers sit entering Week 12 with everything they want still within reach.

If the season ended today, the Packers would make the playoffs, occupying the seventh seed in the NFC field, a position that will surely one day be named for Matt LaFleur. The Rams and Seahawks are hot on their tails, but a win this week — certainly very achievable against an injury-diminished Rams squad — would solidify their lead over both teams. They'd widen their lead over the Seahawks by strengthening their conference record and put a serious dent in the Rams' chances by taking ownership of the head-to-head tiebreaker.

And if the Packers make the playoffs, who knows what could happen? Right now, the playoff field would see them matched up against the Detroit Lions in the wildcard round. That, too, is an eminently winnable game. The Packers had the Lions all but dead to rights in Week 7 but for their semi-permanent kicking woes. And even so, if Josh Jacobs takes the small but significant step of simply catching the ball in the end zone, there's a very good chance they beat the Lions.

I think outside of the Vikings, the Packers could at least be competitive against pretty much any team in the NFC. There are certainly some talented teams in the field, but none of them have the borderline psychopathy of Brian Flores. If that's what it takes to beat the Packers (other than a kicker meltdown), you have to at least think the Packers have a chance.

And I suppose that's the point I'm trying to make here. The Packers still have virtually everything to play for and all of it is within their control. The NFC North title might be out of reach, but the playoffs are still fully achievable. The Packers control their own destiny on every front; keep winning and they're in the playoffs. Stumble and their odds grow more remote by the week.

But it's all up to the Packers. They've shown this year that they can beat anyone — or lose to just about anyone, too. Their weaknesses are almost entirely self inflicted, but the solutions to those weaknesses are all self-driven, too.

There are a lot of different ways you could spin this Packers season, but I think the ultimate story is that this is a team that had everything within its grasp, despite whatever weirdness befell them. And that fact may end up being the biggest indictment anyone can level against the 2026 Green Bay Packers.

#2723. Panthers make season-ending decision on fan-favorite OL

Brady Christensen's 2026 campaign is over before it even got started.

Per a team spokesperson, the Carolina Panthers have decided not to activate Christensen to the 53-man roster on the 21st and final day of his practice window. As a result, the sixth-year offensive lineman will revert back to season-ending injured reserve.

Christensen began the regular season on injured reserve due to a hamstring issue. Carolina designated the former third-round pick to return to practice back on Nov. 3 and, leading into Sunday's win over the Baltimore Ravens, listed him as a full participant throughout the week.

The Panthers have already used six of their eight activations for the campaign. Players still on injured reserve who may be eligible to be activated include running back Jonathon Brooks, left tackle Ikem Ekwonu, linebacker Thomas Incoom and cornerback Jaycee Horn.

Without Christensen, the depth for the offensive line features Stone Forsythe, Corey Bullock, Sam Hecht and Torricelli Simpkins III.

#2722. Game Preview: Knicks vs. Cavaliers, November 25, 2026

Writing a preview for a Cleveland Cavaliers game can be an interesting task these days. Not because the upcoming Knicks-Cavs matchup on Wednesday is another NBA Cup game (sorry, Mr. Silver), but rather because it can get a bit dicey with your deadline. After meandering through stats and stories, recaps and rosters, you inevitably stumble upon some post on social media. Just a short video clip of a guy with an extensive beard not trying to play defense. As is common with train wrecks, you can't look away and click on it. Oh, the algorithm shows you another one?! Bang! One more, maybe?? You try to restrain your hand, but it still clicks on it. It could go on like that forever.

Watching James Harden play something that supposedly passes for NBA defense in 2026 from afar is almost like a guilty pleasure. Only for a moment, of course, until you realize that you're seeing one of the great scorers of this generation making a mockery of the game you dearly love. Then you breathe a sigh of relief that he isn't on your team, even if you've seen him score 51 points the other night against the Indiana Pacers. But it's all right; his own coach seems to be more than happy with the bearded fellow:

Analytically, he may be, but Lil' Coach K still seems a bit delusional here. Better said, he seems to have skipped the last few sessions of media training. You can't say stuff like that and then watch Harden in a game getting blown by like he is Andrea Bargnani after too many glasses of limoncello. Yes, he might, just might, be a top-ten scorer of all time (I certainly think he isn't), but you have to point that out specifically. By phrasing it the way he did, he opens himself up to major criticism, which is exactly what you don't need when your basketball team is in a funk. Or when some of your own players see what we're seeing.

If you want to know why things aren't really clicking for the Cleveland Cavaliers (9-9), you only have to connect the dots between these two clips. Your coach is praising one of your big-name players as if he is the second coming of Julius Erving, and then your big offseason acquisition is saying that the effort of said star player, among other things, is the reason why your team can't play a lick of defense. Now, he didn't say the name James Harden out loud, but you don't need the guys from "Mindhunter" to know where Watson is going here. He might just as well be talking about Donovan Mitchell, who is nowhere to be found as a leader these days. Either way, the Cavs are in a world of trouble.

You could argue they were before the last game against the Knicks, too, a contest they won and that figured to get them going a little bit. But in the six games since, it has been more of the inconsistent same for Cleveland. Missing Evan Mobley with a toe injury has been a major problem for their defense, as they now start three subpar defenders in Harden, Mitchell, and sharpshooter Sam Merrill. Their point-of-attack D has been nonexistent and looks even worse without Mobley behind it to at least erase a few mistakes by the matadors on the perimeter. Peyton Watson is working as hard as he can on that end (1.8 blocks per game as a wing), but ultimately, it's not enough.

It can't be that bad, can it? Well, without Mobley in their last six games, the Cavs have been 26th in defensive rating in the NBA. In their most recent outing, they allowed 45 second-quarter points to a Chicago Bulls team playing without Caleb Wilson and Norman Powell. If that wasn't embarrassing enough, they topped it off by giving up 63 points to the Windy City's bench mob, a group highlighted by the infamous star power of Jordan Hawkins, Noa Essengue, and Jaylin Sellers—no offense, in more ways than one.

There is a reason, of course, why the Cavs are still at .500, even if they'd make Tom Thibodeau rip out his last Homer Simpson hairs if he had to watch them play defense every night. That reason is their offense, their firepower. Cleveland has a top-five scoring group in the entire NBA; they've got two guys who can explode for half a hundred on any given night and plenty of role players who know how to put the ball in the basket, as the Knicks saw in their last meeting. If the Cavs win, they do it by outscoring teams, mostly in the half-court (Cleveland and New York are the two slowest teams in terms of pace right now), and by letting their individual talent create favorable situations. That can be enough on a lot of nights in the NBA regular season; it sure doesn't look like a recipe tasty enough for a team that went to the conference finals last year and would love to go a step further this time around.

New York isn't sure yet about the status of the wounded OG Anunoby, Deuce McBride, and Jordan Clarkson, which might be a problem in a game where you will need to score some points to keep up. We also know that big stars want to perform on the big MSG stage, so there is that. In the end, though, I'd say the Knicks will surely feel they owe Cleveland one after that last home defeat. Maybe they can even manage to get into one of those clips with the bearded guy. Who knows?

Who: Cleveland Cavaliers (9-9) at New York Knicks (11-6)Date: Wednesday, November 25, 2026Time: 7:30 PM ETPlace: Madison Square Garden, New York, NYTV: MSG, DAZN, NBA TVFollow: @ptknicksblog and bsky

#2721. NFL Thanksgiving week games: Ranking the 5 weekday games by what's on the line

The NFL went out of its way to schedule five compelling matchups for Thanksgiving Eve, Thanksgiving and Black Friday.

The schedule comes out in May, and things can change dramatically over six months. Yet four of the five matchups have held up very well. That's not a bad percentage, even if the Eagles and Cowboys have let us down. Everyone will watch those two marquee franchises anyway.

There's a lot on the line in terms of the playoff race during this week. We rank the most important games, in terms of what's at stake.

We could look back on this game as the one that determined where the AFC championship game is played. The Bills lead the conference at 9-1, but the 8-2 Chiefs can pull even with a win and also clinch the head-to-head tiebreaker. The 8-2 Broncos also will have a big say in the race for the No. 1 seed, as could the Jaguars and Bengals who are each 7-3. But a big part of the playoff picture will be revealed in the primetime Thanksgiving game.

The Bills are trying to win their first Super Bowl, and a No. 1 seed would help that quest. The Bills are 0-4 in the playoffs against the Chiefs in the Josh Allen/Patrick Mahomes era but three of those games came in Kansas City. A win Thursday night would probably ensure the Bills don't have to play at Arrowhead Stadium in January.

Josh Allen has consistently felt the pain from the Chiefs in big games.

The game will go a long way in determining the NFC North race. The Bears and Lions are tied for first place at 8-2, and the winner Thursday will have a one-game lead and an edge in the tiebreaker. They meet again in Week 17, but Thursday will shape the race over December.

The Vikings aren't out of the race either at 7-3, and the Packers are still lingering at 6-4. The NFC North is really good, and whoever wins it will have earned it.

The Lions keep overcoming injuries and have won six in a row. The Bears are on a roll too; they haven't trailed during a five-game winning streak. There was a long stretch in which the Lions' traditional Thanksgiving game didn't matter much because they weren't very good. Detroit has gotten a lot better and this game is one of the most important of the season in terms of division races.

The NFL practically invented a Thanksgiving Eve holiday as an excuse to put a game on Wednesday. At least it made it a good matchup.

This game is huge in a competitive NFC wild-card race. The Rams' chances of going to the playoffs would rise from 34% to 47% with a win, according to The Athletic, while the Packers' playoff odds would fall from 40% to 25%. If the Packers win they'll have a 61% chance of making it while the Rams' odds would fall all the way to 16%.

It's stunning to think that with a loss, the overwhelming preseason Super Bowl favorite would have worse than a one-in-five shot of even making the postseason field.

The teams are on opposite ends of recent injury news. The Rams found out that receiver Puka Nacua (knee) will miss the rest of the season. Meanwhile, the Packers will get edge rusher Micah Parsons back into the lineup for the first time since he tore his ACL last December. But it might not matter for Green Bay if it loses a key game in a tough NFC wild-card field.

The Broncos and Steelers are going in different directions. The Broncos improved to 8-2 and have a very easy remaining schedule after facing many top teams in the first half of the season. The Steelers were 6-2 a couple weeks ago, but then were blown out by the Bengals and took a bad loss to an Eagles team that had lost seven in a row. At 6-4, the Steelers are suddenly more vulnerable in the wild-card race, especially with a Week 13 matchup against the 4-6 Texans coming up. A loss to Denver would knock Pittsburgh closer to the glut of teams trying to stay in the mix for a playoff spot.

The Broncos' game against the Steelers is one of their toughest remaining, and a win would help them solidify their spot atop the AFC West. The Chiefs have a much tougher remaining schedule (second-toughest to only the Rams, according to DVOA), including a game at Buffalo on Thanksgiving. It's hard to imagine the Broncos running away with the division title, but there's a possibility they could find themselves with a one-game lead after Week 12 with a much easier path the rest of the way.

Of the 10 teams playing between Wednesday and Friday, nine have legitimate playoff hopes. The Eagles' chances of making it to the postseason are less than 1%, according to NFL.com. They're a very disappointing 3-7.

Still, it would be nice to ruin their division rival's playoff chances. The Cowboys have had plenty of defensive issues but still have a 22% chance to make the playoffs because the NFC East isn't very good this season. They can't, however, afford a loss to the Eagles.

There is some playoff intrigue to the middle game on Thanksgiving. Just not nearly as much as any of the other four games during this week.

#2720. Edmonton expecting big Ryan Nugent-Hopkins boost vs. Golden Knights after 5-game absence

Edmonton expecting big Ryan Nugent-Hopkins boost vs. Golden Knights after 5-game absence appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Edmonton Oilers are getting a major boost to their forward group ahead of their matchup with the Vegas Golden Knights. Ryan Nugent-Hopkins is set to return Tuesday after missing five straight games. His availability comes at a crucial time as Edmonton looks to snap a three-game losing streak.

The Oilers confirmed Nugent-Hopkins' return through their official X account. The veteran last played on November 14 against the Toronto Maple Leafs before an undisclosed issue sidelined him. He shared an encouraging update ahead of his comeback, saying, "I feel good now." Head coach Mike Babcock plans to start him on the left wing alongside Connor McDavid and Kasperi Kapanen. "Getting Nuge back, we're going to play him on the wall here for one game," Babcock explained.

Nugent-Hopkins has made a strong contribution whenever available this season. He has recorded four goals and seven assists for 11 points in 12 appearances. The 33-year-old also missed Edmonton's first nine games because of a training camp injury. His latest absence created another interruption to his campaign. The Oilers went 2-3 during the five games without him.

His return should give Babcock more flexibility with his forward combinations. Nugent-Hopkins can play both wing and center, allowing Edmonton to adjust its lineup as needed. However, the team will remain without Zach Hyman, who continues to deal with a migraine after missing consecutive games.

#2719. Karl-Anthony Towns for MVP? Knicks star is making case as the best player in the East

It's not the point of the video, I know. The clip the New York Knicks posted late Sunday night was intended to celebrate Josh Hart producing arguably his best performance in what's been a rocky start to the season — 10 points, 11 rebounds, 10 assists and a steal, his 18th triple-double in a Knicks uniform, tied for the second-most in franchise history — in a 113-106 win over the rival Indiana Pacers at Madison Square Garden.

What you see at the start of the video, though, is a sign posted inside the Knicks' locker room highlighting the principles that head coach Mike Brown has identified as the non-negotiable foundations of New York's offense: pace, space, quick decisions, paint touches, crash the glass. And at the very start of the clip, before the camera pans over to Hart, there's only one Knicks player in sight: Karl-Anthony Towns.

That's kind of perfect, because nobody's exemplifying those principles better right now than the Knicks' All-Star center … and, when you really take a look at it, very, very few people in the NBA can credibly claim to be playing any better than him.

The early-season MVP conversation pretty much starts and ends with Victor Wembanyama and Luka Dončić, the scoreboard-breaking titans leading the top two squads in the Western Conference. Expand the parameters out a little bit, though, and Towns — whose contract Knicks owner James L. Dolan declined to extend before the start of the season, and man, does it sure seem like the price is going up with every passing game — is forcing his way into the tier below them.

The Knicks are, and always will be, Jalen Brunson's team. Through the first month of the 2026-27 NBA season, though — and, really, since the start of last postseason — you can make a legitimate case that Towns has been their best all-around player. And, if you're feeling spicy, maybe the best player in the entire conference.

That's debatable, of course. Reasonable people can forward a number of names: Giannis Antetokounmpo, off to a scorching start to his tenure in South Beach; Cade Cunningham, still the straw that stirs the drink in Detroit; Tyrese Maxey, who's been the best player on the conference's No. 1 seed in Philly; Jayson Tatum, who had Boston off to a roaring start before some slippage out West.

Dig into the numbers, though, and Towns — the two-way heartbeat of a team that has won 11 of its past 14 games since an 0-3 start to move into a tie for second place in the East, boasting the NBA's third-best net rating in that span — has as good a case as any of them.

Entering Tuesday's action, Towns ranks third in the NBA in ESPN's net points per 100 possessions, behind only Wembanyama and Dončić. He's fourth in win shares, behind only Wembanyama, Dončić and Houston Rockets center Alperen Şengün. He's fifth in The BBall Index's LEBRON and box plus-minus, and sixth in win shares per 48 minutes, value over replacement player, Neil Paine's wins above replacement and Mike Beuoy's win probability added. He's seventh in player efficiency rating, ninth in DARKO daily plus-minus and tied for 10th among healthy players in Jeremias Engelmann's xRAPM. Which is to say: Take your pick of impact metrics, and KAT's right near the top, rubbing shoulders with the best of the best.

Maybe you're not such a big fan of the all-in-one alphabet-soup metrics; maybe you like your numbers a little less advanced. Well, how's this:

Towns enters Wednesday's 2026 Emirates NBA Cup meeting with the rival Cleveland Cavaliers averaging 21.8 points, 13.9 rebounds and 4.0 assists in 32.6 minutes per game. That makes him one of only three players in the NBA this season averaging at least 20 points, 10 rebounds and four dimes a night, alongside Antetokounmpo and Nikola Jokić. He's hitting those numbers more efficiently than ever, too, shooting 61% on 2-pointers and 42% from long distance, scoring under 1.32 points per shot attempt with a .650 true shooting percentage (which factors in 2-point, 3-point and free-throw accuracy) — both career highs.

Scoring plus rebounding plus playmaking plus efficiency is a pretty damn good combination. Prior to this season, Stathead's database lists only two players who've ever averaged more than 20 points per game, grabbed more than 20% of available rebounds, dished assists on more than 20% of his teammates' baskets, and posted a true shooting percentage north of .600: Giannis, back in 2019-20, and Jokić, who did it in 2021-22, '22-23 and '25-26.

Those seasons resulted in two MVP trophies and two runner-up finishes. That's the company that KAT's keeping this season.

(For the record: Wemby and Giannis are also keeping that company this season. Whew.)

Much of this stellar start goes back to those five principles — pace, space, quick decisions, paint touches, crash the glass — that Brown's been hammering ever since he got to New York. Towns might not be the first person you think of when you hear the word "pace," and the Knicks probably wouldn't be the first team to come to mind: They rank dead last in the NBA in number of possessions per 48 minutes, and tied for 28th in the average length of their offensive possessions. (Much, at times, to Brown's chagrin.) But while nobody will confuse Brown's charges for the Seven Seconds or Less Suns, they do move more swiftly with the 7-footer involved: The Knicks' average offensive possession is 1.3 seconds faster with Towns on the floor than off it — the largest differential of any Knick who's played actual minutes.

"Space," obviously, is a little easier to square here. Long heralded as perhaps the sweetest-shooting big man of all time (including, um, by himself), Towns is attempting eight 3-pointers per 100 possessions this season, the most since his last season in Minnesota and the fourth-most of his career. He boasts the third-highest average gravity of any center — an NBA Advanced Stats metric aiming to quantify "how much a player pulls defenders towards them [...] how much attention they draw compared to what the spacing on the floor predicts" — behind only Wembanyama and Joel Embiid. (Towns also outpaces both in perimeter gravity when he's off the ball, meaning he's drawing more attention than either of them when he's spaced and doesn't have possession — creating wider driving and passing lanes for his teammates to exploit.)

When Towns does have the ball, he's wasting precious little time before attacking. Among 80 players averaging at least 50 touches per game this season, Towns averages the second-fewest dribbles per touch and has the seventh-lowest average touch time, holding on to the ball for just 1.8 seconds on average, according to Second Spectrum tracking data. He's one of 12 Knicks under two seconds per touch — evidence of widespread adherence to Brown's insistence on quick decision-making.

When the big fella decides to move, he's often aimed at the lane. Despite spending a significant share of his floor time setting high ball screens, spacing the floor or facilitating from up top, Towns leads the Knicks in paint touches per game — he's 26th in the league in total paint touches — and is taking a career-high 53% of his shots at the rim this season.

He's actually driving to the cup less frequently — just 5.5 dribble-drives per game this season, down from 8.1 last season and 11.3 in 2024-25. He's been an absolute monster on those forays to the basket, though, scoring nearly a point per drive and shooting 67% on those plays. He's finishing tough through the contact he draws in the lane, too: Nearly 35% of the shooting fouls he absorbs have ended in an and-one — also near a career-high.

Thanks partly to the pressure Towns puts on the rim himself, and partly to how his presence on the perimeter opens up the floor for his teammates, the Knicks take 34.5% of their field-goal attempts at the rim with him in the game — a near-top-five rate in the league, according to Cleaning the Glass. That rate drops to 25.8% when he's off the floor, which would sit just 26th in the NBA.

That just leaves "crash the glass" … which Towns is doing to the tune of hauling in a career-high 11.3% of available offensive rebounds. He's turning the garbage into gold, too, tied for sixth in the NBA in second-chance points per game — one big reason why New York ranks in the top five in putback scoring efficiency.

When Brown took over, he made it clear exactly what he wanted his players to do — what he believed they had to do to reach the grandest goal in the game. After achieving it, he demanded nothing less. More than any other player in orange and blue, Towns has delivered it, night after night after night.

"Ever since I got here, all I've ever said is that I want to do whatever it takes to win a title," Towns told reporters earlier this month. "You win one and, you know, this year I want to do it again. I'm always willing to do whatever it takes. I've always sacrificed for the betterment of the team."

That's the funny thing about basketball, though. Sometimes, being willing to give it up means you wind up getting it back in an even better position to do something with it.

The sacrifice-minded Towns' usage rate, average time of possession and field goal attempts per 100 possessions are all up a tick from last season. And with New York more frequently operating its offense through Towns as a playmaking hub at the top of the floor — a carryover from the success the Knicks found with those looks last postseason, which has helped unlock Brunson as an off-ball weapon and unleashed a devastating split-cut game — he's averaging 43.4 frontcourt touches per game this season, according to Second Spectrum tracking. That's the fifth-highest mark in the NBA this season, and KAT's heaviest half-court workload in five years.

Towns has been brilliant in that more central role, whether looking for his own opportunities or quarterbacking the offense to set the table for Brunson, OG Anunoby, Mikal Bridges, Hart and the rest of the defending champs. He has notched the assist on 21.9% of his teammates' baskets during his time on the court — a significant leap from his first two seasons as a Knick, and a near-top-10 mark among power forward/center types thus far this season.

New York's live-ball turnover rate drops by eight percentage points in KAT's minutes, and its shooting efficiency improves by eight percentage points, according to PBP Stats. When he's on the floor, nearly 60% of the Knicks' 2-point field goals have come via assist — up from 50% when he's on the bench. That's the difference between top-three in the league and bottom-five.

"They've done a great job of making their offense multifaceted," Timberwolves head coach Chris Finch told reporters when the Knicks visited — and beat — Minnesota last week. "They still have Brunson doing his thing. And now KAT, playing through him — we talked about how that was something that they found last year to go to late in the season, in the playoffs. And now, the Brunson-KAT two-man game is better than it's ever been."

So, too, is Towns' work on the defensive end — so often maligned over the course of his 12-year career, but something that started to become a legitimate strength early last season, sustained at a high level through the Knicks' championship run, and has carried over to the start of this season.

As aggressively as Towns crashes the offensive glass, he has hit the boards even harder on the other end, leading the NBA in defensive rebounds and total rebounds per game. All that possession-finishing has led to him finishing all 16 games he's played this season with a double-double — the longest such streak to open a season by any Knicks player ever, and the fourth-longest season-starting streak by any player since the ABA-NBA merger, according to Justin Kubatko of Statitudes. KAT's company there, again: Jokić, Giannis … and prime Bill Walton.

Towns' defensive contributions extend beyond rebounding, though. He's become much more comfortable playing multiple defensive schemes, including more frequently playing up higher on the floor to take away ball-handlers' airspace in the pick-and-roll. He's been active and aggressive with his hands, posting the highest steal rate of his career and his highest block rate since 2021-22 — and, crucially, doing so without hacking, averaging his fewest personal fouls per 36 minutes since 2019-20.

"KAT's got tremendous feel," Brown recently told reporters. "He's a smart, smart dude when it comes to a lot of things, but especially when it comes to the game of basketball."

And when he's sunk back into the paint in drop coverage, Towns has proved a sturdier interior deterrent than his reputation would suggest. The share of opponents' field goal attempts that come at the basket plummets with him in the game, and opponents are shooting 55.3% at the rim when he's the nearest defender, according to Second Spectrum tracking — that's 25th among 86 players who've defended at least 40 up-close attempts, and on pace to be the second-best mark of his career. All told, the Knicks are allowing just 106.3 points-per-100 in Towns' minutes — a level of defensive efficiency that would be No. 2 in the NBA, behind only his old pals in Minnesota.

So: What do you get when you combine a skeleton-key stretch big man who unlocks a championship-level offense with a defensive anchor capable of serving as the backstop of a top-five defense, all while playing major minutes at an elite level of offensive efficiency? It sure sounds like one of the best, most important — dare we say, most valuable? — players you can have in the modern NBA.

Maybe, in a world where Wemby and Luka and SGA exist, that all doesn't add up to an MVP trophy. It does, though, produce a player that's a hell of a lot closer to top-five in the world than he is to missing the All-NBA ballot — which Towns did last season (unjustly, in my view). And, if KAT and the Knicks can keep up the flow they've found since their slow start, it ought to produce one hell of a title defense.
